"Phil Squod " calls it a "dastardly murder." Poor Brett was certainly Murdered, in the eyes of the law, and it was a most deplorable result to the efforts of opening that do9r. But English minds more- charitably dis Posed and calmly critical, are inclined to describe it in a less offensive manner than "Phil Squod." At a very critical period in the legislative history of Ireland, Lord Frederick Cavendish was suddenly appointed Chief Secretary for Ire- land, and left London for Dublin. without the fact being known to the majority of his col- leagues in the Government, and certainly with- out the knowledge of anyone in • Ireland. Amongst a large section of extreme men in Ibublin, Mr. Burke, the Permanent Under Secretary, living in Dublin, was, rightly, or wrongly, charged with being the advisor and ' initiator of the repressive and coercive laws Ire- land was then experiencing, and a conspiracy was formed to take his life. But the men who determined to commit the crime did not know the man, whom they found out was in the habit of taking an early walk in Plicenix Park. The attempt on Burke's life by an unfortunate eoin- ciderice was made the morning of Lord Frederick Cavendish's arrival, he joining Burke for the morning walk. Burke was pointed -out to the conspirators, who attacked him. They did not know Cavendish from Adam, but Cavendish, like the brave English gentleman he was, went to the assistance of Burke, and, in doing so, lost his life also, a loss grievously deplored by all Irishmen of every shade of opinion or creed. Nationalists were astounded when they found he had gone fo Ireland, and all realised that a terrible disaster had occurred. Irish justice demanded five lives in expiation, and got it, one of the condemned being a lad of 19 years. And this is what "Phil Squod " describes as Ire- land's response with a dagger. Let me here say, the bereaved widow, Lady Frederick Cavendish, in the hour of her great grief, in the nobleness of her mind, and the charity of her heart, ab- solved the Irish nation from all thought or par- ticipation in that crime; and, to his . eternal credit, his brother, the late Duke of Devonshire, opposed as he was to the scheme of Home Rule, never made any political capital out of it. The Devonshires were good landlords in the south, and amidst all the troubles had little or no -diffi- culties with their tenants, the good relations continuing after that calamity. But it remained for the irresponsibles of that time and since to try and brutally fasten upon the Irish people the odium of the 'crime. 'There is more in—` Phil Sqiiod's " letter with which I would like to deal, but I fear I am ex- hausting your space, as well as my limited time. There seems very little left of the missionary of peace abbut him. I would like him to realise that Nationalists have a lot to remember and forgive of invective prejudice and contempt in the past, but they are anxious to let the dead nest bury its .dead. With our recognition of the kindlier, fair and generous spirit now pre- vailing, we are anxious to say: Let the Qrangp Lily be thy badge, my patriot brotlTer, The everlasting Green for Me, and we for one

another; -n.rnd all of us for. the Empire of Great Britain, of which we are proud. PATRICK O'BRIEN.

THE TWELVE MONTHS' TEST.

A series of experiments have been made for some time with a view to seeing whether by lengthening the day of the ordinary domestic fowl, the result would be a more prolific supply of eggs. It is claimed that this is so. At the Ideal Home Exhibition coops of six hens are shown side by side. In one case the hens are allowed to go to roost as daylight fades at their own sweet will and pleasure, whilst in adjoin- ing coops the electric light is kept full on until the close of . the exhibition. It is -claimed that by so lengthening the day the supply of egg: from the coops whose inmates have had their days so lengthened by artificial light ,increases materially. The experiments are being car- ried out by 111:Ir. W. Cook, of Orpington, the well-known breeder of fowls.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
CATARRH IN FOWLS.

Catarrh or common Cold, doubtless due to the climatic changes seems to be prevalent in many poultry yards. Poultry suffering from cold. seem .mopish, and have a discharge from the nostrils, at first watery, then gradually increas- ing and getting thicker. The symptoms are similar to the fii'st stage of roup, and it may be said that unless carefully treated colds eventu- ally end in that dreaded disease. . All affected birds should be isolated ,and given three drops of aconite in half a pint of water, which will act on the inueous membrane ,and reduce the inflammation. Chlorate of potash is a safe remedy, and. easily prepared. In a pint of rain water-put 2oz. of the chlorate and 2oz. of crushed lump sugar, and blow them to dissaive, giving two to three teaspoon- fuls daily to grown fowls, whilst for young birds it is advisable to increase the quantity of water.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
GOOD HENS NECESSARY.

Poultry-keepers are reminded that to ensure profitable egg production three essential factors make for ultimate success—good hens from a known laying strain, congenial surroundings, and suitable food, all of which are •easily ob- tainable, even by young beginners. The hens must possess an inborn tendency to lay, also an excess of vigour to enable them to withstand the heavy drain on the system. Fowls never succeed better than when well housed in clean, roomy warm houses, well ventilated„ and en- suring plenty of fresh air and sunshine, with a dust-bath kept filled with dry ashes. Such hens, fed on simple wholesome ,food, pro- duce an abundance of eggs in depth of winter, whilst others, kept in expensive houses and overfed on elaborate ratiohs, are generally un- productive.

THE DUAL MARRIAGE.

A Strange Story from Warrington.

A charge of bigamy which counsel described as of a rather extraordinary character was pre- ferred against a Warrington woman, named Mary Ann M`Donald (34), at Liverpool Assizes on Wednesday. Mr. Lindon Riley, who prosecuted, said the prisoner was lawfully married in January, 1901, and it was alleged that the bigamous marriage took place two years later, between these two events. Prisoner's lawful husband, who was in the Army, was drafted to India. He remained there several years, and within a short time of his going prisoner married. the second man, with whom she lived until about 18 months ago. The lawful husband' in the meantime returned from India, and prisoner joined him again until the date she gave herself up at the Police Station for bigamy. Prisoner had been hiring with him. The Judge: Another curious feature of the case is that the brother and sister of the pri- soner were witnesses to both ,marriages. Ido not know that they realise they are liable to be charged with aiding and abetting. Mr. Lindon Riley.: The marriage seems to have been with the entire family approval. A Warrington police inspector stated that he understood the prisoner received a letter from her first husband in India saying she could get married again, as he would never come back. ,Mr. Justice A.vory, commenting npon the con- .duct' of the prisoner's brother and sister, said if people did this sort of thing they would find themselves in the dock, charged with aiding and abetting. ' As the prisoner had returned to her lawful husband, and he *as willing.to maintain , her, his Lordship bound her over to be of good behaviour.

FARM AND GARDEN.

REN !VAL OF 11AX GROWING.

_ The Development Commissioners have been instrumental in establishing some trials by. which the resuscitation of flax-growing is to be attempted under a careful supervision of all the conditions which make for success in both the growing and the after-treatment of the crop. In many villages in Yorkshire and North Lincoln- shire up to a few years Ng° the flax-growing and retting industries, for the production of fibre, were important features of the agricul- tural production of the district; but the gradual disappearance of a market for the straw and for the fibre caused the discontinuance of the growing of the crop. The fibre grown in some districts was admittedly excellent in quality, and much sought after by spinners and weavers, but there was little or no organisation among the producers.

DA IR Y SHORTHORNS. The pedigree dairy Shorthorn has made enormous strides of late years, and many far- mers who in the ordinary way would never have thought of going in for registered cattle have been struck' with the records made at the Dairy Show and in such herds as that of the late Mr. George Taylor. It is noteworthy that another herd of these cattle is about, to be dis- persed, Mr. Ewen Macintosh disposing 'of all his Stock on October 23rd, at the Mill Farm, near Broxbourne Station, owing to the farm being given up. The herd was established only six years 'ago, when some heavy milking cows of, good strains were purchused, and the stock now offered are for the most part descended from Rosebud 10th, bred by Mr. Joseph Stratton, with a milk record of 930 gallons, and from Floss, bred by Mr. T. B. Hulbert, whose yield has on each of the last two years reached /the thou- sand gallon. The records given in the* cata- logue just issued by Messrs. Thornton show that the yields ot the cows bred on the farm are in keeping with' their distinguished ancestry. The sii•es include' Sir Millicent, used in the Cranford herd, Wild Baron, bred by Mr. Tay- lor, Fanny's Boy, Sir Millicent's Prince and Wild Prince. 'Rosebud 13th gave 9,0501 b. of milk from January 15th to December 15th in 1911, ' and 8,9171 b. from January' 18th to September 20th in 1912.

IN THE GARDEN.

Renovate lawns with top dressings of well- decayed manure. Take further cuttings of Violas and Pansies. Divide and re-plant Phloxes. Move Geraniums and Fuchsias under cover. Hoe between the rows of Cabbages and dust a little soot along the lines to ward off slugs. Runner Beans will'keep fresh for a fortnight if the •stalks are placed in water, changing every four days. - Protect Cauliflowers by breaking the largest leaf and covering the head. Sow Grass seed on bare patches on lawns. When Gladioli cornis , are taken up after flowering, ,the small offsets' may be separated from the parent bulbs. At planting time next year put the ,small bulbs in a bed by them- selves.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
CLIMBING CLEMATIS..

The Clematis, from its free-climbing habit, is welt adapted to cover a porch, verandah, sum- mer house, pergola, or an old tree. Utilised for any purpose the Clematis gives delightful effects. Plants do much better grown 'away-from the house, where the soil is usually dry. This is the cause of ,many failures of close-to-the-house- cultivation. The soil may not IA better in more open positions, but the plants get theiri.share of whatever rain falls. The Clematis is a gross feeding plant.

FLOWER GARDEN COMBINATIONS. ThoSe who seek to produce great flower con- trasts in the garden should make note (says "The Garden") or two plants which by reason of habit and the merit of profuse as well as abundant flowering would 'appear well suited for purposes of display. The plants we have in mind are the brilliant scarlet Salvia Glory Of Zupich and the pure white Anemone4fowered Marguerite, better known perhaps as Chrysan- themum Mrs. Sander. For effective work it may not be necessary to mix the two or even to "border the one with the other, a better way being, perhaps, to plant a bed of each in near proximity, and so mirror into greater purity or brilliance the finer attributes of these unique subjects.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
BLACK AND RED CURRANTS.

Prune Black Currant bushes by thinning off old and, unfruitful branches, after which dus the trees over with lime. Black Currants shouk not be allowed .to become overcrowded wilt wood.

The fruit of the Black Currant is borne on the young wood. A, lot of the old wood is, there- fore, useless for fruiting, and also prevents sun and air reaching all parts of the tree. Thin out now all but the -bright and fresh-looking wood. Each branch should be free of each other.

Red Currants require more severe pruning than the black, as they fruit quite differently. Cut back to three or four 'eyes all this year's side shoots, and shorten back tile leaders to the required length.

DECREPIT HORSES.

Sir,—The information conveyed in Mr. Ernest Bell's letter on the traffic in worn-out horses is most distressing. -One had hoped the condi- tions under which these unfortunate animals are shipped had been greatly improved of late. Legislation to prohibit their export alive, and ensure a merciful death by the most up-to-date methods this side the water seems to be what is required. Let us hope some humane and energetic Member Of Parliament may at once take the matter up.

E. L. DALTBENY. Pittville Lawn, Cheltenham, Gloucester, Oct. 28th, 1913.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
NATIONAL SERVICE LEAGUE.

Sir,—Miss Thompson, as an exponent of the views of the Society of Friends on, national defence, certainly (if she is reported 'correctly) departed last Sunday far away from the original ideas of the oldest mem- bers of the Society of Friends, men like George Fox and Isaac Pennington. Both of these men recognised the necessity of "the sword of, jus- tice" with which to defend themselves. In fact, Isaac Pennington says clearly in his works that he is not opposed to "any magistrate or peoples defending themselves against foreign invasion," and expressly states that "a great blessing will attend the sword where it is born uprightly to that end." Will Miss Thompson say clearly Whether she agrees with the founders of her faith or not? If she agrees with -them, how can she object to a jUst system of home defence? T. R. ASHWORTH. 5, Goulden-street, Warrington.

Sir,—The National Service League asks that every,- man should be trained for defence alone, but emphasises the fact that those 'men who have conscientious objections to becoming citi- zen soldiers shall be exempt and shall be trained to save life in the Medical and Sanitary Corps. Accordingly, all the Sunday night labours of our friends the Quakers, and their appeals to "conscientiousness." are outside the question, if they are criticising our proposals. • ; R. BRIMELOW. • 22, Pickmere-street, Warrington.

Sir,—lt is no use saying, as certain respect- able people- in Warrington are doing, that one does not believe in war. We cannot escape from war by pretending that it does not exist; rather, we must make preparations against war as, we do against fires and, disease. _ . _

Advocate's of National Service do not advo- cate war, but we .must remember that it was Christ Himself who said; "When a strong Man armed keepeth his palace,his gods are in peace" (St. Luke xi. 20, 21). There is nothing in the teaching of Christ to show that he disapproved of the Minan, soldiers, whose business it was to keep the peace in Palestine. 34, Pickmere-street, Warrington.

A. LONG

===== 0002601_19131101_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
SPIRMSM AND ROMAN CATHOLICISM.

Sir,—ln reply to Mr. Chrimes, I have very lig to add to what I said in my previous late!. I made a distinction between '" spon- taneous " and " induced " phenomena, and ex- plained what I meant at some length. This distinction is really at the root of the mari7er, and it is a distinction that is perfectly legiti- mate and intelligible. The Catholic Church does not condemn as necessarily diabolical every spirit phenomena, apparition, &c., but has, to put it • mildly, 'strong reasons for suspecting, both on a priori and on a posteriori ground, that those phenomena induced and invited at a Spiritualistic seance by means of that state of mind known as' mind-passivity," are of such origin. Such conduct we hold is immora7;and I quoted a strong passage from Deuteronomy xviii., showing how God has strictly forbidden slick -practices.- I notice Mr. .Chrimes does not face this quotation, though apparently he be- lieves in the Scriptures, as shown by the twO or three passages he tears from their context in support of his opinions. .However, Mr. Chrimes rejects my distinction between " spontaneous " and " induced" phenomena as worthless, and so, of coukse, does not see how a Catholic would regard in a different light such a case as the apparition to the Passionists at Belfast, and a manifestation occurring at a Spiritualistic seance. It does not seem, then, we shall get any nearer an agreement, but I did not expect we should.

One more criticism I should like to make. Mr. Chrimes, -in both his letters, speaks as though the conclusions of scientific men in psychical research were all in favour of Spiritualism. This is claiming far too much. While it is true some prominent scientists have asserted their belief that these researches have proved that spirit agencies are at work, and are, ;indeed, the spirits of the dead, the 'Society for Psychical 'Research and the most experienced of scientific experimenters are much more cautious in their pronouncements. They (always making allowance for some fraud) agree that science has proved that independent intelli- gences must be at work, but they do not ven- ture to express an opinion as to the nature of these intelligences„ Professor Flammarion, for example (whom ,Mr. Chrimes himself hails as a great leader in these things), in his "Psychic Forces," has said, "As to beings different from ourselves—what may their nature be? Of this. we cannot form any idea. Souls of the dead? This is far from being demonstrated. The in- numerable observations which I have collected during more than 40 years, all prove to- me the contrary. No satisfactory identification has .been made."

(Rev.) P. L. BUCIetINS. St. Alban's, Bewsey-street.

IRELAND AND THE CONSTITUTION.

Sir,—The lamentations of "Phil Squod " over the present position of the Constitution can be safely left to his reading of Mr. Asquith's speech last Saturday (October .25th).- If "Phil Squod " was satisfied with the position of the Rouse of Lords, as a Registration Committee, to 'ratify and confirm all Conservative measures when they were in power, and emasculate or reject all measures when Liberals were in office, be was content with a condition of affairs that bad grown , intolerable and repugnant to Liberals, many of Whom wish the limitation of the power of the Lords had gone Much further. The Parliament Act was designed as a means to an end, viz., to pass Home Rule, Welsh Dis- establishment_and the Abolition of Plural Vot- ing. Whether you agree or otherwise with any, or, either, of these Bills, is quite another ques- tion. The fact remains that there was no otner evident way of securing the • wishes of the majority of the electorate of Great Britain and Ireland. _ . _

"Phil Squod " scree bis himself behind " Time" when, Lecky's History, and the Royal Commis- sion on Orangeism are quoted against him, and •says, "it is old,". and "A lot of water has flowed under Wwington Bridge since then." But he does not hesitate to melo--dramatickly throw back the curtain, and usher into the light of the present , the tragedies of Manchester. and Phcenix Park. Well, let us examine them in the order in which "Phil Squod ".places them. In' Manchester two men were, arrested for complicity in Fenianism. No -other crime was attributed to them. Some Manchester men con- ceived the 'idea of attacking the police van on its way from the police court to the gaol, breaking it open and releasing' the two prisoners.'. They surrounded and stopped the van, but found they. could not open the door, and called upon Sergt. Brett.," who was inside," to hand but the keys or open the door. The Sergeant, animated by a high sense of duty, declined, when the boy Allen (he was barely ~.20 years of age) put a pistol to the lock to blow it open, and suc- ceeded, but the bullet deflected and struck poor Brett, mortally wounding him. WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT MISSIONARY EXHIBITION.

•reference to missionary work, one. hears the same old objections brought out again and again. Within the last few weeks the same statement has been _made-to-me by three differ- , ent people, wbo have respectively resided in

Burmah and .South :Africa. "Oh,". they say, " nq one ever engages a converted native for a servant, for if he is at all inclined that way his conversion only makes him a bigger liar and thief than he was before. His own re- ligion is, thevfore, much better adapted to his ,needs." And when they have said that they think the last word, has been said on the sub- ject. But this objection, reasonable as it appears, for the moment, will not hold in the light of past experience. St. Paul had the same, diffi- culty, and wrote to his converts that ther was such sin amongst them as was not even named by their heathen neighbours. But did that 'deter him? Not .at all. Had it done so, the result would have been that our own country would never have been converted, and all that Christianity has • done fc-7 England in the past would never have been a .mplished, nor could all the glorious possibilities of what it may do in the future be entertained. Another objection one often bears is the use- lessness of :trying to convert the heathen abroad when there are so many at home' to try our hand on. But the answer to this, which, by the way, should appeal to the business man, is that the Church can get a far beiter return for her labour and money abroad than she can by spending all her energies at home. Ex- perience has shown that wherever the Church's message is preached there will always be some .who are attracted to it. The best-paying policy, then, from the Church's point of view, and the one which from the first she has adopted, is to endeavour to plant herself everywhere, for wherever she obtains a footing she ensures a faithful few to keep alight the lamp of truth, and a leaven which may in time, as in 6ur own country, leaven the whole lump. BUSINESS MAN.

THE SUNDAY SCHOOL OF TO-DAY.

Sir,—' have ;lot the least doubt that a report of the 'illustrated lecture given by Mr. Arthur Mounfield in connection with the local Sunday School Union in Bewsey Lecture Hall will ap- pear in your pages, and I would crave a little space, by your indulgence, for a few impres- sions of my own.

By means of some 50 slides, prepared from photos procured from the four corners of the earth, and of that fund of knowledge conse- quent upon .wide and thorough reading, Mr. Mouiifield was able to •take his audience along many an ancient roadway, and through fields of primitive faith, there to dig and to unearth the overlaid archteological treasures. of the home of :early man in Assyria,. Babylonia, and Egypt. Books, letters,' commercial ...documents, - and obelisks were in turn produced, showing the thought and custom of thei day, and, as inde- pendent witnesses, emphasising the reliability of the Bible story. In the light thus hurriedly and transiently thrown out, one was able better to understand many things erstwhile dim, e.g., as one gazed on the cruel features and gigantic stature of the Pharaoh of the oppression, the photo of whose mummy was cast on the sheet, one appreciated the reluctance of Moses to face him, and in many •parts the Bible became a newer and more living book.

This is ,the day of criticism, and no one more than the Sunday school teacher needs to be well equipped for his work, and to understand the most effective method of manipulating the great instrument of his profession: As a responsible member .of the Union, I would like to suggest to every teacher who is able to afford it, or as he may be able to afford it, and to schools which have a teachers' library,, the procuring of the "Century Bible." This cinnmentary, apart from annotations, possesses. a systematically divided introduction to each book, which Con- stitutes the 'latest word in paodern scholarship and research, and. gives much, in text and pic- ture, of what Mr. Mounfield has - presented. When I mention the names of such contributors as Driver,' Skinner, Adeney, Peakc, Bennett, Kennedy, Charles, . Currie Martin, &c., it will- be seen that the best thought of Anglican, Pres- byterian, and many other " isms " is placed at the disposal of he studint. .I would also sug- gest that teachers try and place in their own and in the hands of suitable scholars, a most excellent little penny book by the late W. T. Stead, called the "Bairns' Bible."'

W. REID

St. John's, Warrington, October 22, 1913. !the lecture mentioned Will be found on Page 9.

Evans, value ' 205., and with. ste4,ling a purse containing 48. 2d. in cash. The matter was reported to the police. The boy tried to dispose of it to a, cycle dealer in town, but he was handed over to the police. Power, 5, Buttermarket- street, the previous Saturday night.—The Chief Constable stated that Thomas Whittle, an errand boy, saw the prisoner loitering \about the shop, and observed him take the boot from the front of the premises, put it under his coat, and go into the Cross Keys Hotel. A policeman was informed, and when arrested accused was at the back of the premises. He ,had not the boot, but prior to his arrest a thud was heard, and later the boot wits found over the wall. When charged at the Police Station, prisoner, in a half measure, admitted the theft. He now pleaded guilty.—Prisoner said that he had a wife and four children.—The Mayor: Why did you take one boot?—PrisOner.: I was drunk and did not know what I was doing.—The Mayor :. I wonder whether you had seen the one boot and thought it was a pair. (Laughter.)—Accused had been three times previously charged with arceny, and again the Bench said they were dealing leniently with him in ordering him to pay 205., including costs, or ten days.

_ _ ASSISTED TO MANCHESTER.—WiIIiaM Murphy, a stranger to the town, when arrested for begging, said, "It is just what I am waiting for; I wanted to get to Manchester on Monday." They were not related, and the magistrates fined them ss. each, but the elder had to payohis costs. , -

A QUESTION OF HAIR.—Mrs. Cooper and Fanny Cooper, 91, Hoyle-street, were charged with assaulting Dora Berry.—Plaintiff gave evidence, in which she stated that the defendants, with- out any provocation, dragged her from her own house into their own, and committed the Assault. Witness produced a quantity of hair, which she alleged had been torn out of her head. —Mrs. Cooper also produced hair, which she said had been torn from her head by Mrs. Berry.—Each of the Coopers was fined 10s., in- cluding costs.

AN UNDUTIFUL SoN.—The story of a son's cruel treatment of his mother was told when Hannah Sankey, 1, Hopwood-street', Warrington, gave evidence against Nathan Sankey, her son,. charged on a warrant with stealing a, quantity of •turpentine, hair-pins, and studs, to the value of 3s.—The Chief Constable stated that on Mon- day, September.29tll, the prisoner packed up the goods tO take out for sale. His mother told him that it was his last chance, and if he did not return lie would be given over to the police. Prisoner returned home at night in a drunken condition. He did not hand any money over, Jut asked for a half-penny, which the prosecu- trix refused to give him. He obtained one from a lodger, went out, and when he returned he was in a still more drunken condition. Prisoner later left the town and a warrant was issued.for his arrest, which was effected the previous night by Acting-Sergeant Clough, who, on reading over the warran4, received the answer: "I have nothing to say."—The mother, who is a widow, and' Acting-Sergeant Clough gave evidence.—The prisoner- persisted in the remarks that the pro- perty' was as much his as his mother's, where- upon the Chief Constable informed the Bench that the whole of the property in the shop be- longed to prosecutrix. She had a heart as big as that Court for prisoner, who had been a per- sistent thief in the house and had ill-used her. —He was sentenced to two months' imprison- ment, with hard labour.

THE LAND CAMPAIGN.

Evidence that will Lead to the Sweep- ing Away of Injustices.

RURAL PROPOSALS. -

The first volume of the Land Inquiry Com- kittee's report and Mr. Lloyd George's speeches 14 Bedford and Swindon contain the broad out- lines of the Government's proposals in regard to What is called the "rural side" of the land question.

• The Land Inquiry Committee have provided "I their report an exhaustive and illuminating alcetch of agricultural conditions. Many facts, colling for immediate reform of the land system, Which were known before, are now irrefutably lbade clear by a variety and volume of illustra- tion and example. We knew for instance that ligricultural Workers were badly paid and badly 11°1Iscu-', but never before perhaps has so much concrete evidence of the fact been collected to- ether in one volume. The very bringing to- gether and publication of su,ch a mass of evidence 4.lgainst the existing land system provides in itself a potent factor in creating the necessary Public opinion for sweeping away the injus- tice of prevailing conditions. - - - - -

Most'of the pr-oPosals Put forward in this re- Dort have been officially adopted by 11r. Lloyd George and a united Cabinet. The Bedford 81)eech was devoted almost entirely to a state- , Oient of the evils caused by the existing, sntem of lana; tenure, but at Swindon the chancellor laid down certain definite remedies. His pro- Dosals for the regeneration of the countryside comprised :

A legislative minimum wage for agricultural labourers. A State housing scheme, under which cot- tages would be erected by the State to be let at rents sufficiently high to repay cost, but which would be lower than if the cot- tages were erected by private enterprise, because the State would be able to borrow

money at a less than current rate of interest. _

Security Of tenure for farmers to be secured through powers conferred on Commis- sioners of controlling notices to quit and awarding compensation for improvements and damages for disturbance in cases of eviction. Fair Rent Courts. - •

Extension of Small Holdings by State Vor chase of land.

LAND VALUES CRITICISM.

To Encourage the industrious and Enterprising. Mr. Sidney J. Phillips, of the Lands Value Press Bureau, writing to criticise the proPosals t° establish Wage Boards, to.fix a legal minimum wage, and to erect cottages under Government control, with the aid of money borrowed on State credit, says : Fair rents, higher wages and adequate housing accommodation depend primatily and absolutely on free access to land on fair terms. How, then, to secure, this boom? The way will be made more clear if we can discover why it is that such access is now restricted, for is it not a strange fact that in this land of milk and honey, cap- able, as Mr. Lloyd George has rightly pointed out, of producing easily twice the amount of agricultural produce that is now obtained, with a large class of farmer f and labourers, yearning for, and sweating their hearts out because of the lack of, the opportunity of consummating this production, .there is at the same time square mile after square mile of the' most fertile land in the world lying derelict, under-used, or de- voted to the selfish- whims and tastes of an idle plutocracy? The reason is to be found in the Land Inquiry Committee's report in the chapter that deals with rural rating. - _

- "We can fairly say," is ;Wed in the report, "that the method of assessment valuation by Overseers and Assessment Committees often leads to grave injustice and that the burden of rating is heavy where it ought to be light, and light where it ought to be heavy. The building of cottages, the establishment of Small Holdings, the better equipment of farms, and all the higher or more intensive cultivation is penalised. Encouragement is given to the decaying village, the underfarmed holding, the badly managed estate, and the game preserving landowner. Thus the effect of the whole system upon rural devel- opment is one oftteady depression." Here surely is the whole trouble. Reverse

this process, and encourage the .building of cot- tages, the establishment \of small holdings, the better equipment of farms, higher and more intensive cultivation And discourage badly used land and penalise the holding idle of land. IR other words levy rates on land values and exempt improvements altogether from rating. Then those who are industrious and enterprising will be encouraged and stimulated. The erection of cottages will be free from the burden of the rat- ing tariff. The underfarmed holding and badly managed estate will not pay and swill become anachronisms. The imposition of the rate in respect of idle and badly used land (according to its market value) Will force millions of acres now idle into use and badly used land into better use, for the dimple reason that being faced with -a concurring obligation to contribute to public revenues in proportion to the value that attaches to their land (through no expenditure nor energy on their own part, be it - noted) land- owners will be irresistibly persuaded to get the most from their land either by working it them- selves or releasing—on the best terms they can arrange isi an extended market—to others willing to do so. The entire front of the economic posi- tion would be changed. In place. of the existing condition of landowners wilfully or ignorantly withholding land from use and thus throwing land users into competition for access to land and raising rents, restricting production and opportunities for labour, we shoUld have land- owners competing with each other for tenants, and at once, and without the intervention of Jair Rent Courts, rents would fall to a fair level. as a natural process; access to land would be obtainable on fair terms, with 'security of tenure, and so the scope of agricultural develop- ment would be indefinitely enlarged, and through easily acquired small holdings there would be available to the labourer the alternative of working independently, thus increasing the op- portunities for labour and raising wages without State intervention of any kind. And with wages high and cottage land easily available, builders and builders' workers would eagerly come in and quickly supply all needed cottages.

LABOUR TOPICS.

The Iron Work Labourers' Advance; Other Works to be Tackled.

SCHOOL CARETAKERS' GRIEV- ANCES.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
BY **A WORKER."

An object lesson in the value of trade union- ism has been provided locally by the advance secured for the labourers in two of .the largest iron works in the town, as was exclusively an- nounced in the last issue of "The Examiner."

This result proved, conclusively the value of solidarity amongst the workers, for, if the iron labourers had not presented a solid phalanx in their demand they would have received scant consideration, and certainly they Would not have got the conditions, 'which appear .to have given satisfaction, Conceded at almost the last moment. Only those in the inner circle know how. near Warrington was' to the greatest in- dustrial upheaval 'known in its history. Last week a strike seemed certain, and it was only averted through the tact displayed by the men's representatives, the patience—most commend- able Patience—of the workers, and the late hour concessions by the employers of the men's reasonable deinands.. Three Years' Work.

Thr.je—Years— ago the Nr.'arriii-ghin W—orke.i\s wOl-0 found in an almost hopeless state of dis- organisation, and the remark chiefly applies to the unskilled and seini7skilled‘men. Then the. National Union •of .Gas Workers and General Labourers came: along, and spade work was commenced in grim earnest. This was fos- tered by Mr. J. T. Clynes, the Lancashire secre- tary of the Union, and Mr. Charles Dukes, the local organiser: The result that has accrued proves One of the most unique experiences in the history of trade unionism. The weak have grown remarkably strong. Concessions and improvements have •been steadily secured. Three years ago the wages in the iron works were 17s. 10d, for a .week of 58i hours; to-day the wages have advanced to 225. for a 53 hours' week. Thus while the hours have been reduced by 51, the wages have increased 4s. 2d. per week. Would this have been achieved without trade unionism? I, and I think every worker will agree with me, emphatically say it would not. "Buttons up" has had an excellent result; and still more is to come.

Other Developments. With the iron workers' conditions settled for a little while, other places are about to be tackled. In some wages rule low and hours prove long, and these places are to be taken in hand. The negotiations will be started as soon as possible, and the men's claims will be quietly but firmly laid before the employers. Of course, we may anticipate the usual delay. Masters are never too ready to make concessions, but there is such a spirit of unrest abroad amongst their employes that they will have to do something. and they must not delay too long in doing it. What the particular works involved are, I do not think it judicious to mehtion,. at •.present, but their wages and hours sadly need over- hauling to bring the conditions up to those ex- isting in similar places elsewhere. I 'hope to he able to keep my readers informed in regard to the matter in future issues,-but one thing is cer- tain, the men are- determined to have better- ment. ' School Caretakers.

Much unrest, I learn,. exists amongst school caretakers, and this is a subject which we may soon hear raised in the Town Council. A peti- tion has been forwarded on the question, and I cannot see how this can avoid coining before the Council in some shape or other. One griev- ance is the. inequality in regard .to pay. Hours are long and 'Wages low.-`4 There are labour- ers," said one' caretaker, "who have better hours, better pay, and less responsibility than we have, and we want at least a fair day's wage for a fair .day's work." Another sore point is the question of payment when schools are turned into polling stations. Some care- takers get pald for the extra work • thus en- tailed, and others do not.. Their 'claim to be put on an equal footing, and to be reasonably paid for work which scarcely comes under the category of the duties of ,a school caretaker, seems. only, just and reasonable.

Brewery Workers. There is an increasing agitation amongst local brewery workers for better pay and other conditions. In other centres, of this trade ad- vances have been conceded, and in the matter of wages Warrington remains unsatisfactory. In Stockport, a demand has been put forward for a minimum of 255. per week, and the brew- very employes are likely to get an advance, if. they do not get all they ask for at once. Naturally the Warrington men learn of what their mates are getting elsewhere, and they arc far from satisfied.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
TIGERLAN D.

. In " Tigerland," Mr. C. E. Gouldsburg de- scribes the wild beasts of the jungle from ex- perience gained in many hunting expeditions. For the rhinoceros he entertains Much respect, He says : In weight and bulk. almost equal ,to an elephant, its short, stumpy legs give it a stability not possessed by the latter, and the shortness of its legs in no way interferes with its speed, which, when charging, is tremendous. Its charge is practically irresistible—cmes, in- deed, being known of elephimis,- caught broad- sidP on, being knocked completely over. - The injuries, too, which it can .inflict with its sharp-pointed, powerful horn; sometimes a foot in length, and it strong teeth, sharp. at the edge as a razor, makes the animal a most dangerous foe. hence it is little wonder that—with very rare exceptions—the staunchest elephant cannot be induced to face a "rhino," much less stand and wait its charge. Under these circumstances to shoot a charging " rhino " is no easy matter, and the sportsman who has been fortunate •enough to bag one under these conditions has I much to be thankful for., .

Mr. Gouldsburg gives the following vivid de- scription of the depredations .of a man-eating leopard. This animal fir§t, manifested its man- eating propensities when it, carried off and ate girl. Later on in the same month it kikled a boy of eight and devoured an infant of one and, a half years. The next human victidi was 'a child killed a month later.

--- - From January to,the'end of December in that year it had killed and wholly or partially de- voured exactly one hundred persons, mostly Women and children, and in the following year, up to the following April, When it was shot, it had accounted for 54 more ; this making a total of 154 persons killed within a space of' 21 months !

The terror created by such a wholesale slaugh- ter of human beings in one particular groin) of villages may be more easily imagined than described. The people .were fairly panic- stricken. Some fled and sought refuge in dis- tant villages, others, abandoning all thoughts of slcep, barricaded. their doors and Windows and kept on the watch all night, while some of the younger and able-bodied men, goaded to desperation by the loss of wife, child, or other near relative, lay in wait for this demipn• in. feline-form, and when it next made its onslaught attacked it in a body, armed with sticks and stones, but only to lose some of their own num- ber. For the bloodthirsty brute, ,encouraged by former -vecesses and whollY devoid Of - fear, charged boldly into the crowd,sorrietimes killing one or. two, and always mauling others before making its escape, which it invariably did; prac- tically uninjured.

WELSH TITHES.

How a Warrington Church Gets Part of Its Annua! Support.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. PETER'S SALE OF WORK.

The annual two days' sale of work in connec- tion with the St. Peter's parish was commenced on Monday afternoon in the John-street Schools, Warrington, when, in the absence of Mrs. Charlton Parr, Miss Parr performed the open- ing ceremony. Those present included the Rev. B. Percival (the vicar), who presided; Mrs. Percival, the Rev. J. H. Garnett, the Rev. H. V. and Mrs. Pigot, the Rev. J. H. G. Honing- worth, Mrs. Harrison, Mrs. Bracecamp and 'Mrs. Willis. .

The VICAR said the sale was one of their hardy annuals, and one of those things which they could not possibly do without. In a large poor parish it was very difficult. for them to raise anything like sufficient to keep the various organisations going, and the sale enabled their own'poer people to give their small contribu- tions, and those outside the parish could, give their larger contributions, for, after all, they relied upon outside help. In tliose days they heard that church endowments were national property, and that they had State-aid churches. He had been at that. church for 21 years and had received nothing from the State, neither had anybody else received anything, and he had never heard of anybody receiving it throughout .the length and breadth of the land. They had raised, their money lust other people people had raised it. If, unfortunately, the Disestablishment and Disendowment in Wales should.pass, it might affect them, becuse they raised tithes to about .t 1.30 a year from _Wales. They -should • know how they came by those tithes. The reason was that the tithes were in the market a few. years ago, and Mr. William Sharp, nephew of Mrs. Beamont, saw them advertised, and very kinaly purchased and gave them to St. Peter's Church. They should clearly understand that it was just as right for them to have Welsh tithes as, it was for those present to have Welsh pictures in. their houses, so long as they paid for them, and that was what Mr. Sharp thought in regard to that parish. (Applause.) Miss PARR formally 'declared the sale open, .saying that a growing parish needed a growing expenditure. (Applause.) The Rev. J. H. GARNETT proposed and the Rev. H. V. PIGOT seconded, a vote of thanks to Miss Parr.

The Stallholders. The following were the stallholders : Vicarage Stall.-3lesdames Percival, Greaves, Padmore, East, Kay, and Dickens. . Men's Bible Class Stall—Rey. J. 11.. Garnett and members of the Bible Class Committee.

Teachers' Stall.—Mrs. Smith -the Misses Booth, Hughes and Percival. Wardens' Stall.—Mesdames Steel and Cowdell. Refreshment Stall.—Mesdames Barnforth, Grounds, Atkinson, Reddish, and ISiss Laing. Flower Stall.—Mesdames Young and McQuil- lan.

Crockery Stall—Mrs. Tickle and Miss Lily Percival.

Sweet Stall.—The Atisses DoHie Percival and Fletcher. H Padmore.

UPHILL WORK IN THE PARISH.

On Tuesday afternoon the sale was re-opened by Mrs. Rylands.

The VICAR presided and said that the previous day's takings amounted to ...f:77 16s. 9d., which was regarded as satisfactory. He had forgotten, to mention that in connection with that school there was a partition which was quite new, and a cloak-room, and the cost was .012. They had the architects to pay, and there was also, the cost of the heating apparatus, and if there was anything to spare out of the proceeds of the sale of work it might be given towards the great cost to which he had referred.

Mrs. RYLANDS said she had often thought what uphill work theirs must be in a parish in which a large proportion of the parishioners were so very poor, to raise the necessary funds to main- tain the various organisations which existed. The Vicar, in these circumstances, must natur- ally expect help from outside; she (lid not see how he 'could do without it. But he had an energetic and efficient band of workers. She had had a long experience of bazaars, and she knew the hard work they entailed, and she could, therefore, thoroughly appreciate the labour and self-denial of the stallholders and others. She wished them every success. (Ap- plause.) Mr. T. S. STEEL proposed a vote of thanks to Mrs. Rylands. Mr. REDDISH seconded and the motion was car- ried unanimously.

Rellifon in the Sc

Preaching on SUnday evening at St. Peter's 'Church, the Bishop of Liverpool dealt mainly with the subject of parental control, and said children were allowed ..too much of their own way. He spoke 'emphatically on the fact that they did not give religious instruction in their secondary schools, and so young people would go out into the world without any knowledge of Tclioion.. He suggested that they, as parents, thould not give their support to men in the Council who would not try to remedy this state of things. • The parents should be considered.

iber. A keener interest in the many activities Church life was required, personal service pul- sating with a real, warm enthusiasm, under- taken by each individual, would have an in- fluence imposSible to measure by its strength and power. A more loyal obedience to the com- mand "Go ye into all the world," in missionary effort, in social improvements, and in all the problems of every-day life, was urgently needed, so that a mighty influence for purity and righteousness be spread abroad.—A good discus- sion followed.

Wycliffe P.S.A.

Mr. Wright, of Bewsey-road, gave an address upon ." Character." God, he said, chose a 'de- liverer .for Israel- from the oppression of. the Midianites, and Gideon was the chosen one; but he felt his inability to lead, but God used him for a deliverer and saved Israel. Daniel, Moses and David were men of .weakness, yet men of character. Men who are leaders of men to-day are men of character. The unrest around us is guided by men of character. Smiles said in his work, "Character is the glory of the State, the human life at its best." A visit to Westminster Abbey reminded us of men of strong character, men who gave their lives for the good of others. Perseverance was necessary. to 'the making of character. •

Mr. Beaver, of Sheffield, who was present, brought greetings from the Wycliffe Brother- hood. Sheffield, and spoke of their work there. The Secretary proposed votes of sympathy with Messrs. Pritchard, who. had lost their sister by death; -Mr. A-. Buckley, who had lost his Mother; and also to the family of Mr. R. Wyllie. who suddenly passed away on Saturday. Mr. McEachern and Mr. Healey took part in the meeting, and four' new members received a hearty welcome to the P.S.A.

Harvest Festival at Elmwood Avenue . Congregational Church.

The new church at Elmwood-avenue has met with considerable success in all its movements since the recent opening. It is quite evident, from the large attendances each Sunday, that it has supplied a long-felt want. Mr. H. M. Llewellyn occupied the pulpit on Sunday morning, on the occasion of the harvest thanksgiving. In the evening the building was filled to its utmost capacity. Councillor It. Hen- shall took the service, and preached appropri—. ately of "The joy of harvest." The choir ren- dered suitable music at each service.

On Monday evening the festival was continued with a kocial. The Rev. T. T. James presided over a large gathering. Songs were rendered by Mrs. J. Hawthorn, Miss E. Hewitt, Mr. J. E. Hannan, and Mr. W. H. Wallington, in such a delightful manner that the audience demanded several encores. Miss Corney delighted the audience with her able reciting, whilst Mrs. 11. Haviour and Miss F. Senior were all that could be desired in their violin and pianoforte solos. Mr. W. H. Payton was the accompanist. Refresh- ments were provided during the interval, and collections were taken at each service for the church funds.

The Driver Cadle Case.

Outspoken Sermon by the Rev. H. Ogden.

Driver Caudle's sentence, the much discus- outcome of the terrible Aisgill railway disaster, was criticised by the Rev.-H. Ogden at Golborne- street Baptist Church -on Sunday night. One would be justified, said Mr. Ogden, in ask-, ' ing why , Driver 'Caudle.was charged, only with the death of Sir Norman Douglas'; why not charged also with the deaths of the others? If he was guilty of one death, he was guilty of all. "The probability is," said Mr. Ogden, "that if there had been a duke killed through the collision we would never have heard of Sir Norman Douglas." Driver Caudle was charged with manslaughter, the jury found him guilty, and the judge sentenced -him to two months. Two months was but playing with a sentence—if Driver Caudle was culpably responsible. But, said the preacher with emphasis, the crux of the sentence lies here—Was he guilty of a crime? As a criminal to-day he is punished, as a crim- inal to-day ho is suffering, and his sentence the sentence of a criminal. 'Even the severest critics could only say that Driver Caudle erred, and if every man, because he erred in his daily 'business, was to be punished by a two months' sentence, 80 per cent. of the Cabinee would be in gaol. The judge evidently regarded the sentence of two months a deterrent. Could two months be a deterrent? Driver Caudle was not a man who risked his life; he had every inducement to careful, conditional to his meeting the com- pany's requirements of speed. Then, further, his own life was the first to be imperilled. The deterrent on the railway driver was not-the two months, but the risks to his own life. One stould hardly be amazed, unless Driver Caudle was released, if railway men did not down their tools.

In fairness and justice to that man, he ought not to stand stamped as a criminal; Ile ought not to be suffering imprisonment as a criminal, and it behoved every right thinking man, every man with a sense of justice and fairness, every man who had a love of right, to urge, Petition, speak, and even threaten, to secure a result that will mean the just release of Driver Caudle.

The Cheshire Police Committee on Saturday decided that there should he such an amend- ment of the Police Pensions Act as would give a reasonable discretion to police authorities to grant gratuities to the dependents of deceased officers other than widows and children.

Mr. W. Ward, the secretary of the Nelson and District Weavers' Association, who recently rO- - the position of secretary of the Nelson and District Textile Trade Federation after 21 years' service, was on Saturday night at Nelson presented with a purse of gold. Mrs. Ward re- ceived a gold watch and chain.

Mr. J. E. Place, a member of the firm of Messrs. Joseph Place and Sons, colliery pro- prietors and ,sanitary pipe makers, Hoddlesden. Darren, died on Saturday at his residence. Highlawn, Darwen. He was a Liberal and, Nonconformist.

AI) ULT SCHOOLS.

A Visit from Representatives of the Birkenhead Schools.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
INTERESTING GATHERINGS.

The- Warrington Adult Schools had a visit of about 20 members of the Birkenhead Schools last week-end: On arrival on Saturday they were met at the station and taken to the Museum and Art Gallery. The party were re- iceived by Mr. Madeley, who conducted them round the collections. After tea with the United Schools, one of the most successful and enjoyable concerts in the history of the local movement was hold in Academy-street School. On Sunday afternoon a united "Fireside" was held in the Stockton Heath School, when Mr. Alfred Davies, president of Oxton-road School, Birkenhead, took charge of a conference on "How to keep men in the Adult School." For over an hour the reader, by judicious leading queStions, kept the audience fully interested in various practical 'methods of making the school helpful and educative. Many of the visitors took part in the animated discussion which' followed.

BEWSEY MEN'S. 'On Sunday morning Mr. William Hughes, of Birkenflead, gave a lecturette on "Modern Political Movements." The speaker traced the development of thought in the political sphere during the last 50 years, dealing with the rise of trade unions, Socialism, and Syndicalism. References were made to the formative influ- ences of Robert Owen, Carl Marx, Engel, and Bebel. Mr. Harold Baldock, of Birkenhead, opened the Bible lesson on "Man and his work." He contended that work was a necessity lttid upon 'man, if he was to live a full life. But man, under present conditions, was condemned to too much uncongenial and badly paid toil. It was impossible, under these conditions, to develop to any appreciable extent the idea of social service which should be involved in daily work. Much reconstruCtion was urgently needed to bring 'industrial conditions more in accordance -with this idea. Social service, in some form, slionld characterise every man's life and work. In the evening Mr. Alfred Davies spoke on "Life and its satisfactions." Ile said man could never be really satisfied by living exclu- sively on a material plane of life, lie needed 'higher phases. The, speaker showed the close relation of art, literature and science, &c., to the life of man. The higher life was ever seek- ing to express itself through these media, and their influences would bring light; beauty and .joy to the general life of the community.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
LONGFORD MEN'S:

This new school was opened on Sunday morn- ing at 60, Longford-street, by a visit of Mr. Alfred Davies, of Bil-kenhead, and the President of. the County Union of Adult Schools, Mr. J. A. Itansome. Several members of other Warring- ton Schools assisted. The attendance was very encouraging, and shows the need for a school in this thickly-populated district. • A well-discussed Bible lesson on "Man and his work" proved most useful. It was fully realised that much of men's and women's work nowadays maybe monotonous and dull, but if we are engaged in usend, productive work, it an only be for the benefit of the individuals; and the State, if the work is carried out to the best ability of those so engaged. The point Was raised by one of the visitors as to whether it would not be .of more utility to the nation if some of the labour and money which is now yearly spent on armaments were spent in some more productive way, such as, say, building cottage homes for our rural popu- lation, 'or Convalescent homes for the sickly ones, or in some Other, way by which actual benefit to the people would result.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
SANKEY BRIDGES MEN'S.

On Sunday morning Mr. William Hewitt, of Latchford, gave a most interesting lecturette on "A successful life." To illustrate his theme the lecturer took the life of Thomas Edwards, the Scotch naturalist.

In a brief sketch of Edwards' career, Mr. Hewitt said that he was born of poor parents in 1814; Leaving school at the age of six, he became a bootmaker. Very early in life he showed a keen interest in Natural History, and in spite of his limited knowledge and the ex- cessive hours of labour, he studied and collected zealously. Misfortunes followed one after an- other, but Edwards plodded on, from-time to time adding to the stock of knowledge of better known •men than himself.

In the end his worth was acknowledged, and he died honoured by the scientific world, but without having amassed any wealth, the usual criterion of a successful life. The lecturer considered Edwards' life a suc- cessful one for the following reasons He was never idle, always having his time well occupied; (2) he was persevering, even When everything seemed against him; (3) he culti- vated patience, and moved steadily from point

to point, in his search for knowledge. Remade the most of his opportunities, and did not rail at Fortune because she did not smile on him.

A Leicester correspondent tells the following story of how, at the age of 76, he was cured of a dread complaint of over 30 years' standing. his own letter, which is published herewith, tells how he overcame this complaint, and the recital certainly "puts a feather in the cap" of the remedy which rooted out a disease so firmly established. By George Edgar.

Miss May Fannon was purstiing her career. The career was art, and at the moment it con- sisted of earnest attempts to render the sun- light blazing on the downs. There was no particular reason why May should pursue anything in the form of a career, beyond the fact that she was a typical modern young woman, who believed that members of her sex are as capable as men, and that she, a parti- cularly bright member of it, had a destiny larger than being a mere social idler, with marriage as the apex of her dreams. The career, therefore, was painting, and the results justified the choice, for the picture on the easel, unfinished as it was, betrayed consider- able knowledge of technique and what some garrulous artists call "feeling. She had chosen to cycle five miles to the scene she was industriously trying to repro- duce, and, for the purpose of making the day longer by 'being independent of such country places of refreshment as were available on her route, had brought her luncheon daintily packed in a neat basket. May was having a. delishtful morning, uninterrupted in a con- genial task, and miles away from anyone, when two things happened. A gipsy woman appeared, apparently from nowhere. She was dark- skinned, bold- eyed, and had that brazen mysticism which some gipsy women still possess—the gipsies who are nearly vagrants, and exist by hawk- ing trifles and telling fortunes, while their men folk idle about small racecourses, or commit petty larceny. "Cross my hand with a, piece of silver, pretty lady, and I will tell you about the hus- band waiting for you—the handsome, fair man with the blue eyes. I can see him." the woman with the bold black eyes said, mystically—" I can see him in the skies." It was on the tip of May's tongue to say " Nonsense !" But after all she was dis- tinctly' feminine, and at least it promised to be an adventure. She opened a well-filled purse and passed a shilling across the gipsy woman's paim. The dark woman's fingers closed on the coin, and her eager eyes lit up as they lingered, for a second, on the contents of the purse. Following the usual nonsense of flattery and prediction, the gipsy droned the fortune out, mechanically. "I can see the life of a pretty girl who is genereus, high-minded, and May smiled, clever. She is not appreciated." unconvinced. "She has had her troubles, but they are over. There are two men --one is dark. She must beware of him—he wants her money. Another is fair, with blue eyes; a brave, fearless gentleman. The lady will marry the fair gentleman with the blue eyes; but not before she has trouble with the dark man." And so on. At first it amused May. But the gipsy wanted her hand crossed with silver again. May tendered another shilling, .but this did not suffice. The gipsy departed from her Method of fawning flattery, and became rude. May told her very pointedly that the inci- dent had ceased to be a jest.

"If you do not go at once," ehe said, stamping her foot, "I'll mount my bicycle and bring the police." "Not so fast, my pretty lady," said the gipy woman, with a sneer. "You are a. good way from the police, and if you will not cross my hand with more silver, I'll do it for myself." _

The gipsy woman's brown hand clutched at May's white hand which held the purse. May struck at her feebly with a light and in- adequate paint-brush. The gipay laughed. " 'Erb ! " she cried. 'Erb appeared—he had apparently been ?oncealed in some bushes near by. He was a monstrously big, dark-skinned gipsy, in velvet and corduroy. His movements were slow and lazy. His face was sullen, and his eyes were shifty. "What's the rumpus?" he growled. "This hussy has let me tell her fortune and won't ray, and she has struck me. I'm going to help myself." , "That's all right," growled 'Erb. He clutched at May's elbows from the back, and she gave a frightened scream. As she stood there, fixed and frightened, the gipsy woman seized the 'purse. "Hurry up,. old girl," 'Erb growled; "I can hear a noise like a motor." A voice at the back of the tree startled them. "If I were you,, it said, in. the clean.. cut, even tones of an English public school man,." I should do three things—l mean you two gipsy rascals." 'Erb f-.eeed the .girl's elbows, and they all turned round to inspect the newcomer. He was a big, upstanding Englishman, dressed in tweeds and without a hat. His hair was fair, and there was a smile on his lean face, while his blue eyes mocked the gipsy's glowering glances. "What would you do if you were us?" the you have already The other duty dg oi ps ‘i:oyo nb%wyr ooufnl ehtd, unhanding dr itunhdgrele et b. y thingse lady. is to give her back the purse. The nonchalant, careless -air of the new- comer cowed the gipsies. May, on looking at this six feet of confident manhood, not only recovered from her brief fright, but began to enjoy the situation. Suppose we don't?" said 'Erb, truculently. "‘ There is no supposing," the fair man an- swered. "This is not a children's game. You just do it, now, or—" "Or what?" 'Erb growled. "I'll give you the biggest hiding you have ever deserved, you brown-skinned bully, and I'll have you and that black-eyed fury in the hands of the police in ten minutes, or my name's not Rupert for nothing!" 'Erb rushed at the laughing man facing him. It was the ,mistake of his life. Rupert knocked both his hands up as he came on, with the practised case of a, professional boxer, caught the gipsy a heavy blow in the velvet waistcoat, which sent his head forward, and knocked the head back with a second blow which toppled the gipsy on to the long g Pram. "To resume where I was interrupted," Rupert began, turning to the gipsy woman— "the la idy s still waiting for her purse." The gipsy woman smiled .with the wheedling flattery of the fortune-teller. She handed May the purse. "It was only a little play-acting," she said. "Yes," said Rupert, a melodrama with a comic stage-villain and secondhand gipsy fortune-teller in it, tryilig to frighten the deknceless heroine. It is lucky I-turned up, because melodrama never goes really well without the hero." _ . _ .

He stirred the big ruffian with •his foot. Get up and say you're sorry," Rupert com- manded. _ _ _

The man rose slowly. "I'm sorry," he growled at May. "Don't mention it, Mr. 'Erb," she replied. "16 that enought—eurse you I? 'Erb asked, .surlily, turning to Rupert._ _ _

'Take him away before I hurt him, Rupert replied, looking at the gipsy woman.

They both began to shamble away in the airection of the road. May picked up her paints and turned to her picture, and, though her hand trembled, Rupert admiringly noted that she was under Superb control, and did not show a hint of her recent fright.

After a pause May %spoke. "It was very kind of you to get me out of such a diffi- culty," she began, absent-mindedly painting in a piece of cloud. "Most opportune," he admitted; "and quite orthodox. Together they sat over an impromti and .Rupert astonished the girl by the vigour of his appetite. They made' a hilarious hour of it. "I must be going," she said at length. The nearest station is two miles."

" Yes, but I'm a wizard. Where do you go in Saddleborough, may I ask?" "To the Oaks," she answered. _

"I know—Dr. Ellimore's place. He's an old friend of mine. We were at Oxford to- gether." "He is my uncle," she explained. "I am staying there for the summer." Well, I'll make a sporting bet with you. I'll give you ten minutes on the bicycle, and undertake to be there first. If I am, you owe me six kisses; if I am not, I'll owe you a dozen pairs of gloves." She looked round the empty horizon. She remembered the long road to the nearest station, and she was convinced. "It's a bet," she answered.

He helped to pack the easel and paints, and waved his hand as she mounted her machine. "You are a very reckless man," she sug- gested. "I give you ten clear minutes," he answered. "I shall kiss you six times, during the day, or you'll be owing them to me." "I don't know how you are going to do it, but "—her eyes ahgtr, a swift challenge—" I'll pay if I lose." 'Well, good-bye. "No motor-car and no bicycle—remember." "Honest Injun," he answered, and waved his hand as he saw her push on, rapidly, along the nearly white highway. •

May -aughed to herself as she steadily drove the bicycle along the road to Saddle. borough. "It was really astonishing that he should turn up with his blue eyes and fair hair. I begin to believe in gipsies. And he really has a nice smile. Yes, and there's some good in a man after all, when he plies such a—what do the sporting papers call it I'---such .a straight left. But I shall win my dozen of gloves. I don't think he can possibly arrive. He cer- tainly cannot run there faster than I can cycle, with ten minutes' start. And I know there is no train until nearly six."

She was musing thus as she sped speedily on the way to Saddleborough. She did not lose any time, because she was not quite sure of her knight of the afternoon. He had a whimsical, laughing face, hut he seemed to be astonishingly able to take care of himself, and to be an unlikely man to make rash bets, which would result in his humiliation before her uncle, the doctor, and herself.

She remembered also the final assurance that he would claim the kisses. Womanlike, she did not wish to see him win and yet half , hoped he would.

Ahe had eaten up a gn od three miles Of he journey—the road winthug past her like a piece of white ribbon—when the sound of something like a motor behind made the girl start.

"No, it cannot be 'him," she thought, ai instinctively she itssociated the noise with her cavalier. I am sure he would not break the conditions." She tossed her •• head at the thought of it. "Any way, I shall not pay:" The noise behind her increased, but she did not look round. It WM different from any motor she had ever heard. The rhythmic beat of the engine was the same, but there was no noise of wheels on the road, and the sound seemed high above her. Her doubt. was soon removed. The noise behind was rapidly overhauling her. It was practically upon May, but she would not stay her course and turn round. In some instinc- tive way- she associated the. sound with Rupert. ,

Iter instinct was right. A voice challenged her. " Hullo ! Hullo ! Hullo!" it sang out— overhead.

Something dark flew above her and ahead in the air, about a hundred yards high. She recognised it at a glance as a very up-to-date form of the biplane. She could see its driver perched on a precarious seat under the 'big wings. And she would have recognised him, if she 1ui4.1 not already recognised the voice. The great fabric dipped a little and swung round. Its driver waved his hand as he circled about her. She could hear the whirr of the ensine, and above it all, as the biplane resumed its direction, the sound of b:.

" Six kisses at six" he W 56 shout 0, triumphant song.

She stopped her machine and dismounted. " Bounder !" she cried, half in laughter, half in earnest, and 'moved her hand menacingly.

He swept round once more, and again, over the whirr of the machinery, she could hear his voice, still singing a sort of chant "Sea you at six, six kisses at six." Then, with a final wave of his hand, the, driver of the bi- plane turned towards Saddleborough, and the -big white Wings, gleaming in the sun, quickly drew away unfit they receded to the size of a seagull, and finally vanished altogether.

May did not hurry to her uncle' .4 home She was feeling, again, the temporary weak- ness of her sex and the crushing capability of the male. And she was also busily employed ~on possibilities of evading the price. It was .just, about 5.30 when she reached the Oaks at Saddleborough. She 'pushed her bicycle hastily into the coachhouse, and went to- wards the Hall. _ ,

LATEST REPORTS.

Confirmation of the fact, that rheumatism is crippling an unusually large number of people this autumn has been received from almost every district in the kingdom. Adults of both sexes complain of being dis- abled more or less. With some persons the trouble starts in the joints, the shoulders and back being also affected. Others experience the greatest torture in the muscles of the limbs. From Hull evidence is to hand. Mr. P. Penna, of 23, Garden-street, states: "Rheuma- tism started in my knee joints, and crippled me so that I could only hobble with a stick. Em- brocations and liniments failed.. I took medi- cine and rested, but my joints swelled; fearful shooting pains flew through me, and for several weeks 1 was bed-ridden. Then a friend told me to try a course of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. Thanks to these splendid pills the rheumatism was speedily banished." Not a.few of the reported instances of 'sudden severe pain in the nether limbs clearly refer to sciatica, an ailment affecting the great sciatic nerve that runs from the hips to the heels. Mr. W. Franklin, of 8, Hermitage-walk, Frank- well, Shrewsbury, reports an experience as follows :

Cold, wet autumn weather began the attack," Mr. Franklin explained, and then pro- ceeded: "I thought the dreadful torture would never leave me, and that• the sciatica would cripple me for • life. I was in awful agony; pains flying from my hips down my legs every minute, day and night. Treatment seemed in vain until I turned to Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. From the first few doses the pills brought immense relief; before long the sciatica was cured and all pain gone. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills alone did it." Those of the fair sex who have already ex- perienced painful reminders of the evil in the form of acute pains in the limbs and .shoulders, should read the advice of one sufferer from Coventry, who states: "At one time every spell of bad weather left me crippled with rheuma- tism; often I was completely helpless. The pains usually settled in, my back and muscles. Frequently I was bedridden, and could not turn, in bed or help.myself. Nothing oared Me until I was ,persuaded to try Dr.. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. Then the rheumatism got easier, my general health improved, and the pills drove the rheumatism right away." This comes to hand from Mrs. E. Collier, of 13, Field- row, Stoke, Coventry. Your name and address forwarded on a post- card Of 46, Holborn Viaduct, London, E.C., will bring by return of post an interesting health guide free. BED-WARMERS IN AFRICA.

Interesting accounts of the adventures of a party of Gertnan explorers in Africa itu'e given by Adolph Friedrich, Duke of Mecklenburg, in a book entitled "From the Conger to the Niger and the Nile." Three chapters of the book are by the Duke and the remainder 14. his com- panions. The artist of the party visited the 'country of the Musgums, where the people live in conical-shaped huts with fluted decorations, which are really beautiful to look at and Vi,orthy of the name of architecture. The huts are about 35 feet high. "The first. time I entered one of these huts I was very muCh astonished.; the voice echoed from the smooth inner .walls, and a dim light penetrated from an opening in the roof, Which also served as a chimney. In front of me stood a. richly ornamented coffin-hke structure, which I ascertained ,to be the house- holder's bed. At the foot of the bed was an opening communicating with a small pipe ; lire is lighted in, it on cold nights in order to warm the bed. Bed-warmers in Central Africa I The walls were richly ornamented, and the Nvork, though irregular, had a distinct ptyle of its own. I cannot imagine how these pfimitive Nlusgunis can have learned to draw, thee designs, which I have .never seen elsewhere." • The Musgum women disfigure their lips in a terrible fashion by the introduction of metal plates and other objects. " This custom is ter- ribly disfiguring, and in old women the lip often gives way, the torn edges hanging down on each side. As a result Musguin women are no longbr bought as slaves at Dikoa, or at any of the -Sudanese slave markets."

Part of the Duke's journeyings included some exploration of Lake Chad and a few' of its unex- plored Nlands lying in the centre of this region of mystery. In the Bagirmi country, on the shores of the lake, the Duke .found a curious system of coinage. "Money is replaced by the. gabak, a strip of cotton about one inch wide and 100 yards long; this length is equivalent to three francs,' and is divided into yard lengths: Anyone who intends to make large purchases must, therefore, bring great rolls of this mate- 'rial, suspended in pockets from each side of his saddle. The Government at Meta received in tribute and takes for the year 1910 36,000 francs, paid in the form of gabak ; this amounted to over a million of these strips, which not only filled all the warehouses to the' roof, but had to be stacked in heaps in the 'Open air." At Lai, in the military district of Lake Chad, he found that the taxes of that region were still paid everywhere in the form of iron knives of various shapes, two of which are reckoned as being worth about fivepence. " The interior of a warehouse containing 10.000 francs worth of these knives can readily be imagined, and the desire of the Government to introduce a coinage is not Unreasonable." . But the people will, have little to do with money, as they never leave their villages, and do not know what to do with it.
