WOMEN'S QUESIIO,t‘S.

Some Features of this Season's

Fashions.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
WORK ON COUNCILS.

A wide field for the activities of leisured women at home is opened Up by the 'admission Of the sex to sears on the municipal councils, Yet so far only eighteen .women have been elected. There are no fewer than three hun- drecrand eight councils in England and .Wales lacking women members, who have, where elected, proved most efficient. Their help is greatly needed in matters educational, in the sanitation of houses, in town planning, in the inspection of factories and workshops, of food, dairies and milkshops, in the 'housing of the working classes, in the erection of public baths and wash houses, municipal 4mperance re- forms, and in the control of cinematograph shows. Nor does this list exhaust the amount of work awaiting the women members of muni- cipal councils.

FEATHERS AND MOUNTS. Hat trimmings are still placed at extraordin- ary angles, and either shoot straight up a con- siderable height above the hat or spread them- selves out like the wings of aeroplanes. The big, ostrich plume is used rather differently this autnnan than ever before. The lower part of the feather is closely bound to the stem, up to .within an inch or two of the top, and there the fashionable tufted effect is obtained. All millinery trimming this autumn have this. pecu- liarity---they are long and slender in stem and broad in the top. The peacock feather would seem to have inspired this idea: For country and sporting hats the feathers Of the pheasant, drake, and merle are chiefly employed. The round-trimmed velours hats have small tufts of any of these, which, give them a ,decided indi- viduality. The longest quills fall short of the height demanded by fashion, and are therefore mounted on stems to make them still longer. Inexpenshre felt hats are decorated with feather bandeaux, to which a pair of wings is attached, and the effect is soft and pleasing.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
FRUIT AND BUTTERFLIES.

The single rose in deep shades of .velvet and silk, usually placed at one side of the hat, and the quaintest of cubist roses, in imitation suede, are the favourite flowers just now, if those of metal are excepted, which are extremely hand- some. Fruit is in great demand, especially apples, in natural shades, oranges, awns, mul- berries, &c., but all are tiny in size. All these can now be bought in the shops, and are made by. manufacturers, whereas 'until lately they were the work of the milliner, who constructed them ,when required. Many small close-fitting toques and turbans are decorated with bunches of these pretty miniature fruits. Lace butter- flies are .used to adorn not only hats, but dresses. On the former, they divide the honours with bumble bees, and other winged creatures. The most popular decoration for the übiquitous velvet hat is a butterfly (4 gigantic size, w:111 anlennce of contrasting colour--that apparently has just settled on the crown or brim. Velvet, plush and panne, the latter in very bright printed patterns, are the three leading materials in this season's millinery. Many hats are covered with suede finished cloth, which is much used in the making of the small fruits, and in some cases for quills also. VEILS AND BELTS. •

The new veils are very quaint, and are in- tended to cover the face more tightly than pre- vious fashions demanded. All the patterns are lace like, rosebuds on a check background being a pretty novelty. On big hats, lace veils are wound. round the brim in rather a complicated manner, and take sortie little time to arrange satisfactorily. Indeed, all veils require more or less skill in their adjustment., and the effect of a smart and successful tbilette may be entirely spoiled by a carelassly arranged veil. Some women seem to be born with the gift of put- ting on a veil, whilst others never acquire it. The new belts are the most important feature of this season's fashions, and will continue so throughout the winter. They are to all intents and purposes, sashes; rather than belts, are usually made of silk, softly swathed around above and below the waistline, and slightly raised in front. The ends are variously treated, and often lap One over the other, and have a big butterfly bow as a finish. A high sash of moire silk on an evening dress is arranged in flat, wide folds, two big. pointed loopsThtanding high above the folds, and the whole finished by two broad ends, falling low on the skirt of the toilette.

THE PERENNIAL BLOUSE. Blouses are a perennial source of interest in the world of fashion. They c'ertainly play a very important part in the scheme of this season's styles and modes. Every day they get more like bodices and like waistcoats. The three-piece costume has, a blouse of the same colour, sometimes of the same fabric, made in a loose-litting style, which is more or less pic- turesque, to complete it. Then for morning wear, white or coloured shirts and blouses are as much in demand as ever. Whether this gar- ment is intended for smart afternoon, evening, or for morning wear; the shoulders must be wide, and ty general looseness of fit characterise the cut. But the details are of the daintiest, the finish perfect, hemstitching and picot edging being in general use. Fur, of course, is a favourite adornment for the fashionable' blouse. Many women will welcome the return of the semi-evening blouse of lace, silk, or satin, with a black silk skirt of the rustling variety, which is a change, if fcir the worse, .from the clinging soft charmeuse and other limp materials, to which fashion has ,clung persist- ently for quite a long time.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
IN CHOOSING AND MAKING TEA.

Very few people 'realise that the best tea, if fifty percent. dearer than the inferior sorts, is at least one h4ndred per cent. better, and the difference in cost amounts to a comparatively stim in the course of a year, where this beverage is taken in moderation, for less tea is required when She quality is good. To make tea properly is an art few people'acquire, and servants are altogether incapable of learning. An authorly on the subject advises that the water should be toiled in an aluminium sauce- pan, kept for the purpose, and most carefully watched as the boiling point ,is being reached. Then the tea should be thrown in with one hand, whilst the saucepan is removed from the gas or fire 41th the other. It should infuse five minutes, with the lid on. At lite end of that time the tea should be poured into a hot tea p(); the leaves remaiaing in the saucepan, and be served at once.

DOCTORS AND SMOKE.

Drs. Bennett and Manson Reply to the Mayor.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
IS SMOKE BENEFICIAL?

Opinions on the Relation of Smoke and Health.

DR. BENNETT'S DEADLY LOGIC.

TO THE EDITOR OF "THE EXAMINER

see from last week's " Examiner the Mayor states he has given proof of his assertion

that smOke is, beneficial to health. When, where, or how did he do it?. No one seems to know. That sulphurous ,acid gas Was used as a disinfectant 40 years ago, and smoke con- tains ,a small amount of this, is no argument that smoke is beneficial. To get the bacteria destroying properties of sulphurous acid gas it must be so Concentrated or so moist as to be absolutely irrespirable to human beings. He forgets to tell the public that when this gas was I,lsed to disinfect rooms, all the chinks of the door had to be pasted over with paper to. pre- vent the obnoxious fumes, even in this 'small quantity, getting into the rest of the house, to the. injury of the other inhabitants, and the room was uninhabitable for the next 21 to 48 hours ;, also he fOrgets to -tell the public of the damage it did in the room to such things as metal fittings, &c., brass bedsteags being prac- tically ruined, so far as appearance goes. He 'forgets to tell us that sulphurous acid is very greedy for moisture and oxygen, that on which our life depentls, and that it readily becomes converted into vitriol, destroying most things, Particularly vegetation. He also forgets to tell us that smoke contains carbonic acid gas, the very gas our lungs are constantly patting rid of as a necessary part ,of, our existence; also, that it contains something even more deadly, namely, carbon' monoxide. These and many other substances are found in the products of Incomplete combustion. Why, it smoke were half as beneficial as the Mayor would have us believe it is, we should never have heard of Lord Lister and antiseptic surgery, at least in manufacturing towns, 'for the simple reason that, his discovery would never have been 'needed. That sulphurous acid gas, now dis- carded, was once used as a disinfectant, is no Proof that smoke—a highly complex body—is beneficial to health.

Luis only other point is.that,possibly an occa- sional asthmatic has benefited by, going into town. Hear what Professors 'Parkes and-Ken- 'wood say. (both medical officers of health and medical school lecturers in London, and authors of a standard work on public health and hygiene):.— _ _ .

"Sulphurous acid and soot are undoubtedly injurious to ..many asthmatics and. to - people ,suffering from bronchitis. During 'dense fog the mortality from lung diseases always in- creases; sulphurous products contained in fog nre extremely irritating to ,the „respiratory mucous membrane."

But there is another side to the question of the aBthmatic ; many such are only improved by removal into the country from the town, as have only recently had an opportunity to observe, and if sulphurous. acid gas is all that is required. to cure them, it could easily be iokeii. to the patient, instead- of bringing the patient to it. But supposing an odd, case did improve by moving into town, is that any adequate reason why 75,000 other people, each liable to 1100 different diseases, all of Which are made worse by smoke, should be debarred from try- ing to improve conditions which they know from experience are injurious to them. If not Injurious, why are local doctors—the Mayor in- cluded—constantly recommending patients to .go, away from them? I myself have seen the Mayor staying for weeks together at Colwyn Bay, evidently seeking a purer air. • If he only Wanted change and rest, Widnes and • Wigan ore much nearer. Only last Thursd,ay I. saw about a- dozen patients all going to various convalescent homes in Southport. Why 06 our local Infirmary authorities have arrangements to send patients to convalescent homes at Southriort and elsewhere, where there is no commercial smoke to aid their recovery? It inust be a wicked 'waste of public money. Why has not the Mayor, as a 'member of the tlealth 'Committee, seen to the erection of an zisthma sanatorium at the bottom of Pierpoint- street, if the smoky atmosphere is ,so benefi- cial? No! as an Irishman .(Hibernians, please Pardon) might say, the air would have to be so full of sulphurous acid gas to be of any bene- tit to the health of man, that nobody could live in it. Then its disinfectant properties might be obvious. But if we do need this gas, et us have it Without the other elements of .smoke. . It is these we are seeking primarily to diminish, if Possible. If it cannot be done without injury to trade, then we are quite content to be as we are. To seek health from. breathing . smoke, becatthe it contains 'd trace of -sulphurous held gas, is like trying to ,sweeten tea with gas tar, because it contains saccharin. Bearing in mind' the dirt and deStruction Which smoke brings about, alsO its -.poisonous Products as detailed above, does the hypothe-

Wm-Ica—se of an- occasional asthmatic benefiting, together with the fact that smoke contains sul- .Phurous acid gas—said by leading authorities to he "undoubtedly injurious "-,-satisfy the pub- that smoke is good for the health? Has the Mayor proved his rase? Now the Mayor says I made assertions with- °tit proof. Let tlit public judge. I state TtclUal eases, which are . only a fraction of the .e,llses I, or, for that .matter, any other practi- ' lioner could state, covering any, number of Itifferent diseases, whilst, he states the disputed ase of the asthmatic. How many patients "Lave during the last 50 years been sent out of warrington to seek health? Thousands and thousands. Flow many have , been ,sent in 15-l Part from hospitals) for the same purpose?' ::tlave,yet to learn of one, beyond the one men- ' 'toned by the Mayor, and I should not like to sec any of my friends risking their reputations „ ',Yen 01-lithe asthmatic. This would be a doubt-. ul experiment, whereas' the other—the sending proved a thousand times over., I should be sciry to accuse the Mayor of in- inecrity, but his, inference that I wanted a condition of perpetual strike because clear skleS and sunsets Were then noticeable, is such Lt. Perversion, Of what I did say, that I can believe he inferred it, and I am glad lie Was publicly Corrected at the time. Perhaps 1 _.;O. Medical -man took a deeper interest in the Tstress of the poor at that time than I (lid, and ~I°r nothing I pave over done have I 'been so 14'ecIUently thanked as for this. May I say that no attack whatever was made 0 nthe Mayor as a medical men at the smoke oratement meeting? Had he been a butcher a baker the remarks would have been uflhly tipplicable. His all too sensitive nature z_used him to intrude his personality into the o%,rgutnent at the Council meeting, when, no thoughtat the smoke abatement meeting even l Of it. Let the public judge alSo as to iw (-) Mayo's good taste in discussing the lige of "of his critics in a public debate. If he re- ti.,l2(ls his opinions as above criticism, then he no business in public life, and judgment as jouroolc Whether he ngheeohmaisproved his case or not would from outside, rather than in himself.

JAMES BENNETT

Dit. MANSON STANDS FIRM.

TO THE EDITOR OF "THE EXAMINER."

Sir;‘---In your last Issue you devoted a good deal of space to the Mayor's attack on me at the last Council meeting. The Mayor based his criticism's on your report of my remarks at the initial meeting of the Warrington branch of the Smoke Abatement League, and as he asserted that I made an "unfair attack" on him, I should like to reply and clear. away his mis- conceptions.

Let me assure him and your readers that I had no idea of a personal attack. All I ven- tured to do was to criticise his publicly ex- pressed opinion, that smoke was "possibly beneficial."

Coming from so influential a source, this opinion, if -allowed to pass, would naturally have hindered, the movement, we were all so anxious to establish. He appeared to think that I was, in some way, referring to him when I. said "that the men in the medical profession would sooner try and keep the .people healthy than give them a bottle of medicine." What I did say was, "That since the advent of the Insurance Act it was to the self-interest of the medical profession to try and keep people healthy, rather than give them a bottleof medi- cine." I said this because the motive of self- interest is understood by the majority of ,people, and such a' confession protects one from being considered a faddist! Further on, in reply to Mclennan Bennett, the Mayor said, He called me childish," I did nothing of the kind. What 1 did say was, "The opinion • that .smoke was not injurious to health was too childish to be considered."

It is now 38 years ago since a Conservative Government under Disraeli passed the great Public Health Act of 1875, and no one can say that that Government Ntould have been in a hurry to declare, as it did in Section 91, that black smoke was a nuisance and injurious to health, unless it lied good grounds for, its action. The term " childish " in the sense of being immature, is not, I think, an inappropri- ate term to apply td an opinion which might have been held in the childhood of. deveVing- industrialism. In my remarks -I referred tO the Mayor's views on the other ingredients of smoke be- sides the carbon particles, and I pointed out that these would be more likely to injure the living tissues, such as the. epithelial. 'cells of the bronchial tube, than the organisms of disease. Far from ridiculing him, I treated his contentions quite seriously. The Mayor admits that Dr. Niven is an authority on the question at issue, and the fol- lowing are some of Dr. Niyen's 'conclusions quoted, verbatith from his paper on "The Rela- tion of Smoke and Health."

"To sum up, there is no doubt whatever that,. both directly and indirectly, smoke causes serious injury .to health, especially during the inontliS of November, December and -January. This injury is manifested in an increased re- spiratory mortality. The same influence, how- ever, must affect the population in all still and heavy states of the atmosphere, though in a lesser degree. In addition, at .all times smoke injures the health of the Manchester population by cutting off the rays of sunlight, which het injuriously on the micro-organisms of disease. It is of vital interest to the health of Manches- ter that a great effort should be made to dimin- ish still further the smoke and fog prevalent here. The first and necessary step is by every possible means .to diminish the mission of smoke. To this end the emission of st-noke, under Section 91 of the Public Health Act, 1875, should be subject to cumulative penalties."

As far as I can gather from your report of Dr. Joseph's speech, he is inclined to doubt the validity of Dr. Niyen's conclusions, because the latter deals with mortality statistics in their relation to fogs. Dr. Niven is in no doubt him- self, for he specifically states "that the effects thus demonstrated are due to the smoke in the fog, and not, merely to the occurrence of fog." Many yew's ago one of the medical officers of health of Glasgow—Dr. Russell, I• think—came to practically the same conclusions, after a similar study.

In Germany, Dr. Ascher, medical officer of Konigsberg, made a study of the effects of smoke in two districts in the industrial area of Westphalia. He found l in the centre district— Gelsenkirchen—that the mortality from acute non-tuberculous pulmonary diseases was almost twice that in the border district, ffamm, and .the difference was nearly trebled in the tikge- period between 15-60 years—the working portion of human life. The populatiOns in both districts are almost equal, and they are en- gaged in similar trades. No • doubt other re- searches of this kind would go far to confirm the a priori opinion that a smoky atmosphere is directly injurious to health. And lastly, if I remember aright, I also made some remarks on the economic side of the smoke problem from tne medical point of view. I pciinted out that the regular employment of the people was a necessary factor in the main- tenance of health, and that .the policy of the Smoke Abatement 4eague should be to try and harmonise the economic and hygienic aspects of the smoke question. I think it should adopt as its motto the Shavian maxim: "The love of economy is the root of all virtue." J. MANSON. 8, Winmarleigh-street, Oct. 27, 1913.

THE ,EXAMINER, SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 1, 1913. SMOKE CONTROL. 'EVIL EFFECTS OF SMOKE.IACTWE WOMEN LIBERALS.

What Manchester is Doing with I Some Expert Opinionsin Concise Form Success.

Sulphur Fumes From ,Chimneys.

The meeting of the Coal Smoke Abatement Society, held in connection with the Exhibition at Shepherd's Bush by the municipal and private gas undertakings of the United King- dom, was presided oVer by Sir William %H. Bennett, who discuwd the loss to health and ' pocket caused by the smoke nuisance due to factories, locomotives, and the domestic hearth. Seventy-five per cent. of the heat, he said, is wasted, and-deccrations,.works of art, fabrics and buildings are destroyed by the action of the fog and sulphur fumes for which it is responsible. It spoils the growth of trees and plants, and indirectly increases the doc- tor's bill. Again, though the abolition of smoke will not altogether prevent the occur- rence of fogs, their effects for evil will be cnn- siderably curtailed. For instance, the 200,000 ,tons of sulphur which is poured over London every year from coal fires would no longer exist, and Londoners would be free from an irritative and destructive element.

Cuts Off the Sun's Rays. Professor Vivian B. Lewes, who spoke of the gas industry in its relation to smoke abate- ment, mentioned that, soot falls in London to the amount of 76,000 tons a year. Though dirty and disagreeable, it is not the most damaging part of smoke.., Soot settles readily from the air, and is washed down quickly by rain, and better still by snow, so that although its dirtying action is great,- it does not do the amount. of harm. that is brought ',about by the cloud of floating vesicles of tar containing flue gases and vapours- that enable them to survive ung such tithe as rough impact with solid :sub- stances cause them- VI burst. It is the tar that glues the dirt to our buildings and stops the pores of animal and plant life, that causes the overhead cloud which cuts off the sun's rays and shortens the hours of yitalising sunshine. It is the tar that 'coats the particles of water in a white mist and transforms it into a yellow fog that lasts until heavy rain or wind clears it away; and tar gives what we term the cumula- tive effect of smoke which darkened the clos- ing years of the last century.

Greatest Evil Created kly Civilisation. Sir William Redmond, FLA., described coal smoke as perhaps the greatest evil, "outside what is positively wicked, that modern civilisa- tion has created." Smoke was not ,necessary to business, but a serious and very expensive nuisance, and manufacturers should be made to pay for its presence. "Absolute consmnp- Lion of coal," said Sir William, "is possible; it is not a Utopian ideal that it may be so. It is so. Very well, then, in the name of all that is sensible, economical, just and wise, coal smoke from factories should be 'made penal."

===== 0002601_19131101_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
SOME OBJECT LESSONS.

Law Administered with Consideration and in.teiligence.

In view of the formation of a Warrington Smoke Abatement League, it is of interest to see what a neighbouring city and its Smoke Abate- ment League has done. This Letigue has just held its Second Annual Meeting, and the Annual Report and an account of the meeting are before us. This meeting was held in the Mayor's Par- lour, an evidence of the good relations between the League and the Authorities. These good relations are manifest in many ways. There is -an absence of that official jealousy, which so often paralyses voluntary effort, and at the same time cuts off the support which official action needs to make it effective. The Manchester Sanitary Authority and the League work cor- dially together. The method of measuring smoke is the same. We believe that the Chair- man of our Health Committee expressed the opinion that the League's inspector, in his recent visits to Warrington, must have classed smoke as black longer than do the Warrington Anspectors. If this be so, Warrington method must differ from the Manchester oflicial method, as well. as that of the League.. This inspector's evidence is sometimes applied for and -freely given in support of the Manchester inspector's , evidence, arid carries •weight. A further noteworthy feature of the Manches- ter Corporation work in regard to smoke is tileir mastery, of the situation. They have estab- lished their authority, the manufacturers acknowledge it, and regulate their doings accordingly. There is no idea of a reversal of thi. situation as we see in many smaller towns, where, through the weakness of the authorities, the manufacturers, through their power, estab- lish an authority over a Health Committee. In Manchester warnings go out almost daily; if these are persistently disregarded, the offender is brought to Court and fined,- and the fine in- creases with each offence (this under a special Act). The control is exercised patiently, but firmly. If an offender shows evidenee that he will put his house in order, time is granted. But the Corporation do not tolerate any trifling with their authority. All works are under the control of the law, including all classes of Work for manipulating iron; none are exempted. Puddling of iron is not done in Manchester. The result of this firm, patient administration has resulted in cordial relations being established between the manufacturers and the Corporation just 6cause the relative positions are clearly defined and established. The Corporation will not suffer unnecessary smoke; manufacturers know this, and they further know that the law is administered with consideration and intelli- gence. The 'remit is that while Manchester, although far from perfect, is, as far as manufac- turers' smoke is concerned, probably the best controlled, and sends out less smoke in propor- tion to its industries, than any town in Lanca- shire.

A Disgrace to Civilisation.

As is well known, Manchester is one of the smokiest towns in,Lancashire, worse than War- rington, the reason being that Xis ringed round with manufacturing towns, in which the con- trol is less efficient than in Manchester. . From whatever quarter the wind. blows it brings smoke to Manchester. Then there is the huge population and their household fires sending out the dirtiest and worst class of smoke. This is what makes Mancheiter smoky, not the Man- chester manufacturers' smoke. An evidence of the intelligent way the Man- chester City Council is dealing with the question is the appointment of an Air Pollution Ad- visory Board. .This iS a sub-committee of the Sanitary Committee, and has power to co-opt experts from outside. One of the things this committee has taken up is a systematic method of recording soot fall in Manchester. Most of the progressive towns nave adopted a like system. To reNrn to the meeting of the Smoke Abate- ment League. Lord Newton was in the chair, and gave, as usual, a racy speech. "The atmos- pheric condition of this part of the country was a disgrace to civilisation. [Was Warrington included?] No other part in the earth's surface presented such a melancholy appearance. A person who never left Manchester, never saw the Sunshine as he ought, never knew the real colour of a tree trunk, and probably went to his grave convinced that all sheep were born black. He remembered that some years ago, when the last Lord Derby but one went to St. Helens to cut the first sod of a railway, some- body remarked 'You cannot find a green sod in St. Helens to cut,' and Lord Derby replied, 'A very creditable thing, too.' That Lord Derby was a typical British Philistine of the Victorian era. • The type represented thought that any attempt to beautify the surroundings of the people represented a kind of maudlin ,sentiment which ought to be discouraged."

No Smoke and Substantial Economies. The Hon. Secretary of the branch, Mr. E. D. Simon, gave an interesting instance, of a Manu- facturers' Ass'ociation in Hamburg. An engi- neer, specially qualified, was employed by the Associati9n to test the eflicency-of various plants and fuels, and advise the members how to secure economy, and at the same time make their chimneys smokeless. The two things are the same. The Association now represented 473 firms and controlled 1,600- boilers, and all the cblinneyS connected With.those boilers were' riOW, virtually sniokeless. The direct result had been that the coal bills of the manufacturerg• had been reduced by about 20 per cent. on the average. The formation of such a League in this country would be one Of the best steps that could be taken.

The report tells us that out of the 139 firms that have been reported by the League's inspec- tor, 19 have set about making alterations in their plants since the League got into Communi- cation with them. The inspector reported that generally he had been well received. In many cases he had been told to come in whenever he saw the chimneys smoking, and say whatever he liked to the fireman. A striking case was given in the report of the relation of smoke to efficiency in boiler plants: "We have come across one interesting in- stance as regards. the relation of smoke to effi- ciency of boiler plant. Firm A, which has a very bad chimney, has four boilers, two of therb 37 years old. They use 60 tons of coal a week, at 13s. a ton, to produce 750 lh.p. for 55 hours. Firm. B, with a good chimney, uses 30 tons of coal a week, at 11s, a ton, to peoduce 750 lh.p. for 55 hours. , In both cases the:engines are of similar type and efficiency. That is to say, Firm A is paying £3O per week for coal, when they might actually save More than half of it, and pay only £l6 10s. per week, if their plant was as effiCient as that of B."

AN ANCIENT EDIFICE.

Canon Willis and the History of Warrington's Old Parish Church.

A RESTORATION SCHEME.

Still further restoration work is to be executed on the ancient Parish Church, and the form this would take was outlined in a speech de- livered by Canon Willis at the-first opening of a three days' bazaar, held in the Parish Church Schools on Thursday afternoon. Canon WILLIS, who presided, said many of sthem did not know the history of the church as well as he did, but it was a matter of great in- terest to him, and should be to all of them. There had been a church on the present site for 1,200 years—the first Celtic or British, and the last of the series English. There had been in all five churches---Saxon, Norman, Decorative, Georgian and Victorian: There were five naves and three chancels, and the three chancels, representing the Saxon, Nor- man and Decorative, still existed. Then there were two naves in the Georgian and Victorian styles. One chancel was an object of the great- est interest. It had existed for 550 years, and the chief feature in the chancel was a crypt vestry. For 200 years that crypt vestry was used as an oratory, and then for 300 years it was closed and forgotten, until 60 years ago, when it was re-opened and used as the clergy vestry.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
Loved the Parish Church.

A great deal had been done in the past in the way of restoration, and in the late Rector's time £15,000 had been raised and spent on the work. He had asked for £5,000, but got the £15,000. During the last 25 years ..t5,000 more had been spent, but still there was a great deal to be done. They had still to reflag part of the churchyard. They had to rebuild the old ves- try and to re-point the chancel and other work, for which they wanted all the money they could get. They had received £6OO for the new choir vestry, and they still required .tlOO for that work, and they would need further sums for the other restorations.

He could tell them that he loved the old Parish Church. He could not say that he liked it when he first came to Warrington, as he had been accustomed to a new church, but now the old church had become more dear to him than he could express, and his great desire, in addi- tion to the restoration, was to see a great im- provement in the services of the church. (Ap- plause.)

===== 0002601_19131101_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
The Opening Ceremony.

Mrs. LYON, who performed the opening cere- mony, hoped that they would all do their best to help the Rector to realise the splendid plans he had laid. for the extension of the 'Master's work. in Warrington. She was glad to see the growth of such vitality amongst those connected with the church, and she hoped that growth would still further develop in a most satisfac- tory way. She pressed upon them the necessity of furnishing the beautiful and useful additions to the church buildings. It was . particularly nice to finish anything one had undertaken, and it was still nicer to do it free of debt. She hoped they would be able to realise all the Rector wished. (Applause.) Mr. CRAIK proposed, as rector's warden, and Mr. R. T. HARDING, as people's warden, seconded, a vote of thanks to Mrs. Lyon.

CHALLENGE TO MR. H. SMITH.

The Women's Liberal Association of Warring- ton is a body in direct contrast to the Women's Tariff Reform League in more ways than one. First of all, the Women Liberals have a policy and an excellent legislative programme to sup- port, whereas Tariff Reform is dead, or at least totally abandoned by the leaders of the Tory party. Then again a great deal is boasted of what the Tariff Reform dames are going to do, but no idle boast is needed in regard to the Liberal women, who work quietly, but yet assiduously, for- the cause and principles they hold so dear. A ward social arranged by- the Women Liberals is always an enjoyable event, and when the first of the season, that of St. John's, was held in the Brick-street Schoolroom on Monday night, there was an exceedingly large attendance.

Mrs. Crosfield presided, and when, a little later, Mr. Crosfield arrived he was accorded a most enthusiastic reception, and it was some minutes before the audience ceased from cheer- ing. There were also on the platform Mr. Elliadi, Miss Elliadi, Mrs. Osborne, Mrs. Peacock, Mrs. Roberts, Mrs. Lindley, Mrs. Pemberton, Mrs. T. Locker, Mrs. J. S. Pendlebury, Mrs. Halliwell, Mrs. Hill, and the Misses Wilkinson, Councillor Shaw and Mr. E. G. Hughes. Prior to the meeting an excellent tea had been provided, Mrs. Roberts (ward captain), Mrs. Shaw and a number of the Committee ren- dering their valuable services willingly. Tea over, the large attendance assembled in the spacious room, and. a most enjoyable evening was spent.

MRS. CROSFIELD AND ULSTER AMAZONS. "This large. gathering, augurs well for the. success of " the Liberal party in Warrington." This was the opening sentence in Mrs. Cros- field's speech.' Continuing, she said: It shows you will all rally round the standard of Liberalism, for which we are fighting, and it is, no wonder you should do so, with leaders like those we have, who are such a contrast to the pitiable and dejected looking mortals on the Opposition Bench—(hear, hear)—and with a programme so crowded with legislation for the benefit of the British people. I will not detain you with the question of Home Rule to- night, because we have had a good deal about it lately, especially on Wednesday last. (Hear, hear, and applause.) I was sorry not to see more of you present on that occasion. We women must educate ourselves in all the poli- tical, questions, so that we shall be able to educate our children in them. Well, we heard a great deal about Home Rule on Wednesday, and Prdon't doubt but that Mr. Harold Smith heard even more. (Laughter and applause.) The Prime Minister made a most important speech last Saturday at' Ladybank, and now we all know' there is no need for discussing the .ques- tion further, because the Home Rule Bill will be quietly, but decidedly, passed. (Applause.) The only thing we don't know is, what will be the fate of the Ulster Volunteers, who have been drilling so ardently lately. (Laughter.) It crossed my mind that the Women's 'Tariff Re- form League would do well to change its title, because, as you have all heard, the Tories are shirking the question of Tariff Reform as hard as they can, and I am sure that their Member here, Mr. Harold Smith, will follow suit. Why should the Women's Tariff Reform League not call theiselves "The Women's League for the Protection of Ulster Amazon's"? (Laughter.)

making things easier and more attractive on the soil we shall get more agricultural labourers. More labourers in the country will help indir- ectly to raise the wages in the town.. If there is more labour on the soil there will be less people seeking employment in the towns, and that would help to keep up wages for labourers in the town. All this is due to Lloyd George and the Liberal Government. (Applause.) Of course, the Tory party will do their utmost to destroy this Bill and try. to prevent it from being passed, but they.will not succeed. (Hear, hear.) They themselves think they can df no wrong, and that the Liberal .party can di nothing right. This reminds of the story a a woman who very often quarrelled: with hei husband. Asked whether she. ever agreed with him, she said: "Sometimes we agree, and when we agree he (the husband) is right. Very often we disagree. When we disagree I am always right." (Loud laughter and applause.) You see she was right both ways. That is the way with the Tory party. I think, however, we can rely on the good sense of the British people to back up Mr. Lloyd George and the good sense of the Warrington Women's Liberal 'Association to back up the Liberal Government and the Liberal party as much as ever they can. (Loud applause.)

WOMEN AND HOME POLITICS. Mr. CROSFIELD caused much laughter at the outset when he addressed Mrs. Crosfield as "Mr. Chairman." He continued:

I am sure, ladies, I am full of gratitude for the cordiality and kindness of your reception. I have had a hint from Madam Chairman—and I am sure I beg her pardon, for the little slip just now—(laughter)—that I am not to exceed her time limit to-night, so I don't propose to detain you at great length, but it is a great pleasure to address any gathering of Liberal women in these times. We men are very proud of our positiozi uncle!' this Liberal Government, but, as I have said before, ladies, I really think that the most significant feature of modern politics; what gives them a reality and an added significance beyond what you have ever seen at any time in English politics before, is the 4-Ity in which the politics of the present day come right down into the home life of our people. (Hear, hear, and applause.) That is why Ido feel that modern Liberalism, if it appeals strongly to men, appeals even more directly to the women of our country. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19131101_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
Poverty in Rural Places.

But to return to the record of the Govern- ment. A most important; question is being brought to the front by Mr. Lloyd- George, and, as it will be much talked about and discussed by both parties, I think that you ought to know something about it. It concerns the ownership and occupation of land, and it is a most serious matter. Whereas in France, with a smaller population, there are 9,000,000 -people supported on the soil, here in Britain, with a larger popu- lation, there are only 1,500,000 supported on the soil. What is the reason of that It is partly because the conditions of labourers in this country are so shameful. A Special Committee was formed to make inquiries into the matter. They have found that the wages of labourers on the whole are not sufficient to support them properly. In four counties the wages of lab- ourers were 20s. and over: That is not so bad, but in five counties they were only between 19s. and 20s. In five counties between' .15s. and. 198.; in eight counties 17s. to 185.; in twelve counties 16s. to 175.; in two counties 17s. to 18s.; and in one county the wages were under 15s. per week. C' Shame.") You all know what that means, a man with a wife and family to live on 165., 17s. and 18s. ,per week. The real earnings of nearly 60 per cent. of the labourers have decreased since 1907. What happens is that the labourer is much too poor to pay a proper rent for his house or cottage, and as he cannot pay, it is not profitable for property owners to build houses or cottages. In 366 villages out of 800 there has not been a new cottage built during the last ten years. A man will not go to the expense of building a .house when it will not bring him in any return. The labourer com- plains of lack of accommodation, and also that he can be given notice at any moment. If a capricious landowner wishes it so, he has to bundle out.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. Lloyd George's Remedies.

All those difficulties cannot be got over, ex- cept if the wages of the labourers are increased, and that is what Mr. Lloyd George wishes to do—(applause)—to make it much easier for every man on the land, not only to have proper wages, but to have land of his own. (A Voice: "So he should.") I quite agree with you. In olden times there was the common, which was common property. A man could keep his poultry, grow his own potatoes, and perhaps even keep a pig, was his own master, and not obliged to work for others, whether he wanted to or not. The commons have most unjustly disappeared, and the land has been taken by individuals. Lloyd George wants to create allotments and give the labourers the chance to acquire a piece of land of their own. If they are hard-working and energetic they may some day become small- holders, and from that the career of farming is open to them, and the most capable and ener- getic can become important farmers in the land. Then in regard to rents. Sometimes there are reasons why rents should be raised, but sometimes when the landlord takes it into his head they are raised for no reason whatever. Lloyd George wants to make these conditions better, to prevent further injustice, and by

AN ANCIENT EDlFlCE—continued.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
Liberal Aims and Achievements.

For the last ten years you have been engaged in helping us, and in co-operating with uS, in the Liberal party, to protect our homes from Protection. (Cheers and laughter.) But wo have not been satisfied with having safeguarded that great policy‘ of Free Trade, won 70 years since with so much effort, and defended so suc- cessfully nowadays. We are not satisfied with that, and we are going on with our constructive work; and what does that mean? What is the upshot of it all? I think I can put it to you without exaggeration that we are; in these days, helping to make the path a little smoother for those who find life's journey hard and stony. (Hear, hear, and applause.) We are helping to make the cruelty of circumstance a little less severe and pitiless for those who have fallen ot evil times. (Hear, hear.) When yoli think 01 the tens of thousands of homesteads which, in old England, will be saved from misfortune, misery and positive disaster by this splendid Insurance Bill that has been passed, I am sure you will bless the men and the Government who are helping our people to make provision against the rainy day and hard times, which may over- take some of us. When you think there are just about 1,000,000 of our old people to-day who are able to hold their heads high, and spend their declining years with some little comfort, yes, and with some dignity as independent citizens, I am certain there is not one of you in this room who does not feel very deeply what a really great blessing for all time the Old-ale Pensions Act is: (Loud applause.) When yon did me the honour to return me to the House of Commons, during the years I was there I had the privilege of sitting on the Com- mittee which dealt with the first Bill ever intro- duced to relieve sweated workers—(cheers)— those who most required the State's assistance. Many of them were men, but more of them were women, and those Women who were so shame- fully ,underpaid are now getting about half as much again as they got before—(applause)— sometimes more, sometimes less than half as much again. Their wages may still be low, but the advance they have received is to them an enormous increase. (Hear, hear.) And this Act has established a precedent which we are extending as time goes on. (Hear, hear.) Mrs, Crosfield tells me she has spoken to you on the land question, and there again the movement for a minimum wage for agricultural labourers is not the least significant feature of that great beneficent and hopeful policy that is being un- folded before your eyes. Past, present and future, we are engaged in a good and blessed work, and, look where I will, I find the leiberal party in good fettle and full of cheerfulness.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
Turning and Rending.

I feel cheerful, too, and not a bit less cheerful when I read the remarks of Mr. Harold Smith —(laughter)—who gives me a very plain hint that I had better look out for the time %hen my fellow-citizens in Warrington may turn ttnd rend me. (Laughter, and a Voice: “Oh I") I hope he did not mean that too literally. I don't want to see any turning and rending going on. I take it that he spoke, I am sure he spoke, metaphorically, and, that being so, friends, all I can tell Mr. Harold Smith is that he had better look elsewhere, and a little nearer home, if he thinks there is going to be any turning and rending. I have got no fear of it. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Last week I had something to say about Mr. Harold Smith and his perform- ances in Ulster and elsewhere, and I will, in conclusion, say a word more now. The Primq, Minister made an important speech on Satur- day, and you will see by the report that Mr. Asquith makes it perfectly clear that we are not going to be deterred by any threats of vio- lence or disloyalty, from carrying through our policy. May I say once more that if any people suffer through these misguided and mischievous proceedings ,my syMpathy will be entirely for the poor dupes—for those who have been mis- led by leaders who ought to be ashamed of themselves. To try tc work up religious anti- pathy between people holding different religious opinions in the United Kingdom is utterly out of date and disgraceful. (Hear, hear, and ap- plause.) About a century ago a great English- man was travelling in Greece to see those relics, records and monuments of antiquity, which the ancestors of my wife, her father and sister, have made for ever famous in the world. (Ap- plause.) He had as his companions men of different nationalities and different faiths. I will give you a few lines, in which he describes them:

We were of all tongues and creeds; Some were those who counted beads; Some of Mosque, and some of Church, And some, or I missay, of neither; Yet through the wide world might yet f search, Nor find a mother crew, nor blither.

There you have, friends, broad-minded com- mon-sense and toleration. From this platform (Continued in next column.)

ACTIVE WOMEN LIBERALS—cont.

I ask Mr. Harold Smith whether he has ever even considered what would happen in the British Empire, where you have slich endlees. diversities of creed, such endless diversities in the shades of religious beliefs, if, because of these very differences, they were all to fly at each other's throats? (Hear, hear.) Yes, • friends, and what would happen if the responsible lead- ers of opinion throughout the British Empire were to encourage them to do it? -

===== 0002601_19131101_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
CHALLENGE TO MR. H. SMITH:

As Mr. Smith is rather fond of challenges, those are questions which I here and now chal- lenge him to answer. (Applause.) I tell Mr. Smith that in these days we have no use for the narrow-minded, bigoted, discreditable, execrable gospel of intolerance and ascendency, which has been preached by those who ought to know better. (Hear, hear.) I thank you for your kindness in giving me the opportunity of saying just a_ few words.

I congratulate you on being associated with the Free Trade and Liberal party in these stir- ring times. (Applause.) I also thank you for the help you give us, and I would be the last to .under-rate your far-reaching influence. You know what that great poet of the North says. I am sure he did not know Lancashire lassies, or he would ha've paid a very different tribute to the gudewife's influence. I have a boundless and enthusiastic admiration for the poetry ot Bobbie Burns, but I cannot agree with what be says about the husband's indifference to what his better-half tells him. (Hear, hear.) These are his four lines about it:

" Ah, Gentle Dames, it gars me greet To think how many counsels sweet, How many lengthened sage advices The husband frae the wife despises." —(Laughter.)

I am perfectly sure that your husbands don't despise your advice, and I know perfectly well thevalue of the generous fund of advice I get. (Much laughter.) t I ask you to continue to use your influence, to help Mrs. Osborne and mem- bers of the Ladies' Executive to swell the num- bers of our Warrington Women's Liberal Asso- ciation, so that we may all co-operate together in working for a magnificent victory at the next election. tHear, hear, and loud applause.)

ACRES FOR DEER; INCHES FOR HOUSES.

Councillor Shaw and the Land Campaign.

Councillor SHAW gave an interesting review of the political history of the country from the time "when working men had no votes" to the present. He also referred to the many, dodges on the part of the Conservative party to prevent legislation for the benefit of the working classes coming into operation.

Mrs: Crosfield had ,spoken honestly, pointedly and lucidly on the , land question, in which direction the Liberal party were turning their energies at the present time. When a man wanted to build a house or cottage he had to pay. a very exorbitant* price for a. bit of land, and that was why they had such small houses for the working classes to live in. As Lloyd George had put it, "when they. want it for pheasants or deer they take it by acres, but when it is wanted for houses you get it by inches." But the workers had always to bear the burden. They had to pay for the extra price of the land in the rent. (Hear, hear.) The Liberal party were going to alter that and make the lives of the working people happier and brighter. ~(Applause.) Speaking in regard to his attendances at Council meetings, he said that he had attended 101 times, whilst his two, colleagues had both together attended 102 times. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19131101_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. CROSFIELD AS NEXT M.P.

Mr. Huoims proposed a comprehensive vote of thanks. He thought the Women's Liberal Asso- ciation were very fortunate in having Mrs. Crosfield as their President. (Hear, hear.) Al- though not a titled lady, they all knew who was the better to follow. They all knew Mr. Cros- field very well, and the way he was shaping he was doing remarkably well. They should not have much difficulty in getting him to the House of Commons next time. (Applause.) Mrs. Osborne was a very hard worker for the cause, and she devoted a large amount of time to the association. (Hear, hear.) Mrs. Roberts was an excellent ward captain, and her work for the party was highly appreciated. (Hear, hear.) The artistes had provided a most enjoy- able evening's entertainment. (Applause.). Councillor SHAW said that Mr. Hughes was an excellent ward secretary for St. John's, and he did everything he could so that victory could be gained. (Applause.)

- During the evening Miss Wilkinson, win) was so successful in "Miss Hook of Holland," de- lighted the audiences with her several songs, as also did Mrs. Sanders, Miss Rigby and Miss Jones. Miss Shaw and Miss Clark were able pianoforte duettists, and the humorous songs by Mr. Bibby caused many laughs.

THE EXAMINER,

SATLIIDAY. NOVENIBEII 1. 1913

MR; BONAR LAW ABANDONS THE PENDULUM.

Liberals have said some hard things about the position in which the Opposition have placed themselves by their retrograde tactics and re-actionary strategy, but they have never said anything quite so distressing as has their lecider, Mr. BONAR LAW. Liberals said the Conservatives were without leaders when Mr. BALroua and Mr. CHAMBERLAIN were deserted by all the men of weight in the party, who refused to swallow the Tariff Reform pill; we said the party were bankrupt in polic); when they had to seek the miSerable shelter of a proposal to put a tax upon the food of the people. But the riddled Opposition came up with praise- worthy persistence, and with 'a 'smiling • coun- tenance, pretending that patience would mend all discomfitures, and time would bring about that swing of the political pendulum that of itself would put the Liberals out and the Con- servatives in. It is not long since Mr. BONAR LAW sub-split his party by assigning food taxes for a roving commission to the Colonies:- This week he abandoned the pendulum at Newcastle. Ile recalled LORD SALISBURY'S reference to the swing of the pendulum, and said his friends had an idea, founded on that saying, "that sooner or later, unless we lose our heads alto- gether, our party will come into power." He refused to be thus comforted. "I am pound," he,said, "to say I see no reason why that result should happen." He appears to blame Mr. LLOYD GEORGE, and the fact that the people have the power for his pessimistic frame of mind. With terrible candour he asked his audience to remember that after the passing of the Reform Bill, the Conservatives were out of power for forty years, because the 'Liberals managed to convince the enfranchised middle classes that they were their friends; while the enfranchise- ment of the working classes since 1885 had placed the power in the hands of the people, who were being convinced by the demagogue, LLOYD GEORGE, that the Liberals lare their friends also. Mr. LAW quoted Mr. SNOWDEN, who said the Labour party had been dished by Lloyd Georgeism. "It may be," he 'sighed, "that that kind of thing will succeed indefin- itely, and if it does we cannot 'help it." It is a hopeless kind of, dawn for w leader to reveal to his party. Meanwhile progress appears to have been made with the Ulster trouble. , The PRIME MINISTER has made it knoWn that the Govern- ment will not be intimidated, and Sir EDWARD GREY haS declared that "if violence is to be used Ao resist Home Rule in order that the existing state of things may be maintained, then you must meet violence by violence." But

===== 0002601_19131101_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 1, 1913.

Mr.. Asourrit has offered to consider proposals for a friendly settlement; subject to the condi- tions that nothing must be done that will inter- fere with the fundamental principle of the Bill, that is, the setting up in Dublin of a subordi- nate Irish legislature, with an executive responsible to it; nothing must be done which will •erect a permanent or an insuperable barrier in the way of Irish unity; while the importance ,of the extension of the principle of devolution to 'other parts of the United King- dom is fully recognised, the claim of Ireland is prior in point of urgency and must be dealt with first. Mr. BONAR LAW, in answer to Mr. ASQUITH'S "free, frank and without prejudice" offer, has replied: "We shall not decline to respond to the invitation, and we shall carefully consider any proposals he.may make to us, and consider them with a real desire to find a solu- tion, if a solution be possible." That is an advance the importance of which cannot be over-rated. It foreshadows a step in real Stdtesmanship which may conceivably do more to rehabilitate Mr. LAW and his party than any- thing that has happened these ten years.

PASSING WEEK,

SMOKE.

Our columns are heavily charged with smoke this week. We have replies from Drs. Bennett and Manson to the remarks the Mayor tDr. Joseph) made at last week's Council meeting. We have also letters from Alderman IB'ehnerneett, promisesdefending to be hisa hard position, in front struggle and- others. of those who wish to improve the state Of the atmosphere in this neighbourhood. Prejudice and disinclination to see the case as the smoke abatement advocates wish to put it, appear to be the chief stumbling block. That trade should be interfered with in the slightest degree is furthest from their thoughts. All that is asked is that unnecessary smoke shall be abol- ished, and it can be so. abolished with benefit not only to the community, but to the manu- facturers themselves. As Alderman Bennett says, if if is, a question between trade .and smoke, and no other alternative, we will select the smoke. But the question we are asking shall be settled is the degree of smoke that has to be tolerated. •

===== 0002601_19131101_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
SEWAGE OF THE TOWN.

In a ial article on this page we draw attention to the state of the river and the need for a proper sewerage system for the town. The question of. sewering Warrington has long /cm a question that has made town council- lors lift their eyes and knit their brows—only to pass it on to the convenience of the future. As the article says, the waitihg lias. not been in in improvements of past years.. In any case, the writer has given the town the nucleus of a discussion that will be the more now 1: f rwe shall be able to take advantage o the . profitable the more it is pursued.

THE CROSFIELD OPERATIC SOCIETY. We have the interesting intimation that the Croslleld Opeiratic Society are in the midst of preparations with .a vie,w to following •up with effect their great successes of past years. • "The or the approval of Warrington ti cb°relidscGlsitbi at lanridtthe"e db; Planquette, is the work that will if.oyal Court Theatre, on February 23rd. On the principle, we suppose, that it is advisable to leave well alone,. the same people will be entrusted with running the business. l'hat is to.say, Dr. Law will produco the opera, Mr. Nfosse \yin be the musical director, and -Mr. B. S. Townroe will act as hon. treasurer. All the old favourites will ,be available for the cast, and we are promised some • new-comers, who will rapidly make themselves into .favourites. The first "voice trial"- took place on Monday this week, when there was .the strongest turn- out in the history of- the.society.

A CaNTRAST IN POLICIES. Mr. Arthur Crosfield, fresh from his bril- liant oratorical effort in the Parr Hall last week, spent Saturday afternoon in the inspiring sur- roundings of the half-yearly meeting of the Lancashire Federation of the National Union of Liberal Clubs at Widnes. Having sung the praises Of clubs in general, and of Liberal Clubs in particular, as they give facilities for a class not of one mental cut to meet and benefit each other by revealing their respective individualities, he passed on,' by a natural transition, to speak of home life and to claim that it had been the chief object of LiberaliSni to defend that home life. "We have," he de- clared finely, "protected the home life against Protection "—so effectually that Liberals and not Tariff Reformers are celebrating with jubi- lation the tenth anniversary of that ill-conceived conspiracy against the best interests of the country. We have established old-age pensions and . passed the Insurance Act and brought about such legislation in the interest of children that had nothing else been done, the Liberal Government would have More than justified Cro\sVfleeldhsaavye a present policy, s, "fires the imagina- of our whole Nti NtiOslinic,elaxinisadtseniN•ocirue:ses the enthusiasm people." "Look Upon this picture and On -that," Mr. C:rosfleld in' effect aslTd his audience. Against this livp Liberalism, this progressive policy, -with its actual adhievements and brighter pros- pects, he placed in contrast latter-day Toryism bankrupt in policy, divided in counsel, dis- credited in tactics, linked to law-breakers " the once great party that stood for law and order, "sunk so as to be a spectacle for gods and men." That is a comprehensive sentence, showing the speaker a his best,when he is in the humour for hitting out. Perhaps the greatest success that has attended the work of this Ad- ministration has been connected with the per- fecton of our defences. The Conservatives used to claim that they alone were to be trusted with looking to the safety of the country on land and sea, just as they set themselves - up as the champions of law and order. They lost their reputation in one respect when they went to war with a small South African Republic, so ill- prepared that our arms suffere4 some of the most disgraceful reverses that figure in our his- tory; they have lost their character in the other direction by inciting a minority in Ireland to armed rebellion if they cannot inflict their wishes upon the majority. Both the Army and the Navy have been placed upon such an effec- tive footing as has never before been known. We do not intend to go to such lengths as to goad the Continent Into further increases of armaments. Liberalism, as Mr. Crosfield says, has other objects in view. , The plan of social reform that is only just opening out before us— though the Orst fruits have been considerable— has to ,be pursued to the utmost to the end that he "good time coming," to which the workers of this country are justly entitled, may be duly expedited.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
FREE CURE FOR DEAFNESS.

SPIRIT OF EDUCATION.

Demand for Knowledge that will Enable Men to Think for Themselves.

WORKERS AND UNIVERSITIES.

The Workers' Ed'national Association Fight for the " Light "

"Let us educate ourselves, and we will show you what power is in us !" That is the cry of men to-day. "Give us knowledge! We have the spirit to use it. We have the will to move, but We cannot see the way- We followed your lead, but you betrayed us. We set up our own leaders, but We dare not trust them. We are lost. We have no faith in others' eyes. We will get sight of our own. We will study the lie of this perplexing land ourselves, and then go where we will," _

- This spirit is behind all modern social move- ments. The Fabians try to equip men as logical defenders of socialism. The Independ- ent Labour party knows that it must educate its rank and file. All the polittcal and social sects, convinced of the security of their own beliefs, are eager to proselytise through educa- tion. The Adult Schools are finding that edu- cation, wide and unbiased, is necessary if Chris- tianity is to be effective. On the other hand, so many quack remedies have been authorita- tively pressed upon the plain man that after much angry bewilderment he is learning that he must take nothing on trust. At last he is roughly pushing aside the false prophets, and crying, "For God's sake, let me get to the bot- tom of all this Myself !"

Basis of a Real Democracy.

To help to satisfy this demand is the object of the Workers' Educational Association. Ten years ago two deep convictions in two widely diffex.ent social classes found their expression in one work. The Universities of England knew that they were out of touch with the hidden springs of national life. Something had to be done to bridge the gulf between them and the mass of the people. They felt not only that they could offer definite knowledge to the perplexed, but that they must get inspired by the earnest- ness of working people. On the other hand, the workers had already been clamouring at the locked doors of the Universities. Thus from the, mutual attraction of two very different bcidies came the conception of this great move- ment. Its 'aim is to offer University education to all MPH. and women. It hopes to make it possible for every one to obtain clear and accu- rate knowledge on any subject of real import- ance. It would spread the love of knowledge for its own sake. It desires to do More than merely satisfy the ,propagandist's greed for useful arguments. Its ideal member is not the man who wants to prove a theory, but the man who wants to learn facts, to study theories, and to judge for himself. It sets itself not to teach doctrines nor to make converts, but to enable men to think. It points out no one way to sal- vation. It throws a light where all ways divide. Its sole dogma is that an educated people is the only basis of a real democracy.

The Duty of Education. . Mere knowledge is to education as food is to the mind. Without it education is impossible; but far more than knowledge is necessary for the attainment of,true education. The Workers' Educational Association is trying to spread that indefinable spirit that helps a man to judge calmly between different ideals even while it fires him to give his soul to one cause. It is a spirit that can fortify him in the personal battle of life and keenly recreate him after his labour. It can delight him when he is alone, and en- rich for him the society Of others. It is the air in which true friendship breathes. Education is a duty, pot a right. Every man owes it to himselfi, to the State, and to that which he may call the material universe, or the Universal Spirit, or God. He owes it to him- self to live to the, full, and in spite of adversity to prove himself it man, with a man's high powers and ideals. He owes it to the State to make himself an efficient citizen, not a mere sheep driven hither and thither by the dogs of officialdom. He owes it to the universe, which; if terrible, is also beautiful, to try to understand it and even to improve it. He owes it to G,od, as a duty of worship, to see what is given .him to see of His great works. Workinu-class Intellect.

There are people who think of education as a means towards social reform. But it must not be forgotten that social reform is not an end in itself. A perfect State is not necessarily :a happy nor a high-souled nation. The Associa- tion offers education as the foundation'of a vqse social reconstruction; but it also insists that education is an end in itself. There are many members of the Universities who feel that they have greatly profited by intellectual friendship with working peOple. They have caught sight of new ideas. They have got into touch with reality. They have learnt enthusiasm. For this they owe a real debt of gratitude to all working men and women. Therefore they are most eager to arouse that hunger for light to which alone splendour is revealed. They are well aware that it was by good fortune that they themselves were given education. They desire to pass on this most rich gift to those who at present cannot even conceive what it really means.

_ Phe Workers' Educational Association repre- sents both the effort of these persons to repay their debt ongratitude, and a determination on the part of working men and women to fight their way into the light. In subsequent issues of this paper an account will be given of the various methods by which the Association is trying to attain it ideal.

THE LORD OF HOSTS IS WITH US. A Paraphrase of Psalm xlvi.

God is our refuge and our strength, Our hope in tribulation ; his mighty aid is ever nigh, His tender consolation ; We wilt not fear though earth he moved, Iler strong foundations shaken ; The Lord of Hosts is with us still, His flock is not forsaken.

Though deep may call to mighty deep, And billows break in thunder ; Though mountains in the depths be hurled, And rocks be rent asunder; Be still, my soul, trust thou in God, Thy King for ever reigned'! The Lord of Hosts is our defence, And Zion still remained'.

A gentle stream of peace and love Through Zion ever floweth; God dwelleth in the holy place, And gifts of grace bestometh: The hosts of God keep watch and ward, His kingdom faileth never; The Lord of Hosts is with us still, And guards His own for ever.

0 Zion, City of Our ( od, On high securely founded ! No foe can shake Thy firm abode, By walls of strength surrounded; Thy King commandeth war to cease, His voice the storm assuages; The Lord of Hosts will give us peace, His throne the Rock of Ages. ALFRED CORNEY. The above fine verses are the last hymn written by Mr. Corney, who has just resigned the headmastership of Wycliffe Day School.

SMOKE.

Alderman Bennett's Reply to Mr. J. J.

Bleckly.

A RIGHT AND DUTY.

Why the Alderman Ceased to reside

in Town.

To. THE EDITOR OF THF, EXAMINER."

read Mr. John J. Bleckly's letter in last Saturday's "Guardian" with interest and some surprise. It struck me as a little spiteful, and it entirely misrepresents my attitude in reference to this important question. Those who have done me the honour to follow my long crusade against what I am still "pleased to call the smoke nuisance" will be aware that, again and again, I have Made it perfectly clear that all I have ever asked or urged is "' that the responsible committee should apply a gentle, but persistent, pressure to the offenders," in order to induce them to reduce their smoke as far as possible consistently with the necessities of their respective trades; and, in the very last speech I made upon the subject, I deliberately stated that, "if the alternatives were no trade or no smoke, I should say, 'Let us have the smoke and the trade,'" expressly adding, "My point is that we can greatly minimise the smoke and still retain the trade. Mr. Meekly admits as much, for he states distinctly that, in the case of the Electricity Works, "to pro- duce electric current without Making smoke from the boilers" is "surely the very simplest proposition in the whole range of engineering science"; and that, "if the chimneys there do smoke . . . . someotie ought to be fined."

Ile will probably admit that what is true of the Electricity Works is true of every other power plant in the town. Then surely "someone ought to be fined," and it is the duty of the Health Committee to bring the offenders to book. He even goes so far as to say that "to some small extent, in subsidiary parts of the works "—at Dallam and Bewsey—" the smoke might possibly be reduced " ; but excuses him- self by adding that the site is "overcrowded," and that "every possible precaution has been taken consistent with the circumstances." I am very glad to hear it, arid ,am extremely sorry that the results have been so disappointing. I will not venture to dispute what he says about the diffictilty of dealing with black smoke "in some of the stages of iron-making." This is not any " news " to me, and is, •of course, a matter of common knoVedge, the difficulty being very properly recognised and provided for by the law. Nobody desires to interfere with any firm because it is not able to achieve impossibilities, and, if science has really said its last word upon the subject, I suppose we shall have to put up with the nuisance as best we may. But that surely is not any reaSon why we should not seek to limit it to the smallest practicable proportions, or why we should extend the statutory exemptions to any process to which they do not apply. Mr. meekly says• that I have "certainly no right to act as a spy upon my neighbours, with the object of bringing them into odium and disrepute." It is not a very pleasant way of putting it, and, as a matter of fact, I had not anything whatever to do with the observations lately made Of some Of our local chimneys, and was not aware that they were in progress 'until I saw the resufts, in "The Examiner." They were made, I understand, by a qualified Inspector of the Smoke Abatement League, who is a gentleman of long experience and intimate knowledge of the standards of smoke measure- ment in Manchester' and the surrounding dis- trict; and, if the results of the observations can be substantiated, it seems to me that "those who Prompted them" are entitled to our thanks and not to our recriminations. As a repre- sentative of the public and a citizen of the town, I have every right, and, moreover, regard it as my absolute duty, to see, as.far as I am able, that the law is not evaded, and that the inhabitants of this :densely-crowded area are properly protected by the responsible autlpri- lies against every.nuisance which it is possible to prevent. It is a little unkind of Mr. Bleckly to sneer at the supporters of the Beautiful Warrington Society, whose only object is to improve the amenities of the place in which they live, and to make life a little More agreeable for our great industrial population. M. Bleckly has the good sense to live at Altrincham, beyond the reach of the smoke which hangs, like a black cloud, above the town. I stood it for ten years, and found it so objectionable that I, too, went to live Outside. Happily, he is able to take care of himself. And so am I. It is not for any selfish reason that I have identified myself with this Movement, from which I have nothing whatever to gain, and which inevitably leads me into controversies which I should much prefer to avoid. It is for the seventy thousand of my fellow-citizens who are doomed to live in the shadow of the great chimneys, and who cannot escape into the fresh •air of the country, that I speak; and, in spite of Mr. Bleckly's good-natured ,suggestion that the Council should refuse to give me a hearing, and his obvious desire to prejudice me in the eyes of my fellow-townsmen, so long as England remains a free country, I shall continue to tell them what I honestly believe to be the truth. Faithfully yours,

ARTHUR BENNETT. Paddington House, near Warrington,

25th October, 1913

"HEAVEN HELP WARRINGTON t"

TO THE EDITOR OF "THE WARRINGTON EXAMINER."

Sir,—! have read in the " Guardian " at letter of Mr. John Bleckly's, which I think is very unjust to Alderman Bennett. All the Alder- man, or anyone else, has claimed on behalf of the public interest is that unnecessary smoke should be done away with.

This being so, I do not think it is a very worthY action on the part of Mr. Bleckly to use his power to scare Warrington, and to try to frighten the Alderman in his efforts to im- prove the conditions of our town life. This Mr. Bleckly strives to do by representing that the advantage of remaining in Warrington, where much of the capital of Pearson and Knowles is anchored, and removing to Parting- ton, are so finely poised, so exquisitively bal- anced, that it only needs a few local " pin- pricks " to throw the balance in favour of removal. Let Warrington rest assured that if removal be considered by the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., it will be de- termined by important financial considerations, and in the sole interests of the company. No other consideration will have any effective in- fluence.

If any effort.of social betterment, any enforce- ment Of the Health Acts (which in the slightest degiTe touches, or is thought to touch, the in- terest; or the dignity of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles) is to be met anti/frustrated by threats of removal, then the helpless condition of War- rington, if dependent upon a big moneyed in- terest, is deplorable. Heaven help Warrington! STAGGERED.
