Can We Restore Our Long Lost Walks, Our Fishing, Our Bathing, and Our Boating?

DRAINAGE OF THE TOWN.

Necessary Work that would Mean the Expenditurs

===== 0002601_19131101_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
of Quarter of a Million.

In this article we show what Warrington has lost by the pollution of its river with crude sewage, assisted by authorities " up stream." We also show how badly Warrington is drained by its ill-conceived scheme to get rid of its filth in the readiest manner that presented itself to our predecessors. The cost of rectifying that mistake is an alarm- ing prospect, but it has to be stated, when we are considering the question, for sewerage schemes are decidedly among those things of Which it may be said you cannot make any progress without the "sinews of war."

Last week, when the Mayor (Dr. Joseph) was Practically taking leave of the members of the Town Council upon the completion of his third term of office, he referred, among other matters, to "the sewage scheme," which has been hinted at so Often, only to be abandoned as a question that is best " dropped " for the present. But the town is badly sew-ered, the town is growing Slowly but perceptibly, and the sewerage ques- tion will become more and more pressing. The Mayor said there was a great tendency to run down the pail syStem and to urge the adop- tion of the water carriage system. He approved of that plan, but he hoped that before anything was done, they would carefully consider the wh* question from Its foundations. Before deciding to adopt the water carriage system in its entirety, they should• consider the practic- ability of treating and purifying the results of that system before the refuse was turned into the river.

There. are, :indeed, seribus considerations, as we shall Show, one of Which is that the prob- ablo cost of -moving to the town would be £250,000.

What Warrinuton Has Lost.

Not nearly all tile crude sewage of the town is carted away in those unlovely pails. • The sewers take a considerable proportion down to the river at various points. Formerly other authorities perpetrated similar abominations-- representing the " wisdom " of civilisation in its attempt to get rid of As excrescences at the least possible expense. Authorities up stream are being forced to adopt other methods, but Warrington and other places between us and the sea have '" enjoyed" exemptions that keep the river. hereabouts polluted. The result has been that the river— which usually lends an in- describable charm to the country through which it flows—has long been a thing from which it is well to keep away. Fishing, which was once plentiful, has long been banished; river bathing has long since gone; boating is followed by only a few of the bravest; and the walks along the riverside, that were a fine attraction two or three generations ago, dre neglected and largely forgotten. It is no so very long since the river was The pride of Warringtonians. The late Major Pick- mere saw salmon Caught near. Latchford Weir. With every tide came huge quantities of fish as far as Warrington, followed by the fishing boats from Liverpool: Tradition says that one man, who amassed a fortune by catch- ing sparling, built some houses with the pro- ceeds of his vocation, and so brought into ex- istence what we know as Sparling-street. It was no uncommon thing to see the banks of the river covered with the nets of fishermen. Valuable Fishing Rights.

Strange as it may appear, when the Corpora- tion bought the Old Warps estate to make Victoria Park to commemorate Queen Victoria's Diamond Jubilee, they also bought the fishing rights—the right to land boats, dry nets, and all the rest of it, giving one an idea of the sub- stantial, character of the river at one time as a fishery.

The island now partakes the nature of a pen- insula, but in those olden times the water ran in the direction of Latchford, instead of War- rington, and turned Back by Lower Wash- lane, making for the point near the present en-, trance to Victoria Park, where the rush of waters caused a great scouring of the bed of the river, thus making a great hole which was known as the Hell Hole. In this deep the salmon' congregated in such' quantities as to make the fishing rights of the island worth hundreds a year. The Mersd'y was once a fine boating river, and bills preserved in the Museum show that in the 'Thirties there was a flourishing War- rington Rowing Club, and that prizes were competed for at its regattas. The Angler's Lament.

Pe'epic are very light-hearted in the matter of pollution. The working men .of Warring- ton, driven away from the river so many years ago, have just sustained a loss which could not be made good by. the expenditure of £5OO. And if made good, there would be no guarantee that the loss would not immediately recur. More- over, the loss is not to be measured by the money standard alone; a means of healthy recreation is menaced.

. The Warrington Anglers' Association have, for many years, rented, among other waters, a length of the old Runcorn Canal, and the sec- tion between Stag Bridge and Moore was so badly polluted during the recent drought that fish were destroyed in thousands. Carp many pounds in weight, roach, perch, and other kinds of fresh water fish that delight the heart of the angler; were found floating dead and dying on the surface of the canal. It was from he small savings of working men that the canal was stocked, and it will be from the same source that the funds will come if it is re-stocked; and it is sad to reflect that the same result may follow again from the same cause.

Our Right to Pollute. The Rivers Pollution ACE, 1876, was passed tc put a stop to the pollution of rivers and streams. One section provides : Every person who puts or causes to be put or to fall or knowingly permits to be put or to fall or to be carried into any stream so as, either singly or in combination with other similar acts of the same or any other person, to interfere with its due flow, or to pollute its waters, the solid refuse of any manufactory, manufacturing process or quarry, or any rub- bish or cinders or any other waste or any putrid solid matter, shall be deemed to have com- mitted an offence.

The succeeding section provides that every person •

Who causes to fall or flow or knowingly per- mits to tall or flow or to be carried into any stream any solid or liquid sewage matter shall be guilty of an offence. A further provision with regard to manufac- turing and mining pollutions provides , that every person who causes to fall or flow or knowingly permits to fall or flow or to be carried into any stream any poisonous, noxious, or polluting liquid proceeding from a manu- factory or manufacturing process, shall also be deemed to have committed an offence.

With regard to each section there are quali-

lore High Tides. This week Warringtou has again been visited by exceptionally high tides, and work at the Bridge was hindered by the flood of water.

Local Diplomat's Illness.

Lord Acton has been obliged to postpone his departure for Germany for a week, on account of an attack of rheumatism.

Mayor's Quaint Election.

With quaint ceremony, which has been per- formed since 1290, Mr. G. F. Armitage, of Messrs. Armitage and Rigby, Warrington and Manches-. ter, was elected Mayor of Altrincham on Wed- nesday. He is the 623rd occupant of the office.

Teachers and pupils in Warrington have been ,on holiday this week in connection with the •auid-term respite. Some of the vacant schools were utilised for the usual crop of sales of work.

Birth and Death Rates.

The births in Warrington last week numbered 33, giving a rate of 23.2, compared with 26.2 for the corresponding week last year. Deaths num- bered 20, giving a death-rate of 14.0, compared with 7.9.

With a view to continue the efforts in asso- ciation with the extension of the Mission pre- mises on account of the general progress of the various institutions, Mrs. J. Hassall opened a sale of work on Thursday afternoon in the Achoolroom. A total of .06 was realised.

C.L.B. Sergeant-Major. Sergeant-Major H. H. Bottomley, C.L.8., has sent in his resignation, through pressure of business. He has been identified with the 2nd Liverpool Battalion, C.L.8., for a number of years, and has for the last two or three occu- pied the post which he now resigns.

A 'Military Funeral.

The remains of the late Mr. Abel Henry :gather, of 7, Paradise-place, Warrington, for many years a member of the local Volunteers, were laid to rest on Sunday afternoon with military honours. Deceased,, who had reached his 61st year, and who died suddenly the pre- ceding Wednesday whilst at work, joined the old Voluntcers when 19 years of age.

Mr. B. S. Townroe.

Mr. B. S. Townroe has removed from 10, Win- marleigh-street, to Mere House, Newton-le- Willows. Mr. Townroe, our readers will be exceedingly pleased to know, intends to keep up his many connections with the social life of the town. He is merely changing his place of residence.

St. Mary's Tea and Dance.

About 400 persons attended the annual tea party and dance in connection with St. Mary's Roman Catholic Church, in the Parr Hall, on Monday evening. To delightful music, supplied by the League of the Cross Band, dancing was indulged in until early on Tuesday morning. Messrs. A. Kilburn (secretary), H. Houghton, J. Eckersley and F. J. Coakley acted as M.C.'s. The whist prize-winners were Messrs. G. 'Vernon and W. Houghton. The proceeds will be devoted to the school funds.

"Bandsmen's Appreciation " : A Correction.

Under the above heading last week, we recorded the fact that the bandsmen of the Territorials had presented Mrs. Elgood, wife of the retiring adjutant, with a framed photo- graph of the band. We regret to say that we were in one respect wrongly informed. Colonel Fairclough did not make the presentation, as stated, nor was he present. At a supper given by the bandsmen to say "Goodbye " to the popular adjutant, the Bandmaster, on behalf of the band, gave Mrs. Elgood the photograph mentioned..

Baker's Signinv-on Money:

- At the Lancashire County Quarter Sessions, held at Liverpool on Tuesday; Annie Baker (20), servant, 11, Beswick-street, Warrington, ,pleaded guilty to stealing SA on October 6th, the money of Joseph Baker, which, it is understood, he received when signing on for Warrington. James Harris (22), Beswick-street, pleaded guilty to receiving the money. It appeared that the girl lived with her brother and went upstairs and got the money from a box. When arrested 'in Bew/Sey they had only ninepence between them.—Baker was bound over, and Harris was sent to prison for six months, with hard labour.

Recently the front room patrons of the Ceme- tery Hotel were provided by the host and hostess with an excellent hot-pot supper. Coal Cures Atheist.

. The Bishop of Liverpool, who addressed a united service in connection with the Warrington Men's Bible Classes in St. Peter's Church on Sunday afternoon, related a story on the cure of an atheist. He said that some years ago, in a colliery Apt far from Liverpool, there worked a man who professed to be an infidel. He was working one day with his mates, when a great piece of 'coal fell from the roof within a few yards of him, and the fear was that the whole roof would • fall in and crush the men. In a moment that man was on his knees, crying to God to have mercy upon him. One of his fellow-workmen dryly remarked that there Was nothing like a good cob of coal for driving infidel notions out of a man's head.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
Lady's Criticism of the Military Bogey.

The campaign for compulsory military ser- vice, introduced by Lord Roberts and the National Service League, was criticised at a meeting of the Society of Friends, held in their Meeting House, Butter.market-street, Warring- ton, on Sunday evening, by Miss Frances Thompson, of Birkenhead. The National. Ser- vice League, she said, told. the public what they aimed at was not conscription, but compulsory enrolment of men for military purposes. That enrolment was really- conscription. It did not necessarily mean that conscription would be carried out on the same lines as in other coun- tries, but if there was compulsory enrolment for military purposes it meant conscription.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
" The Examiner" Office and the Infirmary.

Al r. J. H. J. }lampoon, honorary secretary of the Working Men's Infirmary Committee, attended at "The Examiner" Office on Wednes- day afternoon and addressed the whole of the workpeople,with a view to the establishment of a systematic works collection in aid of the funds of the committee. He explained that his com- mittee contributed 021,248 to the funds of the Infirmary last year, but increased expenditure necessary to keep pace with the demands made upon the resources of the institution by a busy and constantly growing town rendered further assistance absolutely necessary. It was 'ex- plained that the works " chapel " had already begun a systematic collection; but, in order to bring the whole of the employes into one scheme it was formally and unanimously agreed that all under eighteen years of age would subscribe a halfpenny a week and all over eighteen a penny a week to the Infirmary.

Johannes Wolff Farewell Concert.

On Thursday evening the farewell concert of Mr. J. Wolff was held in the Parr Hall, but the audience was small. Mr. Johannes Wolff opened the programme with the Ist movement from Grieg's Sonata in G major. The simple expression and quaint melody was Nell ren- dered. The bowing was delicately executed, the notes clear and sustained. With the "Rapsodiu Hongroise," Mr. Wolff seemed more familiar and put more tone and depth of feeling into the piece.- Mendelssohn'A " Andante " was pro- duced with finished technique, but did not in- spire the same enthusiasm in the audience as did Wieniawsky's "Krakowiak." The encore to these pieces was "Romance dans Re," by Wolff. The violinist displayed the whole of his talents in the rendering of this exquisite little piece. Mr. Wilde is a gifted tenor, possessing a remarkably sweet voice, which rings with a clear intonation. He uses it with power, he can impart fire or tenderness, and his enunciation touches perfection. Miss Alys Bateman is a gifted soprano, with a remarkable range. Her high notes are particularly sweet. Mr. Baker's "Song of the Flea," by Moussorgsky, was warmly received. He sang, it with a clear, fine voice, but the setting seemed far too sad for the satirical verse. The contralto, Miss D. Bottom- ley, sang her numbers well, and her, low notes were particularly sweet. The pianist was Mr. Parlovitz, who accompanied with fine feeling and remarkable. expression.

Polling' Takes Place To-day. To-day three wards—Whitecross, St. Aus- tin's, and Latchford—are asked to poll for the election of councillors. There has been no burning questions before the electors; still a considerable amount of interest has been taken. It is expected that the results will be proclaimed at the Town Hall about nine o'clock.

Prize Competition. As will be seen by an advertisement in an- other column, a prize competition on a rather large scale is being conducted by the proprietors of Dr. Sands' Liver Seeds. There u 3 no en- trance fee, and the competition is one of skill— the writing of catch phrases or testimonials to advertise these well-known pills. The condi- tions should be carefully read.

Liverpool Broker's Handsome Legacy: _£2,000 for League of Welldoers.

The League of Werldoers, Liverpool, has re- ceived word from the solicitors (Messrs. Duncan, Oakshott and Co.) of the late Mr. Rodie Macfee that .22,000, free of duty, has been bequeathed for the propagation of its work, in connection with which he entirely and anonymously financed a free shelter on the Dock-road for worthy, homeless men, besides giving £1 a week to the League's general funds. Seldom has a sagacious and successful business man been. more generous to charity with an equal amount of self-effacement as Mr. Macfee. It may be relied that the guiding spirits of the League will principally apply this very generous legacy to the interests of worthy, homeless men at its Strand-street (Dock-road) shelter, where Mr. Macfee's chief and open-handed 'practical inter- est lay.

DEATH OF MR. R. WYLLIE.

Sudden End to a Varied and Useful Career.

A BRAVE AND TRUSTED COMRADE.

The unexpected and sudden passing away of Mr. Robegt Wyllie has brought a sense of deep personal loss to his many friends. The end came on Saturday morning at the Central Station, Warrington. There had been no illness or warning. Mr. Wyllie left home, cheerful and in ordinary health, but while waiting for the 10.50 train died from heart failure. Help was obtained, but when Dr. Fox. arrived life was extinct.

The career which thus came to its close was a varied and ,useful one. Mr. Wyllie was born in September, 1838, in Kirkcaldy, a seaside town upon the east coast of Scotland. In a tiny school house upon the beach, often wave beaten and sometimes flooded by sea water, he sat for five years at the feet of a typical Scotch domine, receiving, along with the sound and simple teaching, an example which he held in honour all through his long life. His home was one of those from which "old Scotia's grandeur springs," where deep religious inter- est and a thirst for knowledge are communi- cated to children as by a holy contagion. The picture of a community of humble weavers gathering week by week to read and discuss a solitary copy of the "Scotsman" that reached

===== 0002601_19131101_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
LATE MR. WYLLI

- - • ' • , \ \ „ ‘ttx • \ • • • TILE -

===== 0002601_19131101_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
WORK IN WARRINGTON.

As engineering manager he displayed great ability, and won the respect of his colleagues in a remarkable degree. The task was con- genial, and he spent here the remainder of his working life. After twenty-two and a half years he ceased active service in February, 1907, and has spent the last six and a half years in. well-merited retirement. •

His life in Warrington has been spent in un- obtrlisive, yet marked association with re- ligious and philanthropic work. His early life and training belonged to the Presbyterian Church, and he never lost his love and admira- tion for its organisation and its ideals, but the whole of his religious life in Warrington has been spent in association with Wycliffe Church. of which he has been a devoted member and officer. As a deacon he gave to the Church his best thought and counsel, and its affairs were always the subject of his deepest concern. When diminished strength made it difficult for him to continue to serve, it was a source of great- happiness to him that he was retained upon the Church's list of officers as an honorary deacon. His deep interest was awakened some

years ago by the work he waS able to do, in connection with the Young People's Society of he Church. .Here his gifts of leadership and tact found a happy sphere •of operation, and his very best was given to the work. Of equal interest to him was the Young Men's Christian 'Association, founded about twenty years ago in Warrington. .The who laboured with him in this effort were made. aware in the most gratifying way of his fine constructive power and zeal for the welfare of young men. Under a quiet exterior he carried a strong and earnest purpose towards young men and a desire for their equipment for the tasks of life. Caution went with his zeal, but he had vision, and worked with a self-sacrifice that often im- pressed his co-workers and held them to their work. There are many in the far-off places of the world to whom the -news of. his death will tiring ktrong memories, and who will pay their tribute of gratitude for influence and encour- agement given in the days of the Y.M.C.A. at I.Warrington. His closing years were Marked by no diminu- tion of religious interest. The things which have guided his activities through life held their place to the end. Nor was there any slackening of intellectual power. His mental eye was not dim, nor his natural force .abated. He passed away in the midst of wide and joyous interests, the pleasures of friendship and read- ing unspoiled. Intimate friends will remember him for his fine character and by the solid interests which filled his thought and life. Heart and mind "accorded well," and the things that touched the heart governed mind and speech. He lived for the things that elevate, that make for seri- ousness, for reverence and peace, and his friends havb lost a brave and trusted comrade in the battle of life.. A.M.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
LOSS TO WYCLIFFE CHURCH.

The loss that Wycliffe Church had sustained by the death of Mr. Wyllie was mentioned on Sunday by the Rev. T. T. James at the morning and evening services. Mr. Wyllie said the rev. gentleman, had for very many years been a member of tlie church, and by loyal ancltfaithful service had proved his deep interest and con- cern for all that touched its progress and its work. As a member of the diaconate for a very long period, he had given of his best to all tasks which fell to him. His familiar figure would be missed by everyone, and his memory would be cherished and would remain as a truly help- ful influence. Having served freely and well, he had now been called to the higher and fuller service. ;" Thanks be to God, Who giveth us the victory through our Lord Jesus Christ."

RECENT LIBERAL TRIUMPHS AND TORY FAILURES.

Present-day Military and Naval Efficiency.

The value clubs prove to the Liberal move- ment was not the least interesting theme dis- cussed by Mr. A. H., Crosfield at the half-yearly. meeting of the Lancashire Federation of the National Union of Liberal Clubs, held at the Liberal Club, Widnes, on Saturday afternoon. The report stated that several important centres were still outside the Union, and the Federation would welcome assistance to bring them in. They looked with confidence to the Warrington District Federation to help with its influence in that connection. It was a sig- nificant fact that those parts of Lancashire which are federated are overwhelmingly Liberal in their Parliamentary representation, whilst those portions which remain unfederated are just as prominently Tory. On the proposition of Councillor Ernmr, Old- ham, seconded by Mr. PEARSON, neyWOOd, it was agreed to arrange for all the federated clubs a series of lectures on the Land Campaign. Mr. CARTER, of Frodsham, moved a resolution expressing approval of the determination of the Government to pass the Home Rule, Welsh Dis- establishment, and Plural Voting Bills,, and promising them unswerving support in their proposals for dealing with the injustices in the land laws.

He said in Frodsham and Helsby cases arose again and again of houses being closed by local authorities because of delapidation, the families being turned into the road and so finding their way into the towns. The Helsby Local Auth- ority wanted to build six houses to meet the need of the district, and they approached the agent of a well-known county marquis. They were told.that if the houses were to be let to men who lived in Runcorn or Widnes, to postmen, rail- way men, or the employes of the neighbouring cable works, they could not have the land. In this way the efforts of the Local Authority wAre hampered.

against ignorance, prejudice and unreasoning custom. (Hear, hear, and cheers.) So let us make good use of our opportunities, and, as far as we can, extend them to others. (Hear, hear.) It has been said that club life and home life are in conflict. I will not detain you by discussing that more or less domestic issue. (Laughter.) I do not, think there ought to be any trouble on that score so far as Liberals are concerned, because almost the essence of Liberalism is contained in these familiar words of the immortal poet of the North: To make a happy fireside clime For weaus and wife, That's the true pathos, and sublime

Of human life. ! (Cheers.) It is the home lite that we have as Liberals-, during the past tam years, made it our chief object to defend.. (Hear, hear.) We have protected the home rife against Protection (Applause.) Sir Jahn Simon spoke in Glasgow on October 6th, just ten years to a day from the time when the raging, tearing propaganda of Tariff Reform was started in that very city, and I ask you to reflect on the surprising fact that after all their whirlwind campaigns, after their artful dodges—(laughter)—their lavish and pro- fuse expenditure of money, after all the efforts to • play to the gallery, after every devious, de- vice has been tried for ten long years, the Tariff Reformers are running away from their own position. (Applause.) They have thrown over- board in the storm more than half their cargo, and that the very part of the cargo that the captain of the ship told them was Most valu- able and essential for the safety of their voyage. (Laughter and applause.) And so we. have this remarkable fact that it is not the Tariff Re- formers, but the -Liberals who are celebrating the anniversary. (Cheers.)

A Great Record And a Great Contrast. The Liberal triumph is not limited to our victory for Free Trade. Liberals have done in those ten years a lot of constructive work. They have established old-age pensions, and passed the .Insurance Act and other great measures of social reform of the first importance besides; they have rendered noble service to the State both at home and in our Colonies—(hear, hear) —and, gentlemen, are we weary in well-doing? (" No, no.") Is there a sign of it? (" No.") You give me the answer already. Our party indeed is not flagging in either its work or its energy, and we are about to enter on the mighti- est campaign of all—upon a policy of land re- form—(cheers)—which fires the imagination and rouses the enthusiasm of our whole people. (Loud applause.)

And Toryism? What can one think or say of latter-day Toryism? (Laughter.) Nothing, I am bound to say, that is flattering, because, bankrupt in policy, divided in counsel, discred- ited in tactics, linked to law breakers, the great party which once upon a time at least stood for law and order, has now sunk so low that it is a spectacle for gods and men. (Loud ap- plause.)

===== 0002601_19131101_art0169.txt | included=true | included =====
ONCE THE PEOPLE'S LAND.

In Frodsham there were four or five acres of common land which had been enclosed under the Enclosure Acts of 1789. For the privilege of such enclosure the lord of the manor forfeited the market toll of the township, and as a market was only held in Frodsham once a year, it was undoubtedly a very good deal. (Laughter.) He hoped that when the Land Bill was formulated due consideration would be given to the wonder- ful acquisitions which were made to landlords' wealth when the common lands were enclosed in this wholesale manner.

Mr. J. BASKERVILLE, Liberal agent, Widnes, sec- onding, said that too often the life of the agri- cultural labourer was limited to his day's work and the public-house in the evening. He knew nothing of, the intricate problems which agitated the world of men and so vitally affected their lives.

Mr. C. A. DORAN, Rochdale, the president, in asking Mr. A. H. Cro,sfield to address the meet- ing, hoped to see Mr. Crosfield in the House of Commons after are General Election of 1915. Warrington people thought highly of Mr. Cros- field, and Warrington people were determined to send him to Parliament. (Applause.) At present they had in Warrington a not un- distinguished gentleman of the name of Smith, related to a somewhat notorious galloper— (laughter)—and Mr. Smith was at present over- running with sympathy for people who were never likely to be in trouble. Mr. Harold Smith had been seeking to harrow the feelings of the people of Warrington by pretending that there was some necessity to prepare for the housing of the refugees from Ulster. (Laugh- ter.) It was impossible that there would be any such insurrection in North-East Ulster• as Mr. Harold Smith professed was certain. It would be,. added Mr. Doran, far more sen- sible, far more genuinely charitable, and far less silly, for Mr. Harold Smith and his influ- ential friends to turn their practical sympathy to the relief of poor women and children in Dublin, the people who are suffering from the industrial trouble, and not seek to make poli- tical capital out of. trouble that is not likely to come in Ulster. (Applause.)

GLADSTONE'S DEFINITION OF A RADICAL Mr. CROSFIELD, who was enthusiastically re- ceived, said : It is a special honour and privilege to addreP4' a gathering composed entirely of brother Lan- castrians, and I always enjoy making .a speech to members of Liberal Clubs. I am doubly fortunate to-day in being able to do both; I may say I am trebly fortunate, for I am not only speaking to Lancastrians and members of Liberal Clubs, but 1 am also speaking in the pre.sence of one of the most able and most cour- ageous Liberals in Lancashire—Mr. Martindale. (Applause.) lam sure I shall voice your feel- ings and hopes when I heartily wish Mr. Mar- tindale a long and eminently successful career. (Cheers.) I need not say much to emphasise the advan- tage of Liberal Clubs. That is patent to all of you. 'We all know the value of social inter- course for which these clubs furnish the op'por- tunity. The training which the management of a Liberal Club gives to its members is to some emtent a training in self-government. Then it is an admirable thing that Liberals can meet in their clubs and ascertain the differ- ent points of view and learn the diverse opin- ions of fellow-members, because, thank Heaven, Liberals are not all of one mental cut. They have the faculty of thinking for themselves, and therefore mercifully they are alive. People who, like a flock of sheep, follow one particular opinion, and cannot think for themselves, cut a very poor figure by comparison. (Hear, hear.) The best a man can do for his fellow-members is to be himself; to give them the benefit of his individuality, and so help to enrich their lives. If they 'do the same, his character, in turn, is enriched by their varied and striking individuality. (Hear, hear.) Club life does not mean any loss of that, and I should not envy the man who came to give any advice to Lancashire lads which, if followed, would mean a loss of individuality, because there is no ' county in England where men respect and 're- gard individuality and grit more than in the County Palatine. (Hear, hear.) lam not sure that Liberals a generation ago were quite so alive to the advantages to be gained from club life as they are to-day. I have sometimes won- dered how that came about. It may be that it has something to do with what Mr. Gladstone said, many- years ago, when he gave us his famous definition of a Radical. "Radicals," he said, "are men who are .in earnest." (Ap- plause.) Well, it is a very good thing for poli- ticians to be in earnest. (Hear, hear.) We have plenty in modern Liberalism to make us earnest, but still a man cannot be in earnest all the time, and if he could it would not be at all a good thing for him. (Laughter and cheers.) Therefore, gentlemen, I say, let us enjoy our bowls and our billiards, and all the rest of it, and by no means under-estimate the value of this lighter side of club life. (Hear, hear.) If I say that, let me say this also, gentlemen: there is not a man in this room who sets more store by the still more valuable and important opportunities which club life in the Liberal Clubs of Lancashire afford' for political discussion and political education. There is a very familiar and celebrated Eng- lish maxim, ." Knowledge is power," and in a most literal sense that is absolutely true. Don't we all of us instinctively recognise our master in the man who, without sparing himself, has applied his brain power and wrestled with his subject until he has fairly grasped it and made it completely his own. Well, if club life gives opportunities for political education, there are no clubs where that should be more highly valued than the Liberal Clubs, whose functions we are considering. Because, after all, the great battle we Liberals have to fight is the battle

===== 0002601_19131101_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
THE "FISHY" NATIONAL SERVICE AGITATION.

And so, after the sound beating we have given them at three successive elections, the Tories see they have no chance of worsting us in a frontal attack, and it is the more necessary for us, gentlemen, therefore, to be on our guard against any attempt to defeat us by an attack on our flank. (Hear, hear.) I propose to say a word or. two about that, because this part of the country is an active centre for the efforts made by the members of the National Service League to introduce what they describe as uni- versal military service, but what we have to deal with more briefly as threatened conscrip- tion. (Hear, hear.) Now the leaders of this campaign very often take up a non-party atti- tude, and some of them I am sure do so in all good faith. But there is another type of men with whom I am sure.you are familiar—the type of men who are always telling us they hate party politics, and stand themselves only for the interests of th 3 nation, and yet strange to say, seldom miss an opportunity , for stabbing Liberalism and the Progressive party in the back. (Laughter, hear, hear, and cheers.) It seems to me a little bit fishy that this yiolent agitation for compulsory military service, and all the lurid talk we have had about the immi- nent danger of overwhelming disaster with which this country is threatened, should syn- chronise with the advent of a Liberal Govern- ment to power. (Hear, hear.) We heard pre- cious little about it all during those long years when the Tories ruled the State, and it really will not do for agitators who belong to this ex- treme military school to turn round and say that the country only is in danger when Liberals are in power. That seems to be almost the position they take up. But it is an absurdly untenable position for anyone to attempt to occupy at the present time. Why, gentlemec, never in the whole history of our country was our first great line of defence,- the Navy, in a finer state of efficiency. For Imperial purposes, that is to say, for service over sea at a moment's notice, we have an expeditionary force, rela- tively well paid, and very highly trained, as such a force needs to be, which is far in advance of any oversea force possessed by any other world power. (Hear, hear.) , Besides all this, we have in the Territorial Army—(cheers)---a more effective and efficient military organisation than were ever the chi Volunteers before .them. (Cheers.) I grant you there may still be more to be done, 'Jai I do say that these wild cries of alarm, coming from those whose game it is to frighten us into con- scription, are simply preposterous. (Hear, hear.)

Exploiting Lord Roberts.

_ Now this campaign is being led by a very eminent and distinguished soldier, about Thom I have never said, and never would v. hisper, one word of disrespect. He is a man with a brilliant military career behind him, a grand record of devoted service to the State, and holding the strong opinions which he certainly does on this question, I say it is a very fine thing, at his great age, to see Lord Roberts (cheers)—so persistently and strenuously throw- ing his energies into the work of advocating the Cause he has at heart. (Cheers.) _ _ .

Yes, but there are people who are exploiting him; they are trying to make it appear that the decision of a great question of policy comes within the scope and the sphere of Lord Roberts' work as a great soldier. There we Liberals join issue. alear, hear.) On a ques- tion like this, a great question of policy, we look for guidance to our Statesmen—(cheers)— and it is for the Statesman, having weighed the issues and the pros and cons, and taken their decision as to their line of policy, to then in- vite the military expert to advise them as to the best means to give effect to that policy. (Hear, hear.) Liberal Military Policy.

If you ask me, gentlemen, to give you a defini- tion of our policy, I could not do better than quote the language of Mr. J. A. Spender, the brilliant editor of the "Westminster Gazette," who has done such splendid work for the Lib- eral party, and who, amongst other things, has devoted himself to a special study of these mili- tary questions. What Mr. Spender says about our policy is this It is to have a Navy which shill be equal to any probable combination against us, and a policy Which will keep the probable combinations against us to what we can meet without a gross inflation of arma- ments.

GOOD WISHES FROM THE

EMPLOYES.

On Friday, Councillor Peter Peacock, the Mayor-elect, severed his connection with, Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, sOap manu- facturers, with which firm he had been asso- ciated for fourteen years, as a capable and conscientious traffic manager.

Councillor Peacock's staff in the traffic and locomotive department presented him with -a silver framed photograph of himself. They also gave a silver-framed photograph to Mrs. Peacock. Each present bore an inscription to the effect that it was given with the best of wishes.

WORKS' COUNCIL CONGRATULATIONS. The following is a copy of a letter that has been addressed to Councillor Peacock

"Bank Quay, Warrington, 30/10/1913. ' Mr. Peter Peacock:

"Dear Sir,—

"The members of the Works Council, who represent the whole of the departments in the Bank Quay Soap Works, wish, on 'behalf of the employes of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd., to offer their heartiest congra- tulations to you on the occasion of your recent appointment to the Mayoralty of this town. "They sincerely hope and trust that you may be spared to act and judge as discreetly in your new office as you have whilst in touch with the workpeople of the above firm.

"Signed on behalf of the Works Council, "J. R. TAYLOR."

Councillor Peacock desires us to contradict a rumour to the effect that he has accepted an appointment out of town. He does not intend to accept any appointment during his Mayoral year.

Councillor Peacock requested the Rector to act as his Chaplain, and Canon Willis kindly consented to do so. s

The Mayoral election will take place on Monday, November 10th, and Mayor's Sunday wifl be November 16th. The service will be at the Parish Church.

So numerous have been the letters of con- gratulation received by Councillor Peacock that he has been unable to reply to them. He wishes us to express his thanks to his many correspondents, and we have great pleasure in complying.

A NEW CEMENT ENTERPRISE.

The Cement industry of the United Kingdom is mostly confined' to the River Medway, the East Coast and the Midlands, but this will not always obtain. The adoption of the rotary kiln has revolutionised the manufacture of cement, yet the first was only laid down about thirteen years ago. It was a mere pigmy, too. for the length was only 60ft., with a diameter of Oft. Now we see running huge kilns of 2501 t. long and with diameters of 12ft. The rotary kiln was invented by an Englishman named • Ransome, but its development •is due. to the enterprise of the cement manufacturers- of the United States, and with a minor exception Eng- lish cement makers started using this type of kiln only about ten years ago. The advantages of the rotary kiln over other types are con- tinuity of operation, economy, and lastly, but most important of all, the burning is in full view and control of the kiln attendant, thus ensuring the production of properly calcined cement clinker—which when subsequently imil- verised is cement. Engineers and others have now come mainly to rely on Portland cement in their constructional work, using cement in greater quantity, and they were not long in evolving the ferro-concrete system, which for construction purposes has been found to be cheaper and more durable than any other. Every important building is constructed on this principle, and as cement is utilised to a very large extent; it is seen that consumption must grow. The use of cement for constructional purposes will not be confined to palatial edifices, for a cottage can be erected at a cost of £9O, but as we are still prejudiced against anything new, the concrete cottage may not become uni- versal for a few years. Look at the scope for cement on the main roads of this country. It has been laid down that a concrete foundation is essential, having regard to the wear and tear to which they are subject. Nearly every dam, wharf and railway station is now built of con- crete, and had it not been for cement the Panama Canal could never have been cut, because it would never have been possible to find a substitute for cement. Everyone will admit that the scope for a Company manufac- turing cement is practically illimitable, and we are glad to see that an enterprise initiated privately has achieved success. Its title is The Ship Canal Portland Cement Manufac- turers, Limited. The raw materials in the shape of limestone, situated at Orme's Head, and a shale deposit contiguous to the railway between Colwyn Bay and Llandudno have been secured. The works have been concentrated on a site of 60 acres fronting the Manchester. Ship Canal, and into which the two important rail- ways—the Great Western and North-Western— run. Possessing such excellent physical advan- tages, the Company started on its career with everything in its favour. The plant is designed and laid out for eight rotary kilns, two of which are now in operation, and when completed the plant will produce 300,000 tons per annum. Later ‘3ll it may be found advisable to further augment the plant, and the site is large enough to instal sufficient .kilns to manufacture 900,000 tons yearly. The quality of the cement is superb. It has passed the most strenuous tests, and the Company already possesses a long list of customers consisting of the chief railways and public authorities. The Ship Canal Port- land Cement Manufacturers, Eimited, started under such excellent auspices, is likely to rank as one of the most important enterprises on the 7.lfanchester Ship Canal, to whose shareholders it should bring much grist to the mill in the , shape of traffic .receipts. The public will have an opportunity of participating in the New Issue of Shares to be made next Monday.

MR. ARTHUR CROSFlELD—Continued.

Edward Grey. But if in addition to naval and military power we at present possess we were to convert ourselves into a nation in arms, that would lead‘to a gross inflation of armaments. Of course we should become a menace to Con- tinental powers, and the upshot and effect of that would be to intensify this mad rivalry in armaments—(hear, hear)—which already makes large inroads on the nations' resources, both in men and money. (Hear, hear.) Gentlemen, that is not the policy of Liberalism. aur party has very different work, and far better work before it—work which opens up great possibil- ities, and a wide and spacious horizon before us all. (Cheers.) History books tell us of the great and spacious days of good Queen Bess, and we are proud of them, and of those inspir- ing traditions. Sometimes, indeed, whom people begin enlarging over-much on those "good old times," and the "golden days of old," one is inclined to remind them of the seamy side there was to it all. (Hear, hear.) A great his- torian has warned us, gentlemen, against culti- vating too great reverence for antiquity, and whatever may be our admiration for the finer • records of the past, don't let the glamour of tradition blind us to the gigantic opportunities that are opening up before us even now—oppor- tunities which, if we make full use of them under the guidance of the gifted and brilliant,, and really able men who are leading us, will in the long run help us to bring the advene of golden days such as Merrie England never knew before. (Loud cheers.)

BILLIARDS.

WARRINGTON LEAGUE MATCHES.

Some Interesting Contests.

The Warrington Billiard League fixtures are now in full swing, and already some surprises have arisen. The Senior Conservatives gained a victory over Rylands' by 79. • The Senior and Junior Liberals had a close fight, and the elders won by 43 points. The Parish Church Institute have a strong team this season, and succeeded in beating White- -cross Social Club by 109. In the Junior' Divi- sion some close games have been w,itnessed, but the Conservatives gained a 223 points victory over Rylands'. - - .

On Thursday night the Junior Liberals beat the Senior Conservatives by 761 to 738.

A meeting -of the Billiard Council is to take place at the Latchford Conservative Club on Monday, 10th November, at 8 p.m.

ANGLING.

Warrington Workingmen's

Association.

N.A. CENTRE.

A GRAND DACE.

Although a fair crowd of members were out last wee-end, results were very poor, and several have given up at Norton, for at least this season. The GOwy claims their attention at present. Those who visited Norton had very poor sport, and nothing was done to upset the board. • Hartford again Would not yield any speeimens, and those who visited this favourite contest water turned their attention to a practice in •match • angling. The Gowy again was a disappointment, with the exception of about three members. Mr: F. Trigger took a splendid dace of 12oz. 2drs., Which now tops the board for open waters. This is another from a water where the majority of specimens have come from this season, and although anglers may go to the stream and not take a fish, they know the, specimens are there, /and that with the uncer- tainty makes the Gowy a success, although for the number of Warringtonians who visit its banks, we ought to, have better returns. The other successful members were Messrs. Bryers and Jolley, who caught from six to a dozen fish, but none of them • were good enough.' Other members had very poor sport, and the rain we were crying out for has not improved matters as we expqcted.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 1, 1913.

DISTRICTii new:l

STOCKTON HEATH

TOWNSHIP TITTLE-TATTLE.

A new terror awaits the 'Parish Council. A parishioner has made a daring suggestion. It is that the Meetings should be opened with prayer. Is IL true a deputation waited upon The Chairman of the Council to make the suggestion? The Parish Meeting, however, seems to be The proper place to make the proposal. What would some folk have to pay to it? The Somerset, one of the largest ships yet Seen in the Canal,. passed 411.0116 this week. It took skilful seamanship to get The mighty trader past the Swing Bridge. For a moment it looked as if she was Going to run aground and give us A shipping disaster at Our own door. Amateurs gardeners have established a record This season with their outdoor chrysan- themums. • _

Young " Heathens" have been delighted; old folk 'annoyed. A rumour had it that a picture palace Was to be, erected on a prominent site. Fortunately rumour has proved an untruthful jade. "Is the new tower at Wilderspool Brewery," Writes a "Heathen," "to' which you referred last week, "Erected to house the Ulster refugees, promised? "If so, the accommodation would be rather small." Three thunderstorms in 48 hours is not bad. And in addition. we had a minor cyclone, Followed by a deluge of rain and hail. And all this in. the end of Oetober ! Quite a crowd gathered outside the Wesleyan Church • On Sunday evening to listen to the fine music. Was it a dodge to escape paying to the collection? . .

Prhaps it was a passionate love of good music. .

that Stockton Heath can't play football. • _

The local lads gave a fine exhibition on Saturday, And entered the third round of the Cheshire Junior Cup. An "Examiner ' reporter was taken as a reformer at the•match. Lack of writing accommodation led him to appropriate A small board he found in the dressing On the board was the prominent notice, thus: "Swearing not allowed." • A spectator made use of an unparliamentar3/ expression. - "flush!, said his friend, pointing .to the board in the reporter's hands, "There's a committee man taking the names of them that swear." The scribe smiled to find himself playing unconsciously a new part. .

Mr. H. J. Westbrook,--Advices received the week indicate that Councillor H. J. Westbrook, who recently had a serious illness, is progressing favourably at Llandudno, where he went to recuperate.

The Adult Schools.—lnteresting meetings were held at both the Men's Adult School and the Women's Adult School on Sunday. Our account of -these gatherings will be found weekly in an- other column.

The Late Mr. Robert Wyllie.--We regret to announce the sudden death at Warrington Cen- tral Station on Saturday, of Mr. Robert Wyllie. An appreciatiou of his interesting and worthy career and an account of his funeral will be found on page 7.

Fire Brigade Practice.—The re-organised Fire Brigade held another practice on Saturday after- noon. The turn-out of the members was not so large as on the previous occasion, but some use- ful work was accomplished. " Captain ". blcDermid is very hopeful of yet having a smart voluntary brigade formed in the township.

A Thriving Bible Class.--The Bible Class con- nected with the Independent Methodist Church is in a thriving condition, and the members have been greatly impressed by the lectures on the Apostle Paul delivered by Mr. A. Mounfield. Owing to Mr. Mounfield being unable to attend on Wednesday. the Rev. W. Reid conducted the class. Mr. Mounfield resumes his course of lectures next Wednesday.

To Found a Social Class.—At a meeting of the Sunday Men's Afternoon Class connected with the Independent Methodist Church, Stockton 'Heath, on Sunday, it was suggested that the class should hold a week-night meeting for the discu§sion of social topics of the day, more par- tictflarly the Church's relation to the events of the present age. A meeting is to be held to found such a useful class.

Whist Drive and Dance.—The second annual whist drive of the Stockton Heath Young Men's Club was held in the Parish Room on Thursday evening, and proved a great success. The fol- lowing were the prize-winners :—Ladies-1, Miss D. Swinton; consolation, Miss M. Sankey. Gentlemen-1, Mr. B. Shaw; consolation, Mr. B. Isherwood. The dancing wa4 continued until early on the following morning.

Wesleyan Church Anniversary.—The eighth anniversary of the founding of the now thriving Wesleyan Chufch in-Stockton Heath was held on Sunday, when large congregations were in attendance. -.The Rev. J. Galland Hartley, a well-known Wesleyan preacher, provided the sermons at the morning and evening services. In the afternoon the choir gave a special sacred musical service, and the vocal work was of ex- ceptional merit, the choir now being recognised as one of the finest in the Warrington Circuit. On Monday evening a meeting was held. Mean- while the ladies of the congregation are hard at work preparing for the annual sale of work to be held in a few weeks' time.

Useful Men.—The providing of a church tea is so much associated with the ladies that they have come to be looked Upon as the people to perform that function. The men associated with the Wesleyan Church, however, proved that they are capable of the work at a tea party they gave on Monday night. 'Mere men set the tables, cut and buttered the bread, brewed the tea and served the meal to their guests, the 14dies. The service was excellent and the ladies *ere hearty in their appreciation. A musical evening followed and the whole entertainment was a great success.—A presentation of a hand- some timepiece was ,made to Mr. Henry Brer- ton in recognition .of his faithful and devoted services to the church for 21 years.

Motor Fire Engine for .Wilderspool.—The Warrington Fire Brigade, with the new motor engine, held a test at Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co's Wilderspool Brewery on Wednesday. Several members of the Council, along with representatives of the brewery, were interested spectators. The motor engine quickly demon- strated its superiority over the old type of fire engine. The test over, an informal gathering was held at the invitation of Mr. Stone, at which he proposed the toast of "Alderman Burton and the Fire Brigade Committee." In a few words he happily described the good feeling and spirit of comradeship which had always existed be- tween the Wilderspool Brigade and the Corpor- ation Brigade, and he hoped that in the future they' might even be cemented closer together.— In thanking Mr. Stone, and proposing the toast of "Mr. Stone and Messrs. Greenall, Whit- ley and .C 0. Alderman Burton expressed the

hope that although their fire engine was one of the most modern and up-to-date machines in the country, they might never have occasion to use. it. He assured Mr: Stone that the kindly' spirit and sentiment of the Wilderspool Brigade were fully reciprocated by the Corporation Bri- gade. Reference was made to the whole-hearted enthusiasm and efficiency of Superintendent Turner in his long years of service. The feeling was unanimous that Warrington was fortunate in possessing a "fire fighter' of his experience and sense of duty.—Mr. Stone briefly replied to the toast. He stated that the new engine had come so satisfactorily out of the tests that Mr. Whitley had decided on the purchase of a simi- lar engine on a smaller scale for the Wilderspool Brigade.

WEDDING OF MISS H. WOODS AND MR. A. KAY. An Interesting Local Marriage.

A very pretty wedding of much interest to Stockton Heath was solemnised on Thursday afternoon at the Bold-street Wesleyan Church, Warrington. The bride was Miss Helen Woods, eldest daughter of the late Mr. John Woods, 14, Kimberley-drive, Stockton Heath, and the bride- groom was Mr. Arthur Kay, son of the late Mr. Kay, of Lower Walton. The ceremony was performed by the Rev. H. Williamson. The bride, who was given away by her brother, Mr. Peter Woods, wore a dress of cream silk: eolienne, and carried a bouquet of white chry- santhemums, whilst the bridesmaids, Misses .Cissie and Mary Woods, sisters of the bride, were dressed in grey eolienne, and carried cream chrysanthemums. The reception was held at the bride's home, prior to the happy- couple's departure to the Isle of Man for the honeymoon. The best man was Mr. A. Saunders.

AN INTERESTING FUNCTION.

An interesting function marked the meeting of St. Thomas' Men's Class—" B" section—held at that church on Sunday afternoon. At the close of the devotional service a couple of pre- sentations were made to celebrate two interest.- ing events. •

Mr. Morris, the secretary of the class, was presented with a china tea servite on the occa- sion of his marriage to Miss Lloyd, Field's Cot- tage, Burtonwood. Mr. R. Hatton, who is about to depart for Canada, was presented with a scarf pin as a memento of the pleasant times he had spent in connection with the class at Stockton Heath.

In making theipresentatinn to Mr. Morris, Mr. Hallows referred to the great work Mr. Morris had accomplished on behalf of that class. It was no light task for a busy man of business like Mr. Morris to take in hand the work of secretary to a large class like that of St. Thomas' Church. Mr. Morris, however, threw himself heart and soul into the duties of secretary; he did the work admirably and no man could be more painstaking in the fulfilment of his duties. (Applaust.) Mr. C. Hallows, on behalf of the committee, also presented Mr. Morris with a handsome tea- pot. _ _ _

- Mr. Morris briefly responded. He was ex- tremely touched by their kindness and he accepted the -presents in the spirit in which they were meant—a proof of their sincere friend- ship and good feeling.

The class presented -Mr. R. Hatton with a medal, suitably inscribed, and the committee contributed a scarf pin. In making the pre- sentation of the gift from the class, Mr. Hallows said Mr. Hatton would be missed at their meet- ings, but he would be a great gain to the Church in Canada. (Applause)

Mr. Baguley made the presentation on behalf of the committee, and said while regretting the. departure of Mr. .Batton .they Wished him the greatest success in , his new life .across the Atlantic.

Mr. Hatton, deeply touched, replied; and ex- pressed. his regret that he had not been able to do more' for the class which he loved so well, and which he would always remember, although he was So far away from it. (Applause.)

HIGH LE Gil.

Miss Legh's Missionary Life.—There was a very large attendance at the High Legh Schools on Monday night, when Miss May Cornwall Legh, cousin of Colonel Legh, lectured on mis- sionary work in Japan. The Colonel presided, and following upon references to the lecturer's connection with the village in her girlhood, Miss Legh gave illustrations of the Japanese as a smiling and agreeable people. In an entertain- ing manner she explained the customs and the styles of dress of these picturesque people, and asked how it was that they needed Christianity, seeing that they appeared so happy? It was only. when they, got to know the inside of the JapaneSe lives, and their hearts, she explained, that they would begin to know how trethend- ously they needed God. Often behind their smiling faces were hearts with dreadful sorrows. Sometimes these 'people' were not steadfast Christians, but really it was often very difficult to be so, when friends and family were against them. She asked for .their sympathy and sup- port for the missionaries, who were the repre- sentatives of those who stayed at home. The speaker had been working amongst those people for five and a half years, and when she was leaving them a farewell meeting was held, last- ing from seven o'clock in the morning until nine 'o'clock in. the' evening.. (Laughter.) It was, however, her intention to return and spend the 4est of her days there.—The Rev. H. A. D. Surridge thanked Miss Legh on behalf of those present.—Mrs. E. H. Edwards accompanied the singing at the piano.

PADGATE.

Mrs. Colds and I nfluence,Mrs. Dodds pre-- sided atjlie meeting of the Wesley _Guild on. Monday' evening, and the subject• of "Temper- ance" was chiefly dealt with. Mrs. Dodds re- ferred to the inspiring temperance meeting which she attended some weeks ago at Belle Vue. It would be very beneficial if they would all exercise their influence in the cause of tem- perance, Contending that example was better than precept, Mrs. Moores, of Warrington, appealed to all to let their example be on the side of temperance. During the evening a num- ber of girls gave•it concerted recitation, whilst Mrs. Dakin and Mrs. A. Ward pleased with their duet. Miss A. Tomlin 41so gave a recitation in a ;splendid manner. Sister h ood.—A weekly feature at the Wesleyan Church is the meeting of the Sisterhood, an in- s+itution which has taken the place, with satis- factory results, of the defunct Mothers' Meeting. The meetings are held on Tuesday afternoons, and the democratic spirit is fostered. Religious exercises are not allowed to monopolise the whole time, but are intermingled with short, bright addresses, songs, Sze. Recently a Savings Club has been organised, by which the members may save by small weekly instalments. All members, whether needing it or not, are ex- pected to contribute, and smart indeed are those who evade the energetic secretary for the fund. At the meeting held on Tuesday an address‘ was given by Mrs. Thomson. Two solos were beautifully rendered by Miss B. London.

• Punctuality is the life of business. Do our worthy councillors realise The trtith,qf this apci,ent proverb? . The morithly'rneetings:•ai;e:Called for 7.30 p.m. Not once in twelve months have they started At the time announced, and generally They are at least half an hour late. The Choral Society has got a new lease of life. Judging from the first rehearsal they are going to do well.

Teachers' "Rest."—The teachers of ,the Lymm, Oughtrington and Statham Schools have been enjoying a mid-term " rest " most of this week. The short vacation was highly appreci, ated by the children, though hardly received with equal elation by the parents.

Two Gas Lamps.—The Gas Committee of the Council resolved that two incandescent burners for lamps be provided for Heatley Ward, one for Rush Green, 'and the other for Sandy-lane. The Council confirmed the action on Thursday.

The bisinfector.—At a meeting of the Lymin Council on Thursday it WhS decided that Mr. Beckett's name be included in the contract for the disinfector, as an independent person to be referred to in case of any disputes or differences arising out of the contract.

Offertories for the Poor.—The annual offer- tories for the sick and poor fund were taken at the Parish Church on Sunday- last; when, in response to the appeal for £3O, the amount re- quired to meet the necessary calls on the fund during the .next twelve months a total of :R.39 lis. 6d. was realised. •

New Ambulance Brougham.—At a meeting of the Lymm District Council on Thursday 'the Sanitary Inspector reported that the Chairman of the Council and he had inspected some ambu- lances on sale by the Manchester Corporation, and had made an offer of Al 6 for an ambulance brougham.—lt was resolved that the action of the Chairman of the Council and the Inspector be approved.

Setting of Retorts.—At a meeting of the Gas Committee of the Council the Gas Manager re- ported the progress of this work, and said that Mr. Dennis, the contractor; had suggested the raising of the arch, which had sunk nine inches: The work, would necessagily delay, the .comple- tion for about a week.—lt was resolved that the contractor's tender for the raising of the crown of the retort over the arch for £6 be accepted.— The Council on Thursday approved the minute.

Council Meeting.—At the monthly Meeting of the Lymm Urban District Council on Thursday, Councillor S. Davies presided, and there were present: Councillors W. G. Groves, G. B. Thurston, R. H. Gotobed, O. G. Smith, G. L. Welford, W. Gould, J. Holt, J. H. Warburton, with Messrs. W. Mullard (clerk); D. Mort (sur- veYor), W. L. Donaldson (gas manager), and Dr. De Jong (medical officer).

Slot Gas Meters.—The Council decided, on Thursday that the slot gas meters should be emptied more often in order to give the gas manager more time to attend to his duties at the works. It was also resolved that Mr. W. Chal- linor be appointed ,to collect the slot meter money in conjunction with the gas manager, and that he be provided with' a distinguishing badge, and that a guarantee of £5O be taken out, the premium to be paid by the Council.

Choral Society.—Considering the very incle- ment weather, there was a very good. turn up at the Council. Chambers on Tuesday night at the first rehearsal of the Lymm Choral Society, which has been resuscitated after a year's rest. The:society.have been fortunate in Securing the competent and willing services of Mr. J. H. Brazendale as conductor, whilst Mr. F. Champ is as energetic as ever in attending to the duties of secretary.

The Dam Bridge.—lii, regard to ,a proposed bridge over the Pain and, the new road by the Lyram Estate Development Co., Ltd., at a meet- ing of the Roads Committee of the Council the Surveyor produced a plan of the bridge of the upper lake proposed to be built by the company, and the block .plan of the proposed new road on the east side of the lake.—The Surveyor was instructed to obtain further particulars from the architects, Messrs. W. and Segar Owen, and it was resolved that the Council inspect the land and property included in the purchase from the Water Company.—The minutes were approved by the Council on Thursday.

Death of Mr. W. S. Hodgson.—lt was with profound regret that Lymm people learned on Tuesday evening that Mr. William Stanley Hodgson had passed away at his residence, "Fairfield." Cherry-lane. Mr. Hodgson had been ill for a long time as the result of an attack of influenza, and had recently decided that he would not continue his business of cabinet-making and upholstering. The deceased. who was of a very quiet disposition, and highly respected, was an able• worker in connection with the Lymm Church of England Men's Society; he was also the honorary treasurer of the Lymm Tradesmen's Association.

Farmer's Sudden Death.—A painful sensation was caused in the locality last week-end when it became known that Mr. Luke James, of Cherry- lane, had died suddenly on Saturday evening. The deceased, who was 35 years of age, with his brother, Mr. Arthur James, are well-known farmers. and it appears that the former had been working in the fields until dusk on Satur- day. when they had tea together. After the meal was over, Mr. Luke James, who had com- plained of pains in the stomach on the previous day, lay down on the Sofa to rest. Soon after- wards he ,was heard to groan, and the occupants of the room went to hint, but their efforts to ad- minister restoratiVes prPted to -be futile, and he passed away before a doctor could be summoned. —Mr. T. Ridgway conducted an inquest in the

Newfield Mission Room on Monday evening, when Dr. de Jong stated that death was due to angina pectoris, a verdict in accordance there- with being returned.—Mr. L James was an en- thusiastic committee member of the Lymm Liberal Association, and was a very energetic worker at the recent local bye-election. Before coming to Cherry-lane some years ago, the family lived at Grappenhall. The funeral took place at Warrington Cemetery. • A large num- ber of floral tributes were forwarded, including a wrdath from tlfetloCal LiberalS.'

Burford-lane Lighting.—At the meeting of the Council on Thursday Councillor Gotobed pro- posed that the Clerk he instructed to advertise for tenders for pipes necessary for the carrying out of the Burford-lane lighting scheme.—Coun- cillor Gould said the scheme was long over-due, and ought to be undertaken immediately. The ratepayers at that end of the ward felt very keenly on the matter.—The resolution was car- ried unanimously.

Gaswork gtokers' Conditions.—At a meeting of the Gas Committee of the Council considerable. discussion took place in reference to the working conditions of the stokers, . and the arrangement made by the Chairman of the Committee and the Gas Manager, whereby each stoker received one Sunday off every four weeks, in addition to the Saturday's holiday, were . approved by the Council at Thursday's meeting.—A letter was read from the National Union of Gas Workers and General Labourers, and it was decided to let the subject matter stand over for discussion for the purpose of a thorough investigation into the conditions under which .stokers work.—lt was resolved that Councillors Warburton, Goto-‘ bed and Groves, alohg with the Gas Manager, be empowered to attend at the Knutsford, North wick, Winsford, and Frodsham Gasworks to make inquiries' as to the working of stokers and gas management generally, and that their ex- penses be paid.

THE HOUSING PROBLEM.

Matter Again Deferred.

"That steps be at once taken by the Council. to supply the serious shortage of cottages in the Lymm district." Such was a resolution pro- posed by Coun. Smith at the Council meeting on Thursday. He said there was a shortage of cottages. They were told of many instances of overcrowding, and what was their remedy? They could move people from place to place, or turn them out, but that was not a remedy. He would like to remind the Council that they were the Sanitary Authority responsible for the sanitary and hygienic condition of the people, especially the poorer people, and he could not see that they would be doing their duty if they took no notice of the problem which confronted them. The scheme he submitted was self-sup- porting. As an experiment, it was proposed to erect six cottages on Council land, adjoining the Gasworks. They would occupy one-third of an acre, and the cost was worked out at five- pence per cubic foot. The total inclusive cost worked out at £lB4 per cottage. A charge of five per cent. on capital accounts represented £2 a year ground rent. The cost of each cottage would be: Land 68. Bd., .28 interest on capital, rates £2 10s., leaving a balance of contingencies, depreciation, &c., of 4,2 135., 4d. That was basing it on a charge of ss. 3d. per week rental, which yielded 0213 per year. The tenants would be charged an economic rent, and pay such rent as would recoup the Council for its expenditure, and the scheme was not to add to the financial responsibilities of the Council.

Councillor Gotobed seconded. He had men- tioned the matter before and knew how acute the question was in Lymm. Ile did not suppose there were.six empty cottages in Lynam, When they had such .a state of things the property owners could play with the tenants and demand any rent.

Councillor Holt moved that the matter be deferred for a short time. He did not think the condition of things was as had as when the matter was first mooted. There. were a num- ber of empty houses, and they had passed plans for seven cottages, which were almost com- pleted.

The Rev. G. B. Thurston seconded the amend- ment.

On a vote being taken, the amendment was carried and the matter was deferred.

GRAPPENH ALL.

GLEANINGS FROM GRA PPENIIA LL.

It has been- a week of socials, And other pleasant entertainments. A great success Was the drawing-room Concert On behalf of the Seamen's Friends Socirly. Mrs. C. Jackson Holmes made the even f One long to be remembered by those present. The music was not only high-class, but first- class. • The Liberals are to engage in whist Until the next election comes along. Then they will out-trick their opponents In the little game which is called polities.

"The Cry of Burmah."—A full-dress rehear- sal of a missionary pageant entitled "The Cry of Burmah " was given by. the parishioners of Stockton Heath in the large roam, Grappenhall, on Wednesday night. The Rev. James Collins opened the proceedings with a brief account of the habits and customs of the Burmese, and ap- pe-aled to the audience not to look upon it as an entertainment, but as an instructive service. The artistes, who were attired in the native garb of the various tribes, gave a ,very credit- able display. The singing was especially good. Miss Fawcett was the pianist. The proceeds, after paying expenses, were devoted to the forth- coming missionary exhibition.

Social Liberalism.—Through the hospitality of Mr. Frank Fox, the energetic hon. secretary of the Grappenhall District Committee of the Knutsford Division Liberal Association, a well- attended meeting was held at his residencel Victoria-road, on Thursday night. The object of the meeting was to put the committee in an organised state of efficiency, and to hold a series of whist drives, socials and meetings, with a view to stimulating interest in the ranks of Progressive Liberalism in the district. Mr. C. Jackson Holmes was in the chair, and Mr. Tom- linson and Mr. Cliffe, the Liberal agent, were also present. Due notice will be given of each event, which promises to be of an attractive character.

A Drawing-room Concert.—A crowded draw- ing-room concert in aid of the Seamen's Friends Society was given by Mrs. C. Jackson Holmes at Barnacre, Latchford Without, on Monday afternoon. The programme included works of Debussy, Chopin, Chamihade, Mendelsohn and Liszt, very ably rendered by Mr. Wills, of the Royal College of Music, Manchester. Special interest was shown in some beautiful composi- tions for two pianos performed by Mrs. Jackson Holmes and Mr.-Wills. Mrs. Haviour's violin playing was much enjoyed, as were also the songs of Miss Rodick. The collection amounted to £3 145., and Mrs. Ford's collection box was found to contain ..t1 is. 6d., which sums were handed to the local honorary secretary, Mrs. Dimmelow.

CROFT.

A £6OO Farm Fire.—A-fire, which resulted in considerable damage being done to the farm buildings of Eaves Brow Farm, Croft, occupied by Mr: John Gregory, and owned by Mr. Robert Walker, of Atherton, broke out on Sunday night. The Warrington Fire Brigade were summoned, and on arriving with the steam engine, in command of Sergt. W. Monks, they found that the buildings, consisting of barn, shippons and stables, were well alight. With the assistance of farm hands and friends, the live stock was got clear Of the burning build- ings before the arrival of the brigade. The barn contained a 30 ton stack, roughly valued at ,2100, which was completely destroyed. All through the night the firemen were at work, and it was not until 11 o'clock on Monday that they returned to headquarters. The damage is about £6OO, some of which is not insured. The cause of the fire is unknown*.

MOTHER OF LARGE FAMILY

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

'Venice.

This, evening, at the Co-6perative Hall, Cairo- street, that excellent lecturer, Mr. J. E. Phythian, is to enter upon a. Series on Venice. -As I read the public announcement, two tri- vial incidents somehow came uppermost in my mind. I thought how, as a youngster, I re- velled in such stories as "The Bravo "—a book the outstanding features of which are quite .fresh in my recollection, although I have not so Much as opened it for forty odd years. I remembered also a bit of talk I had with a well-to-do if somewhat illiterate Yorkshireman Who had travelled, and had been most deeply impressed with Venice. "We'd a sort o' fright When we got there," said he, and, recalling my old "Bravo," I pressed far the details: "Well," said he, "it was like th' edge o' dark when we got there, an' Of course we'd to be taken in a gondola.tQ where we were going to put .up. I didn't half like th' look o' th' chap i' charge o' cur's, an' when he shot it into a ginnel, I felt Tunny, I can tell you. But it was only a canal leading to th' back to our hotel, which opened on to th' Square o' St. Mark. You know we couldn't have gone th' front way in a gondola. Ile was honest enough was th' chap—but, oh, -dear, these glands!" Suicide.

At a Padgate inquest on a farmer who had been found drowned, a juror Observed that de- ceased had not left anything behind to indicate That he had, contemplated self-destruction, and this led the Coroner to remark that if he in- tended to commit suicide he would not an- nounce the fact to his family. So experienced a functionary, however, must have met with numerous instances where something very like that has actually occurred. It is not unusual for one about to take his life to write 'full par- ticulars- before perpetrating the foolish and wicked act. I have known such a person thus set down his reasons for throwing himself into the canal, and state the precise spot where his body might be found. It wap very thoughtful on his part, of course, being well calculated to save trouble, and no doubt the police were duly grateful; but when the jury returned a verdict of "Suicide 'during. temporary -insanity," vio- lating their solemn oath to give a " true" one, 44 according to the evidence," I was confirmed In my conviction that such inquiries are not in- Trequently farcical and worthless, and that the end might be much better attained by a more simple and inexpensive process without sum- miming a jury at all. All the same, I do not think the remark interposed by Cie Padgate juryman last week by any means uncalled for. Mixed.

In the Stockton Heath "Township Tittle- Tattle" I read of a native declaring he had recently seen the sun set in the east. Well, more than one of our popular novelists have represented the moon as rising in the west, to say nothing of other writers' blunders, of which have compiled a list. So that the Heathen in question stumbled into error in „good company. Beading between the lines, however, I rather suspect the suggestion to be that the gentle- man who was so grossly mistaken in his astronomy had at the Lime of observation dined not wisely hut too well—like the one who para- phrased :," One moon there was when I set out, but now (hic !) four moons I see 1" Fie! I must call round with a temperance pledge. Penitent.

I did once undertake a little missionary work of that character. My wife and I were walking along the main street when a drunken fellow stumbled against me. I said something that seemed to half sober him, and he said, " Tait to me about it, won't .you? I've two girls that go to St. Mary's, and they'd be glad to have a sober and respectable father." I took the man's name and, address and pro- mised to call on the morrow. On my Way to the house .1 drcipped in at St. Mary's for a copy cif the temperance pledge. When I asked for my penitent, his wife, who answered the door, 'said,- "Whatever has he been doing new?" I reassured her, and was in- vited in. I found the man in a 'somewhat diffet- eat mood, and I proceeded accordingly, though to my surprise his wife ,gave no encouragement whatever. He professed to have no knowledge of our encounter of the previous night, so I told him how he would have 'kept me from an important engagement to preach total absti- nence to him, a stranger; how he had men- tioned his children, being 'evidently proud of their association with St. Mary's, and how de- sirous he was that I should call upon him With a view to his reformation. And then, feigning anger and contempt, said: "Are you going to put me to all this trouble, and break Your word? You'll sign that pledge, sir, in- stantly, or I'll 'know the reason why." He Signed without more ado, and I explained to him that I should leave it at St. Mary's, with a request that they would look after him, and that I should•not expect him to make a feel of Ine or tolbring more shame on his sdaughter. tie was my only convert, and I had little reason to be proud of my conquest; for I was destined 800 n to discover that the sow had returned to her wallowing. I may add that my penitent (ought I to use either that or the word " con- vert " in this connection?) was a printer. Too Many of the trade were given that way in those far-off days. New, of course, they 'are all teetotalers and—now, now, now, don't throw it, there's a good soul,. put it down. Eh! \Vhat's that? You'd be only too glad to "put it down" if you might "chalk it up" to me. well; Christmas is not very far off, and— but, dear, dear, this will never do-for one who set out by claiming to be a temperance advo- cate tuld missionary. On this subject, raised at last week's meeting Of the Warrington Town Council, I have written in this column at least twice before. I could •asily fill the whole of my space with further Comments. Pure food laws are not .quite so Modern as might be supposed. Dr.' licisner has made discoveries in Palestine that seem to indicate some sort of supervision of the foOd fliPplies delivered to the palace nearly 3,000 Years ago. Labels have been found that .were °nee affixed to "a jar of pure olive oil."
