THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 1, 1913.

WRITING IJ TNE DAYS OF ABRAHAM.

Letters 1 Envelopes and Seas of Three Thousand Years Ago.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0211.txt | included=true | included =====
TOLD FOR YOUNG PEOPLE.

Twenty-five years ago Oliver Wendell Holmes wrote: "I believe in the 'spade. It has fed the tribes of mankind. It has provided them with water, coal, iron and gold, and now it is giving them truth—the means of which have never been opened till our times." His reference, of course, was to the discoveries which were being made in the East. To-day his words have a greater meaning than ever, for the finds have been such as none could. have anticipated. Classical scholars have rejoiced over the pre- cious architectural remains of Corinth and Delphi, and have been enriched by learning the vanished politics of Aristotle, one of the great political documents of the world, and by. scraps of Euriphides and Sophocles, whose words are more than gold to the eyes of the student. To Bible students have come the richest treasures of all. More than a century ago Vol- taire ventured the assertion that the Old:Testa- ment. is purely legendary, and without cor- roboration in secular history. None could gainsay him, for there was no 'outside light upon the Old Testament, and Voltaire boasted that he had destroyed men's belief in it. Twenty-five years ago, a Frenchman, of quite a different type, Ernest Henan, in his "History of Israel," said that intellectual culture, ,as we understand it, did not exist in the time of Joshua, and that the art of writing was not known in the time of Abraham. The inference was that the books could not have been written by Moses, and that narratives of that period could, therefore, ,only be traditional and of little worth. Henan could say This without fear

the sun they scratched a circle. To describe day they placed the sun coming above the horizon, as in the first line of our illustration. To say month they put the sign for 30 within the circle of the sun, as in the second line. A picture of a fiat-topped house was the word for house. A picture of a foot meant foot and stood for the verb to walk. Ai picture of a man stood for man, and if a mark of greatness were put over him he became a king. our illustration will show how other words were made. Thus we have in the bottom line the sign for " eye " and "water," put them together and we get the meaning "tears." The third line shows the sign for mouth (on the left). In the engraving on the right we have a sign added. This is the mark for food, and as the food is shown within the mouth, it means, to eat. The fourth line shows a foot and the verb to go.

lie fifth is very interesting. On the left is the sign for water, in the middle heaven; in the sign on the right they are shown joirred to- gether. This ,means " heaven water," or rain. But full pictures were difficult to execute, and in process of time easier signs were adopted. As civilisation advanced writing became more common, and stone gave place to moist clay. Since square forms are easier to make than round ones, the signs became more and more angular. Long before Abraham's time it was difficult to distinguish the original hieroglyph, from which the sign had come. Everything was written in straight lines or wedges.

of contradiction, for there was no evidence. Controversy followed the words, and critics asserted emphatically that there was no such thing aS a written language in- Palestine at so remote a date as that of Moses. it happened that during the discussion an Egyptian woman, while searching among some ruins at Tel-el-Amarna, in Egypt, found about 300 clay tabletS covered with Assyrian charac- ters. When it was found that these were letters dated from the cities of Palestine—Tyre, Sidon, Gaza and Jerusalem—during the fifteenth cen- tury before Christ, the debate at once ceased. Here were actual letters written on clay, signed and sealed, and full of interesting detail, writ- ten in the days of Abraham. Since then many things have come to light, and we find that the age of Abraham was period of great literary activity, and had liter- iture botjj abundant and varied.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0214.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EARLIEST WHITING.

It is interesting to get back to these relies of the past, and study them, with view to learn- ing what the'world was like as Abraham saw it. Let us study first the kind of writing upon the. clay. Language was originally written in hieroglyphics, that is, written in pictures. For

===== 0002601_19131101_art0215.txt | included=true | included =====
EARLY DOCUMENTS.

The clay slabs, when inscribed, were dried in the sun or in an oven, and became hard, so hard indeed that they have lasted, and can be easily read to-day. Many of , these small slabs, 4,000 years Old, can be seen in the British Museum, and are as perfect as when written. We usually suppose that envelopes are a modern invention, but discoveries in Babylon show that they are as old as writing itself. When the writer had finished his letter and hardened it, he placed round it a thin coat of clay. On this he wrote the name of the person to whom it was to go. When' baked, the en- velope would harden and shrink a little. The letter inside was therefore 'loose from the en- velope, but sufficiently packed to keep it from breakage. The rtcipient broke away the en- velope by a little bronze tool, specially made for the purpose. In this way lawyers—for there were lawyers in those days—drew up their land leases and agreements, merchants made out their bills, and schoolboys wrote theii. lessons. Among the clay slabs in London are some with lines which the boy has scratched to guide his writ- ing. We may picture the scribe of the day, first shaping. his clay, then patting it flat and smooth, and impressing his words upon it with a writing style made of bronze.

The writers were always careful to give their work a neat appearance, and wrote carefully and in measured lengths. When the letter or contract was written it was usually sealed, the seal consisting of an inscribed stone, often cir- cular, which was rim across the soft clay. If a Babylonian was too poor to posSess a seal, he signed by impressing his thumb -nail uPon he clay, and after his " mark " the name was written. We read often of seals in ,the Bible, especially in the Book of _Esther.

The method of inscription is interesting; and might be imitated to-day. The opening clause always gives the name of the sender and the recipient. Thus Isaac would, in writing to Abraham, say, "To my Father Abram from his son Isaac." It was thus the patriarchs wrote their letters, and as such letters are being found in large numbers every year, who knows but that some day we may find one of the actual letters signed with the seal or with the thumb nail of one of the men whose names are so familiar to us in !3ible history? A.M.

ARCHAOLOGY AND THE BIBLE.

Our Contributor ii A. 111.” Lectures to Teachers on the Marvels of the Ancient World.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
A DISTANT PAST UN,COVERED.

There was a crowded attendance at the Bew- sey-road Wesleyan Lecture Hall on the occasion of a lecture by Mr. Arthur Mountield. The ' meeting was held under the auspices of the Sunday School Union, and was' the first of a series. For mutual advantage the lecture was shared by members of the Wesley Guild, who enjoyed the opportunity to the full, and who were present in large numbers. The Bev. W. Beid presided. . 111 r. MOUNFIELD showed as the first•illustration a map of the ancient world, and the cradle of the bunion race, explaining incidentally the reasons why ths Jewish people were so often embroiled in the quarrels of Egypt, Assyria and Babylon. The first portion of the lecture was devoted to lantern pictures of relics of the earliest civilisatiofls and interesting descrip- tions thereof.. A receipted bill, six thousand years old, was shown, which was prepared ' and receipted like -a modern invoice, save that.. it was written upon a slab of claSi. An inter- esting point was that the values were written in two columns, one showing the value of the goods in corn and the other in silver; which indicated that in earliest, times corn gave the standard of value, but that six thousand years ago`things were changing, and silver was be- coming the standard. Another picture was of an agreement written on clay and kept •in a clay envelope, so shaped that it could be put on the. shelf like a.boOk, its title written on the back. As the land question is so much in mind, the lecturer showed the record of a land purchase. carved on stone by some ancient lawyer.. The fact that.sucli stones were. set up on the land itself to indicate the ownership, explained. the Bible curse upon the one who moved his neighbour's landmark. . .

Another section of the lecture described the Babylonish accounts of the creation of the World, and the Deluge, which were in many features like the stories of the Book of Genesis. An interesting picture of ancient 'worshipper showed that the 13a1)ylonians regarded the World as being a sphere, and Mr. ?Munkld quoted the passage, "He sitteth upon the circle of the earth," as evidence that the ancient Hebrews held the same belief. Touching • 'another aspect of Babylonish civilisation, Mr. Mounfield described the code .of laws 'prepared by Hammurabi in the period of Abraham. The old adage "An eye for an eye." was said to have had a literal application at that early day, for a doctor who caused a patient to lose an eye through an operation, was ordered to have one of his own eyes destroyed. The mOst valuable light given by these laws, was, in the 'lecturer's opinion, that thrown upon the story of Abraham; Sarah and Hagar. Harsh things had ~been said of Abraham and his conduct, but these laws showed that he was moving in harmony with both law and what' might be called "public

opinion," when he acted as he did. The Assyrian pictures were of gveatest in- terest to students Of the lb Tile. They consisted of historical records carved on stone, and relat- ing to-Ahe wars of the Assyrian kings and Of pictorial slabs from their palace walls.. Two monuments of King Shalmanasser were shown

TA BL E TA LK.

The World's Tribute of Interesting Items of News.

QUAINT AND AMUSING.

Matches have not bpen in use for a hundred years yet. It was in 1833 that Isaac liolden, of London, found out the secret of putting the sulphur of the igniting.pixture directly on the wood, and tim match was .made.. They were called lucifer matches at first, because the word, while applied usually to Satan, really means light bringer, from the. Latin lux, light and fero, I bring.

Origin of Leap Year.

The privilege of Leap Year by which a woman may ' ask a man to marry her, originated in 1228, when the Scottish Parliament passed an Act that during the reign of Queen Margaret " ilka maiden ladee of baith high and lowe estate shall hue' liberty to 'speak ye man she likes," and if he refuses he may be fined a hun- dred pounds, unless he can prove ..lat he is already engaged.

For Dancing Shoes.

Dancing shoes with nonskid rubber plugs in the soles are one of the strange, results of the new • eccentric' dances, '.says the . -Popular. Mechanics Magazine. The glassy, slippery dancing soles inherited from former genera, lions are too dangerous for the new. steps, and many of the dancing pumps seen in the shoe stores to-day are equipped with„safOy friction spots.

A Crocodile's Hoard.

A party of well-known ,Johannesburg gentle- men on a shooting trip to the Transvaal-Por- tuguese border recently shot a crocodile which, on being opened by the natives, was found to contain twenty-live sovereigns, some Victorian, some Kruger, and one King Edward dated 1902. Their intrinsic value has, .it is stated, been decreased owing to their deterioration while in the animal's stomach.

A Baby Wildebeeste's Precocity,

A Government ranger in the Subic Reserve, Transvaal, recently came upon a wildebeeste calf on the veld, which could not have been day old. On the instant, it ran off mote atfullthsapneea d and the hunter pursued on horse- back, momentarily expecting it to fall over from utter exhaustion. At the end of a 25 minute burst the animal appeared to have got into its stride, and with a toss of the head put on more pace and rapidly Outdistanced the dis- comfited ranger.

Kind-Hearted Eccentricity.

The policemen on duty outside the House of Coii-imons are Wondering what has become of the elderly lady who used to drive, up three times a week with a companion to feed- the pigeons near the entrance to the House of Lords. For many years she had been in the habit of performing this kindly act until the pigeons got to know slier so well that they would pitch on her hands to be fed. Another elderly lady used to walk round a. square near the Westminster School late at night carrying a basket of cats' meat. With this she fed every stray cat she could find. Sometimes' there would be as many as 100 cats .all mewing after her at once.

141nglish.Nobility Settles in Ontario.

That Ontario is ;jot only attracting from Great Britain the man who wishes to engage in farm work, but Ws.° the capitalist members of titled families, is brought to mind. by the fact that the Earl and Countess of Clarendon are at .present visiting their son, Lord Hyde, who owns 'a farm at Pickering and is working it himself. Not far from his is another English nobleman, Lord Somers, who is carrying on the same work With good results. There are, of course, in other parts of the province many other titled Britishers, and one of the latest to go to Ontario to take up ,fruit farming is Vis- count Glentworth, son of the Earl and Countess of Limerick. Tha settlement of leading British families in Ontario is not of recent date. It is over a century ago since the Hon. Thomas l'albot took up a farm in the province.

His Had Boy was Q.

In a Chicago Police Court, \ niliam Palmer, an aged man, apPealed to the judge for assist- ance in dealing with, his son, William. " Wil- liam .Is sixty-three," said the father, who is himself eighty-six, "and I think he should stop sowing wild oats. He drinks a great-deal. He comes home and acts like ia young .colt. I find it difficult to control Tim You can see for yourself that he must be hard for MC to man- age. He weighs 250 .pounds, while I only weigh 95." " I agree with you," said the Court, addressing the father. " William is getting to be a big boy now. It's time he learned to be- have himself. I'll give him just one minute to sign the pledge." William signed.

What Do Starfish See?

At the end of each arm in the common star- fish there is a little red eye. It is sheltered at the base of the terminal tube foot, which has become altogether sensory. The eye oe eye cushion show numerous little ‘cups each closed by a.,fine . lens, lined by red rod-like, sensory cells, clothed externally by supporting cells and containing a transparent watery substance. Hellmuth Messner has recently, made a num- ber of experiments at Heligoland in order to discover how much a 'starfish sees with these " or eye spots. The answer is: "Not very much." It does not form an image nor does it perceive a moving object. But it has considerable sensitiveness in distinguishing different degrees of light and shade. Even the skin of the starfish is responsive • to differdnees of illumination in the immediate vicinity.

Australia's Wattle Day in London.

Recently a branch of the -Australian Wattle Day League was formed in London, and it is proposel to celebrate. Wattle Day in London in conjunction with Australia Day, in January. The League is already well established in Australia, and the Wattle blossom (Which re- sembles the mimosa) is now. known as Austra- lia's national flower. At the celebration, in Adelaide on. August 30th, the FederaL President of the League, in the course of his speech, re- ferred to the widespread character of the move- ment, and to the fact that the League had se- cured the recognition of the wattle by the Com- monwealth Government, as it bad been placed in the Australian coat-of-arms, and the Post- master-General had had it inserted in the de- sign of the new stamps.

New Books.

Lollardy and the Reformation. By Dr. James Gairdner, C. 13.; edited by Hunt, C.B. Vol. IV. London: Macmillan and Co., 1016 net.

The fourth volume of .Dr. James Gairdner's 'work was left by the author in an unfinished state at his death, and has been completed and seen through the press by the friend, Dr. William Hunt, who contributes an inter- esting preface dealing- mainly with Dr. Gaird- ner and his position as an historian. • The volume is a splendid 'addition to follow on the three books already given to the book world on the above title. Dr. Gairdner wrote it in a fascinating style, which claims the atten- tion of any history I \-0r from beginning to end. The ebaraeter of Mary is a perfect delinea- tion, and the reader's sympathy is often evoked for the position in which she was placed ; but to one idea was she ever constant, viz., that it was for the best to place Church rule above secular authority. We, learn how' the Queen was guided by the Emperor, and how she relied on his counsels. To her fear- of governing alone, along with the idea that she would have strong support in her endeavours to bring back the old faith, .was due her consent to the mar- riage with Philip. One thing we find in this nook Which is not generally mentioned else- where, and that is the effort she made to cor- rect .the debased coinage of her father and brother. The character of Renard, the Imperial Ambassador, is admirably portrayed, but it is not pleasant to read this man's opinions of the English statesmen livMg at that time. Noailles and. Gardner are two statesmen whose careers can be followed with keen' inter- est; and the details of Sir Thomas Wyaft's re- bellion are valuable. One can see that Dr. Gairdner moves through vast masses of docu- mentary evidence, and he leaves upon the reader a better impression of the. Queen's early reign than could be .gained from ally other his- torian. The •book is a welcome .addition to elucidate the facts from the beginnihg of Mary's reign up to her marriage.

The Golden Baugh: A Study in Magic .and Religion. Third Edition, Part VI., "The Scapegoat." By J. E. Frazer, D.C.L., L.L.D., Lin.D. London: Macmillan and Co., 10s. net.

In a previous part of this work Dr. Frazer has traced the practice of killing a god among peoples in the hunting, pastoral and agricul- tural stages of society. In this volume the part which the dying god plays as a Public scape- goat is considered.

Dr. Frazer shows, by citing an immense amount of evidence,, how universal among primitive races is the custom of expelling the evils and troubles with -which people are afflicted. A vehicle is sought, to which it is be- lieved it is possible to transfer lhe particular evil or trouble to be expelled. The Vehicle chosen may be an inanimate Object, such as a stone, stick or leaf; or it may be animate, such as an animal or man. And this custom leads to the attempt to free the whole community at a single stroke of the accumulated ,sorrows, evils and troubles that oppress it. These general cleavances.take the form of expulsions of demons that are thought to be responsible for the evils, and Dr. Frazer •divides them into two 'classes, "according as the expelled evils are immaterial and invisible, or are embodied in a material vehicle or scapegoat. The former may be called the direct or immediate expul- sion of evils; the latter the indirect or Mediate expulsion, or the expulsion by scapegoat." At first the expulsion is occasional, blk tends to become periodic; the custom of ,having, general expulsion of evil Spirts annually being very widespread. A single Ciample of the im- mediate expulsion oi evils must suffice. "A writer .tells us that on the Gold Coast there are stated occasion g when the people turn out en mas.se (generally at night), With clubs and torches, to drive away the evil spirits from the towns. At a given signal, the whole COllll- - start up, commence a most hideous howl- ling, beat about in every nook and corner of their dwellings, then rush into the streets, with their torches and clubs, like so. many frantic maniacs,- beat the air and scream at the tibp of their voices, until someone announces the de- parture of the spirits through some gate of the town, when they are pursued several miles into the woods, and warned not to come, back. A.fter this the people breathe,easier, sleep more quietly, have better health, and the town is once more cheered by an abundance of food." . An example. of the mediate expulsion may oe 'quoted. "Among the, Yoruba 'negroes of West. Africa the human victim chosen for sacri- lice . is, after he has been chosen and marked 'out for the purpose, called an Oluwo. tie is always Well fed and nourished with what- ever he should desire during the period of his confinement. When the occasion arrives for him to be sacrificed and offered up, he is com- monly led about and paraded through the streets of the town or city of the sovereign who would sacrifice him for the well-being of his government and of every family and individual under it, in order that he might carry off the sin, guilt, misfortune and death of all, with- out exception), Ashes and chalk would be em- ployed to- hide his identity, by the one being freely thrown - over his head, and his face painted with the latter, whilst individuals would often rush out of their houses to lay, their hands upon him that they might thus transfer to him their sin, guilt, trouble and death. . . . After he himself has giyen out, or started his last song, which is to be taken up by the large assembly of people., who will have been wait- ing to hear his last word or his last groan, his head is taken off and his blood offered to the gods. The announcement of his last word -or his last groan; heard and taken up by the people, would be a signal for joy, gladness and thanksgiving, and for •drum beating .and 'aanc- ing, as an expression o their their gratification because their sacrifice has been 'accepted, the divine wrath is appeased, and the prospect of prosperity or increased prosperity assured."

The tendency is, of course, for these general clearances of evils to take place at some well- marked change of season, such as the begin- ning pr end of winter in the arctic and tem- perate zones, and the beginning or end of the rainS7 season in the tropics, and among agri- cultural peoples to become merged in the cere- mony of fertilising the ground. • Dr. Frazer suggests as an explanation of the practice of using a divinity as scapego'at, that this is a combination of two customs which were at.one 'time distinct. and .independent, viz., the custom of killing a ,god to save his divine lire from being weakened by age, and the cus- tom of Annually expelling the people's sins. "If it occurred to peopl6," says Dr. Frazer, " to, combine these two customs, the •result would be the employment of the dying god as scape\goat. He was killed, not originally to take away sin, but to Save the divine life from the degeneracy of old age, but since he had to be killed at any rate, people may have thought that they might as well seize the opportunity to lay upon him the burden of their sufferings and sins, in order that he•might bear it away with 'him to the Unknown world beyond the grave." The industry, patience and skill 1100.'1.3S:11Y to the compilation of this work are ,beyond all praise, and it would be difficult to over-estimate its importance to all anthropologists and students of religion.

Literary Gleanings.

AIRS. JORDAN, ACTRESS.

HazHU said about Mrs. Jordan; The child ~f N;iture whose voice was a cordial to the heart . , . to hear whose laugh - was . to drink nectar, . . . who talked far above singing and whose singing was like the twang of Cupid's bow. . . Her person was large, soft, and generous, like her soul. . . Mrs. Jurcian was all exuberance and. grace." Mr. Philip Sergeant, in his book on this stage- light of other days, shows that there are all sorts of uncertain chapters in her career. Ap- parently, she was born near Waterford, in Ire- land, in 1762, and her mother, Grace Phillips, at one time known as Mrs. Frances, was a Dublin actress. Her father's nal& was Bland, but it is extremely doubtful what he was. Dorothy, or Dora, Bland, made her first appearance in Dub- lin as Pihebe in "As. You Like It," and it was at this early time of her career that she fell under the influence of a man whom all con- temporary writers agree to denounce, Richard Daly. In order to get out of his clutches- she transferred herself to Leeds, and appeared in a series of parts, including that of Lady Teazle, under the management of the well-known. Tate Wilkinson. According to his own account, it was Wilkinson who gave her the name of Mrs. Jordan.

In 1785, when she was 23 years of age, she made her first London appearance at Drury Lane as Peggy in "The Country Girl," and before the end of her first season she' had be- come an established public favourite. How. decisive was her conquest; we can best realise when we think how uphill was her task. There was the extraordinary fame of Kitty Clive, also a comic actress, and there was the actual pre- sence in Drury Lane of Mrs. Siddons. In Mr. Sergeant's book we find much more of the. private life of Mrs. Jordan than we do of her public successes. Unfortunately, her pri- vate life was the least creditable thing about. her—not altogether owing to her own fault. She was the genius of her family, and she had all through her life to support her mother and her sisters. She was in-the first instance the victim of the designing arts of Mr. Daly—for which very likely she wtts little to blame, as she was quite a young girl and he a practised libertine. But later in London, somewhere about 1785, she had a liaison with Sir Richard Ford, and bore him four children. This lasted for some five years, till 1790, and through that period she called herself Mrs. Ford. Then came the, more celebrated scandal, for she became the mistress of the Duke of Clarence, afterwards William IV. Ten children were the fruit' of this union (Mrs. 'Jordan had fifteen children in all) mid the ten were all called Fitz- Clarence, the eldest being created Earl of Munster. From 1790 to 1811 these relations lasted, and then came a separation by mutual consent. There is no doubt that the actress was granted a very liberal allowance, nor had she a word to say against her Royal lover dur- ing the whole time of their mutual attachment, although the public Press of the time indulged in many sarcastic remarks. Brilliant artist as she was, she does not seem to have had the skill to manage her own finan- cial affairs, and she ended in obscure poverty.

THE SPANISH INQUISITION AT WORK. Mr. Rafael Sabatini in a volume just pub- lished, entitled " Torquemada and the Spanish Inquisition," shows the Inquisition at work and describes very graphically the system of tor- ture. The three main instruments relied upon were the rack, the hoist, and the potro or tail- der, or water torture. In this last-named " the patient" was fastened on a ladder .so that his head was lower than his feet. His limbs Were bound with whipcord, and a stick was 'thrusl. between the -cord and the flesh, which could be twisted until the cord sank into the 'flesh.-

"The mouth of the patient was now distended and held so by a prong of iron callEd a bostezo. His nostrils were iiluggN and a long strip of linen was placed across his jaws and carried deep into his throat by the weight of w4ter poured into ,his gaping mouth. Down this toca —as the strip was called—water continued to be slowly poured. As this water filtered through the cloth the patient was subjected to all the torments of suffocation, the more cruel because he .was driven by his instincts to . make futile efforts to ease his condition. He would con- stantly exert himself to swallow the water., hoping thus to clear the way for a little air to pass into his bursting lungs. A little would and did pass in—just enough to keep him alive and conscious--but not enough to mitigate' the horrible sufferings of asphyxiation, for the cloth was always wet and constantly charged with water.

W. T. STEAD'S PREMONITIONS.

It is likely that the passages which will attract the greatest attention in Miss'Estelle W. Stead's book on her father—" My Father. Per- sonal and Spiritual Reminiscences" (Heine- mann,l2s. net)—are those relating to his pre- monitions of a violent death. When he was spending his Christmas in Holloway /Gaol in 1885 as a result of his fight against the White slave traffic he wrote a "Christmas letter.to be read to the family," in which he prophesied his own end: "When I have my hour's walk I Per- ambulate the infirmary, going round and round like a horse in a thrashing machine, seeing apocalyptic visions of a new Earth, in which the onlmrthing I see quite distinctly is that I am called to play a great part and then get killed' at the end of it all. How, when, Where, I do not -know."

Some time later Mr. Morley went to consult him as to some slight change which he pro- posed to make in the terms of his engagement. "Excuse me, Mr. Morley," I said, " when will this new arrangement ,come into effect?" "In May, I 'think," was the reply. "Then," said I. " you need not trouble to discuss it:with me. I shall have sole Charge of the Pall.MallGazette beforeAlliat time. You will not be here then, you will be in Parliament." Finding that I was im- 'practicable, Mr. Morley left, and Concluded his arrangement without consultation. , A Month later Mr. Ashton ,Dilke fell fatally ill, 'and Mr. Morley was elected on February 24, 1884. for Newcastle-on-Tyne. Stead's 'premonition was fulfilled, though not quite to the letter.

A Walking Slick.

I am a walking stick, made from a hazel tree by a forester on the estate of Balmoral, and presented to he late Queen Victoria on one Of her visits there. I have accompanied her many times over the hills and moOrs, but once, when she was lunching on the moors, I was accident- ally left behind. I stayed there two days feel- ing very lonely, when I Was picked up by all American tourist, who ascertaining that I belonged to the Queen, highly prizeeme. While I was with him I lived a gay life, not only on the Continent, but all up and down the world.

After seeing many sights and historical places, he was again on a visit to this coun- try, and, during a short run through the High- lands, I was .accidentally left in the rack of a hotel, in Inverness. For a time I was walked out by the boots in his spare time, and -being now rather old, and battered, he presented me to a tramp at Culloden Moor. This was a great "come dawn" in the world, and I spent many years with him tramping about from place to place, lying at night in farm outhouses during the .winter and in an old battered tent in the summer time.

For years we led this free and wandering life together, and it was to me a day of regret when the tramp, finding me worn and rather 'short, left md on a path in a beautiful garden owned by a wealthy old lady, from whom he had been begging. Next morning I was picked up by her gardener, who used me •as a prop for 'a lovely bunch of yellow chrysanthemums, and here - I am now spending- the eventide of my life in peace and quietness.

INTERRUPTERS EJECTED. Lively Incidents at a P.S.A. Meeting

This volume contains the undoctored record of a non-commissioned 'officer's recollections of the South African War, written down anyhow, without method •or affectatiou, for the amuse- ment of his relatives at heme. The whole virtue of the diary is its air of absolute sincerity and directness. The author, Sergeant Murray Cosby Jackson, has no literary pretensions; he writes crudely, and often ungrammatically, and his pages are starred .with slang phrases and colloquialisms. Yet, he possesses a keen eye for a telling situation, and he Can give an mi mediate impression of what he has seen, and these a:i'e two of the cardinal virtues of litera- ture.

The authors modesty does not prevent him from expressing a decided opinidn upon mat- ters which he understands, and he often gives us a shrewd criticism of the Methods of Warfare advocated by writers who have never ventured outside the limits of `their own cosy studies,

0 I think that idea of ,novelists about officers walking about under. fire and showing coolness, being a good thing to keep men steady, is Jill rot. We had a young sub' who used to belong to the and he had evidently read Kipling or someone's yarns of that sort. He sat on an ant-heap with a black great-coat on (against a russet background) and Smoked , cigarettes ostentatiously, probably thinking he was en- couraging the men. They didn't take it that way though, and the remarks would have sur- prised him if he had heard them—' Sergeant, tell that . . . young idiot to get down, he's drawing all the lire in the Free State,' and so on. Besides, I never knew, a ease; for rather on the average the men want no more steadying than the officers, and, -as a mile, the officer knows what is going on. and where he can retire to, &c., which the men never do, so they want more nerve if anything, I take it."

As to much that was written about Boer bravery and Boer brutality, our author has a great deal worth reading to relate, and it is characteristic that his attitude towards his old enemies is generally of sporting and indulgent kind. The Boer was a sportsman himself, and appreciated the same spirit in others.

"There was a man of the Buffs (sth M. 1.) cap- tured the, same night: and they gave him a bad quarter of an hour, too.. They took him to Allandale to the Commandant (I don't know whether it was De. Wet or not). The Command- ant seemed to be in a bad temper, and said: 'Do you belong to those " verdomde " night-riding people? The Buff thought it was no use deny- ing, as he was caught at about 12 p.m., so he said he was. 'Fetch my rifle,' said the other. The Cockney admits that he felt pretty uncom- fortable. When the rifle was brought the Boer cocked it and said : Now, if I let you off, will you promise not to fight again? ' Of course, Tommy knew that the Commandant knew that he would have to, so he said : If I get back to the column, I shall have to fight again, :whether I want to or not.' 'I knew that,' said the other; if you had told a lie, I'd have shot you.' He then handed his rifle to another Boer, and said: Bring my 4 sjambok This did not worry the Buff at all, as he said afterwards: 'They could bring all the as they took away that the Commandant began telling him about some men he had captured once, whom he had made swear all sorts of impossible things, and got so friendly that by the time the sjambox arrived he would not use it, but let the man gO."

There was an exciting interlude in a meeting held on Monday night in the Sun Hall, -Liver- pool, in connection with the Liverpool and Dis- trict Federation of P.S.A. Brotherhoods. Mr. Philip Snowden, M.P., Was one of the speakers, and almost as soon as he commenced a woman in the gallery asked, "What about the women in Holloway Prison?" The Chairman appealed for fair play, but as soon as Mr. Snow‘len resumed half a dozen-other women jumpe4 to their feet and commenced to speak. A great hubbub eftsued, an the inter- rupters, struggling desperately, wpre dragged to the doors, but not =without considerable diffi- culty, for the building was so packed that several people were knocked over and chairs were smashed in the process. The next interruption came from a male Suffragist, who referred to the "Cat and Mouse

Act," and he, too, was conducted to the door with difficulty. When the uproar had subsided Mr. Snowden said he considered the interrup- tions most unfair at such a gathering, and singularly ungrateful when made against him- Ever since he - had, been -in the House of Commons be had made the cause of women especially his own.

Special services in connection with the cen- tenary of the birth of Henry Smart (1813-1879), the famous English organist and composer, were held on Sunday at Waterhead_Parish Church, Oldham.

"I was not drunk; I slipped on a piece orange peel and fell," said a man charged witl drunkenness at Bury. His explanation was no. accepted, a fine of 3s. and costs being imposed.

RM. JOEtli TAYLOR ESTABLISHES A UNIQUE • RECORD.

To do anour to one who had guided the Rail- waymen's Union -of Warrington through many difficulties, one who for nearly a quarter of u century had acted as secretary of the local branch of the old .A.S.R.S., a large number, of railwaymen assembled in the Co-operative Hall on Sunday night. The: railwaymen of War- rington and district showed their appreciation of the valuable services rendered to the union by Mr. John Taylor by making _him a present of a gold albert and a medallion. For the sacrifices she had made duripg her husbUnd's long' term of office, Mrs. Taylor received a' gold-mounted umbrella as some. slight recognition. Mr. A. Bellamy, president 'of the National Union of Railwaymen, delivered an .interesting speech on . the present conditions under .which railwaymen laboured, and strong statements were made in Hregard to the conviction. of, Driver Caudle. A ' petition to the Home Secretary, asking for the immediate release of Caudle, was signed by ti great majority of the members. Mr. C. Wozencroft (branch secretary)presidcd. On the platform were Mr. and Mrs. Bellamy Mr. and Mrs. Taylor, Brassingten (organ: ising secretaryt Mr. P. Rigby, Mr. C.. Taylor and other prominent members of the union: . A letter of apology for non-attendance was read from Mr. J. Williams, general secretary in which he said he was delighted that the ar

She was glad to recognise that in Mrs. Taylor. - Whichever way their work lay she hoped Mrs. Taylor's sacrifice would be an example to all of them to give of their best for the benefit of others.- (Applause.) Mr. -.BELLAMY replied on ,behalf of Mrs. Taylor.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0236.txt | included=true | included =====
Companies' Tremendcus Influence.

Mr. BRASSINGTON also spoke of the good work Mr and Mrs. Taylor had .done for the society. In he course of an inspiring speech he said this country was the motherland of trade unionism. The British people had done their share for developing democratic institutions. At the pre- sent time railwaymen Were faced with the most powerful combination of capital in the country. All the big associations paled into insignificance when compared with the Railway Companies' AK.oeiation, where they had probably thirteen hundred millions of capital. That 'federation Of capital had a tremendous influence in the House of -Commons. When they came down to the actual pullers of the strings there were. only a few men, and these,- up to a few weeks ago, had, totally ignored the fact- that there was an organisation on the part of their employes. They were be2inning to learn it now, but they would have to learn a little quicker. The old antiquated ideas of trade unionism

rington branch were recognising long and faith- ful services. A Unique Occasion.

The CHAIRMAN said the occasion was a unique one in connection with the railwaymen of War- rington. They fully appreciated the excellent services rendered by Mr. Taylor during the last 20 or 25 years. Mrs. Taylor had also made many sacrifices, which they appreciated fully. (Ap- plause.) Mr. P. RIGBY moved the following resolution: "That this meeting of railwaymen of Warring- ton and district expresses its great appreciation at the increasing strength of the N.U.R., and urges all railwaymen to join at once and remain members, so strengthening the movement about to be made for a reduction of hours; increase of wages and improvements in the conditions of all grades of railway servants!' They had, he said, got to do the work as a labour of love, striving for the uplifting of man. They , should try to uplift the bottom man and let the higher grade. men fight for the lowei grade men. (Applause.) Mr. C. TAYLOR seconded the resolution. Speak- ing of Mr. John Taylor, he said they' had a mem- bershiji of something like 700, and it had been heavy work for their secretary. (Hear, heal.) Mr. BELLAMY paid his tribute to the services rendered by Mr. Taylor, and said that long ago he had acted as fireman for their secretary, who had also tutored him in his business. At that time the A.S.R.S. was not a stropg body, and it was not an easy matter for a man to be a pro- minent trade unionist. The railway companies took no interference from anyone, and any man who took a prominent part in any trade union organisation ran the risk of losing his employ- ment. Then they thought it was a great boast to to have 50,000 members, but to-day they ha': 260,000. (Applause.) Mr. Taylor had been one of those men who had worked most assiduouslr, without thinking of remuneration, .to get tbe union greater and more powerful. When it was not such an easy path for the trade unionist, Mr. Taylor steadily plodded on, and when things were discouraging he Stuck to his work. He was largely responsible for. the success attend- ing the Warrington branch. He had great plea- sure in making the presentation. The medallion was inscribed • "Presented to Brother Taylor by the mem- sbee.irisicoefs trheendWereadrritnogttohne. Abi:sa.nßch .s. faosr valuable secretary for over 20 years. Dated this 20th day oi October, 1913."

were no use against the modern innovations of capital Even rive years ago one could not under- stand that there - would-be Such a position as had arisen in Dublin. Two years ago the great port of Liverpool was tied- up for two or three weeks. Although the, trade was tied up for a fortnight the railway officials folded their arms and ignored the fact that there was opposition so strong and of such a magnitude in Liverpool. They had been compelled to climb from their Position, but they would have to come down more. Unless railway .managers climbed from their high pedestal, they would be like Humpty- Dumpty and have such a fall that they would not be able to •pull themselves together again. (Hear, hear, and laughter.) The lesson they learned from Dublin and Liverpool was that sectional organisation was of no use. During the next 18 months the railwaymen would put before the working classes of the country such a position that would compel every organisation to adopt their principle of organisation by in- dustry rather than organisation by trade or calling. They would require all their strength to meet the .new developments of the Capitalists. (Hear, hear.) The Government were forming a commission on railway matters, because they feared the great combination of railway com- imrties. Even the Capitalist Press were now beginning to say something about the matter, and express 'their opinions.' One 'of the old ideas which was being trotted out anew was that it would be no advantege to the worker if railways were .nationalised. If the Government took over tbe control of the railways the- were aware that ,therP woold be some 'people displaced. Railway officials that Week and lie hoped that .jte men would treat him as courteously as the official did.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0239.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. Taylor Replies.

In reply, Mr. TAYLOR dealt at some , length with the history of the branch, which was started in 1872, when 200 men belonging to the London and North-Western Railway joined. There were one a two of those old members pre- sent tltat night. There was a great withdrawal from the society in 1873, and from then' up to 1886 there were only four members who kept the branch open,' thus maintaining the reputation of the " Warrington branch, the oldest in the Railway. Servants' Union.

Twenty-five years ago there was an influx Of some 20. members. They owed a great deal to ex-railwaymen who had- sacrificed their living and positionior the principle of trade unionism. It was owing to the ex-railwaymen that raaway workers at the present time enjoyed the privi- leges they did. When he became secretary of the branch there were 40 members, and when he resigned there were over 500 members. In the two local branches there were nearly 900 mem, hers, and he was hoping that by the end of the year they would have -1,000. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19131101_art0240.txt | included=true | included =====
Mrs. Taylor Honoured.

Mrs. BELLAMY made the ' presentation, to Mrs. Taylor and said the was always proud to pay her tribute to people who had served the society in day § gone by. It was to the individual Mem- bers of the rank and file that they owed their present position, and all honour to those who had risked' their ' positions at the time when trade unionism was not so popular. The War- rington railwaymen greatly valued!the work done by Mr. Taylor. Great sacrifices Were exacted of women on behalf of the labour movement. They were asked to make the greatest Sacrifice in their lives-4o 'sacrifice their home life. During the last two years the President had only been at home for five 'Sundays. Women did not do

===== 0002601_19131101_art0241.txt | included=true | included =====
A Fight For 1 mprovements.

After stating that municipalities paid standard rates of waes I)ecate the workers had control over the councillors, Mr. Brassington said he was convinced that it was possible for the mem- bers of. the N.U.R. to wring from the railway companies more for their men. It was possible they would have to fight for it. The Govern- ment were model employers, because there were eircumsances compelling them, and it was Pos- sible for the N. 1.7.11. to force from the great rail way companies something more than the mini- mum rti.ths paid' for outside ,work. . The example of the .N.-U-.11. would be copied by all the other trade unions in the country, and in a few years the whole aspect of British trade unionisth would be changed. Trade - unionism was not going to stand simply for wages and hours, but for the general improvement of the conditions of men. (Hear, hear, .and applause.)

The following was a petition which he asked them to sign at the door: • • • Warrington, October 26th, 1913. To the Rt. Hon. R..llclienna, Home Secretary.

Dear Sir,—We, the undersigned railwaymen and friends of Warrington and district, protest against the verdict and imprisonment of Driver Caudle, and -we respectfully- demand his im- mediate release, as we believe the mistake to be due to the onerous duties imposed on: the driver by the railway company.—Yours respectfully, C. WOZENCROF"f, Branch Secretary.

indiscreet Frankness.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0243.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. Bellamy's Protest.

Mr. BELLAMY said that in spite of critics who had criticised the advisability or otherwise of the fusion of the unions of railway workers, the great -justification for the combination lay in the fact that they had increas& their member- ship since the fusion by over 100,000. During the week they had had the announcement that the Government, having Considered the question of the relationship of the railways with the pation, had appointed a Royal Commission on the matter.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0244.txt | included=true | included =====
State Railways.

It seemed singular that within twelve mondis after the Government had. given the railway cotapanies the opportunity of compensating themselves if their wages bill went up that .they should appoint a Commission to inquire into the relations between the railways and the Stan. He hoped the Commission was not going to .Lake the line of the average Royal commission. Sometimes they had a job to find the origin-al members of a Commission. He hoped ;this Com- mission meant real business. If the underlying notion of the present Government was that the connection betweeli the railways,and the general public had become so intimate that steps woui4 have to be taken to regulate it, the reply must be: "You cannot regulate railways by passing measures that give the railway companies powe to increase rates. The only way in which you can do it is by deciding that the railways shall -become the property of the nation." If the inquiry proceeded on those lines, ,he hoped they would get to business very quickly. They would have been Well advised if they had had one' or two representatives of the work- ing classes on the CoMmission. All the brains did not lie in the civil service or the House of Lords. There. were. men with brains amongst the working classes. (Hear, hear.) A signifi- cant faet Was that the Commission was not to inquire. into the safety of railway working, or the conditions of emploYment. That :was an indication to him that they did nnt mean busi- ness. The Government would .have acted wisely in widening the Scope of the inouiry, so as to include the whole question of the control of railways.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0245.txt | included=true | included =====
In the Felon's Cell.

His mind went to the felon's cell'-at Carlisle, where Caudle was. The conditions that com- pelled Caudle to be in that place were conditions ,under which every railway servant was working to-day. There was no man on the railway who might not be in the same position. (" Shame.l Caudle had been given a locomotive upon which the Midland Company had been making experi- ments, the oil and coal vbeing affected. Caudle knew that if he did not Work the train through without stop or loss of time he would be reduced in,status, and he had to do it with poor steaming coal.

In Manchester a motorist had 'killed a man and had been fined 40s. and .costs. He had ad- mitted having five Whiskies and sodas. If Caudle had admitted that he would have been hung. (Hear, hear.) A fine for the motorist, a felon's cell for the working man! But some day the working Men and women of the country would realise that by the cross they used at election times they could send their own class to Parlia- ment, and they would appoint their own judges and civil servants. When that time came, if there was any bias it would be on the side of the working classes. (Applause.) In conclusion, .he said the railway companies in their craze for economy, were turning engines out with repairs not completed, and' perhaps defective. The company went scot free and the worker was to he the scapegoat all the time. If it Was thought that was going to be the posi- tion, as it was now, some morning the people of England would awake to the fact that the men. having such conditions imposed upon them would bear it no longer. Votes of thanks closed the meeting.

DEEDS NOT WORDS.

cured. "My backache. was brought on by a cold that affected my kidneys," said Mr. J. White, of Westfield-road, Runcorn, TWO YEARS AGO. For several weeks I suffered agony with the com- plaint. The pains would attack me when stoaped to lift anything, and then I could not get upright again for some time. My sleep was disturbed, in fact, I seemed to be worse after I got warm in bed. "The kidney excretions were disordered, and contained a dark heavy sediment. "I heard Of. Doan's Backache Kidney Pills, and made up my mind to try. them. lam pleased I did so, for they proved to be just the medicine for me. I got relief as soon as I started on the Pills, and before long I was as 'right as rain.' My kidneys are now perfectly healthy. and my back does not pain' me in the least. I hope this account of my, experience of Doan's pills will help other sufferers. (Signed) J. White." Two YEARS LATER, Mr. White said: "My Cure has been permanent. HOCKEY.

LATCHFORD DEFEATS CHESTER.

Latchford defeated the Chester Club on Sat- urday and returned yictors by five goals to one. Shortly after the' commencement a regrettable accident occurred, one of the Chester men hav- ing the misforcune to receive a nasty cut over the eye. However; another player happened to be available, so that Chester were not unduly handicapped. About half-way through the first half Chester opened the 'storing from a corner hit. Latchford replied with a couple of goals, both scored by Pearson, and they retained this lead until half-time.

In the second half the visitors had Practically all the play, scoring twice through Pearson and once through Minard, without reply from tEe home team. " For Latchford, Fletcher and Knight excelled themselves, while Pearson again showed what a capable goal-getter he is. The second-eleven were engaged with Bowdon, at Bowdon, and, for the second time this season played then): to a draw. During the first half Bowdon played a very good game and had a lead at the interval of 1-70, hut Latchford seemed to pull together more after half-time, and after a fine wing run by McCawley, finishing up with a sharp centre, Latchford equalised with a first time shot from Furness. The Latchford half- back line again played excellent hockey, and Marsden, in the forwards,, gave a good exhibi- tion.

CANCER AND SMOKE.

Cancer, after careful investigation in Scot- land, has been found to be limited to areas where coal is the staple fuel, hardly existing where peat alone is used," said Sir W. 11. Bennett, F.R.C.S., presiding at a conference on coal smoke abatement at the National Gas Exhibition,`Shepherd's Bush, W.

The textile trade in Dewsbury and Batley, especially Batley, has suddenly fallen off, and hundreds of textile workers are out of work.

The historic abbey of Jedburgh, one of the finest ecclesiastical buildings in Scotland, has been transferred to the nation by. the Marquis of Lothian..

===== 0002601_19131101_art0253.txt | included=true | included =====
at Runcorn and Widnes.

BURSTING OF A WHIRLWIND.

Warrington and district was .visited by a sharp thunderstorm on Monday night, but so far as can be ascertained no damage was done by the lightning. A gale arose afterwards, and for a time the wind blew like a medium hurri- cane.

Warrington was not quite in the centre of the electric zone, however, as was"Widnes and Run- corn, and at these places a considerable amount of damage was done. In Widnes the storm was followed by a whirlwind of terrific force, which caused much damage. The heavy - rain had ceased when a gust of wind which increased in velocity was heard. The strong wind developed in force, and swept over the town. At the junction of some of the streets, slates and bricks formed a maelstrom. The northerly, portion of the town,, particularly in the vicinity of Kent- street, encountered the utmost severity of the storm, and many injuries are reported from broken glass. Scarcely a hOuse escaped damage. Roofs were stripped and slates and bricks were cast over pavements. Many windows were broken by flying debris, and the whole of the population in the affected area was in a state of perturbation. A man named Bellot, who was walking along the street, was lifted off his 'feet, and a house, No. 16, Kent-street, was almost demolished: The Empire Rink, a huge wooden erection at the end of Frederick-street, was treated severely, and while a dance was in pro- gress the participants had alarming experiences. A portion of the London and North-Western siding from which goods are conveyed' to the respective works of the United Alkali Company and Messrs. Gossage's was strewn with wreckage from a huge Shed belongins to :Messrs. Gossage.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0254.txt | included=true | included =====
GRAND-STAND STRUCK.

At Runcorn the grand-stand whiCh had been constructed by the Runcorn N.IJ. Club was struck by lightning, and the roof was torn off. Wood and bricks were strewn about in all direc- tions. Wivern-place, lkyhich lies at the rear of the stand, practically only a• few feet dividing it and the. houses, received the full- force of the falling timbers. Chimney-stacks were demol- ished, slates were scattered in all directions, and the occupants of the houses had the most re- markable escapes. Sergeant Clague, who was in close proximity when the football stand was struck, said the crash was followed by a sudden squall of wind. The roof of the stand was car- ried across the house tops, demolishing chimney stacks and damaging the houses. In some houses which he had visited he found the rooms full of soot, and the chimney pots had fallen over the fireplaces, and several Of the occupants had had most marvellous escapes.. Many chil- dren who were in bed at the time were terrified by the experience. Luckily no one was hurt. The stand has only been recently reconstructed on the new ground of the Football Club.

The Rey. T. E. Ruth, 'rninister.at the llogh- ton-street .Chapel, Southport; has announced to his congregation his acceptance of the offer of the pulpit of the Collins-street Baptist Church, Melbourne. lie will sail early in January.

A verdict Of "Not 'guilty " was returned at Lancaster Assizes on, Saturday in the case in which James Carr, a labourer, was charged with causing the death of Thomas Nall, a painter, at Lancaster on the 18th inst.

Grappenhall St. Wilfred's v. Newton Congregational.

At Grappenhall on Saturday. The home side were the first to become aggressive, but later the visitors attacked and the home goal had a narrow escape. Newton continued to press, and after good combination on the part of their for.. wards Dodds scored. At this.reverse Grappen- hall got going, and it was not lox* before Hamp- son put his side on equal terms, the interval arriving with the score 13-1. Resuming, the visitors made the pace, and Dodds gave his side the lead. A few minutes later Newton again scored, Turner being the contributor. Grappenhall now began to force Matters, and from a corner-kick Richards suc- ceeded in scoring, time arriving with the score 3-2 in favour of Newton.

Emmanuel Old Boys v. Latchford Athletic. At Victoria park. The following teams lined out before a good muster of spectators. Old Boys: Knight; Wainwright and Ireland; Law- less, Hatton (captain) and O'Brien; Singleton, Carter, Isherwood, Caldwell and Potter. Latch- ford 'Athletic—Brooks; Evans and, Craddick ; Brooks, Dickenson and Gains; Dunbobbin, Mas- sey, Crozier (captain), W. Hall and Littlewood. Referee, G. Bamber. Isherwood led off, but the Athletic were early aggressive, and Dun- bobbin passing at the critical moment to Brooks, the latter scored with a fast shot from close range. The Old Boys were very listless in their methods, and the Athletic pressed continu- ally, but gloriocs chances went astray, , and eventually they were repulsed by Wainwright and Ireland, who defended stoutly. The home , team now livened up, and the Athletic goal was continually in danger, Brooks making many fine saves, while the posts saved splendid efforts , from Singleton, Carter and Lawless. Singleton. 'got in a great shot, which was partly saved, butt Caldwell snapped up the rebound and beat Brooks easily. Interval: Old Boys, one goal; Latchford Athletic, one. On the resumption, the play was very keen, both sides playing hard for the lead, Crozier and Evans -being prominent. Caldwell, how- ever, burst through on his own, and secured the • lead, while shortly after he converted a goost centre from Isherwood. This double reverse unnerved the Athletic, with the result that further goals were added by Carter (2), O'Brien and Potter. Brooks, despite the score against him, played well, while Crozier Evans and Dun- -bobbin lent him valuable assistance. The Old Boys played fine all round, the new players, Carter, Ireland and Singleton, giving every sat- isfaction. Result: Emmanuel Old Boys, seven. goals; Latchford Athletic, one.

MEETING OF THE WHITE ROSES. The Warrington White Rose and the St. Helens White Rose met in the St. Helens League; and, as is usual when the "Roses" meet, there was a keen and well-fought game. The result was a draw. St. Helens, on their own pitch, were early the aggressorsi and the match was not in progress long before a " Saint " three- quarter dropped a fine goal. Play ruled even after this and the interval found the "Saints" two points to the good. The Warrington men made matters • more lively in the second Portion and had the hardest of hard luck in not getting a try. • The "Saintly" defence used a bit too much hustling in their .tactics, and at last this became so pal- pable that the referee had to penalise the 'home side. J. Barlow took the kick for Warrington and landed a clever penalty goal. There was no further scoring and time produced the result: Warrington White Rose,. two points; St. Helens White Rose, two points.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0263.txt | included=true | included =====
Middle row: T. Scarisbrick, P. Morris, A. Stretford (captain), S. Carter, G. Floyd,

and J. Bra ndwood.

Front row: A. Horrocks, J. Gallop and F. liorrocks.

During the four seasons that the Rylands' Northern Union Rugby Club has been in exist- ence it has been favoured with success. During the first two seasons they. were affiliated to the St. Helens and District League, and at the first time .of asking .were successful in winning the cup by 8 points to 3, their opponents being Stockton Heath. In the second season they again reached the final, but were beaten at St. Helens by -the White Rose to the extent of 8 points to 5. As the fortunes of the club were increasing, they embarked into the Second Divi- sion of the. Manchester League in their third season, and distinguished themselves by going through the competition with only one defeat. That year saw them run into the final for the Lancashire Junior Cup Competition, when they received their dismissal at the hands of the

• White Star, a Runcorn club, who only won after a hard struggle. The Manchester and District Shield and the Warrington Works Competition also came into their hands that season, which was one to he proud of.

The, prospecis. for this season are as rbsy as any of the others, when, so far, defeat has only once fell upon them—and that in a friendly game. The success of the team is a credit to the town, and more especially to their works, where everyone of the players is employed. The play of several of the members has from time to time attracted the • attention of prominent Northern Union Clubs, and Hennessey, Leah, and Bramhall have been, respectively, induced to throw in their lot with Salford, Swinton, and Warrington. jr. Burgess is the best of secre- taries.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0268.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON "A" WIN AT WILDERSPOOL. Hornets Lose Their First Match.

There was a good attendance at Wilderspool, where the Hornets had a strong side on view. Warrington Played Jordan in place of Davies on the left wing. In winning by 1 goal 2 tries (8 points) to 1 try (3 points) our Reserve men gave by no means a satisfactory display. In fact, for a long time I thought they were in danger of losing. They certainly started off in promising style, but the play of the home three- quarters gradually deteriorated as the game progressed. Rochdale kicked off and Warrington at once pressed. Their ;forwards worked the ball right on to the Rochdale line, - but off Side tactics spoiled a glorious chance. Rochdale then, got going and showed splendid combihation. They were near scoring, and Bramhall was lucky in clearing his lines. Burton intercepted a -pass smartly, but ,Ruddick brought him down near the quarter-line. Naylor then made good use of his feet and dribbled the ball well into the Roch- dale "25," but the poor display of the War- rington centres spoiled any chance of breaking through the Hornets' defence. The Hornets' half-backs again came away from the pack and fed their threequarters nicely, and another hot raid was made on the Warrington line, but somehow or other they could not Put on the finishing touches to their good movements. Baker got well away on the right wing, and had exceedingly hard lines in not scoring. Ruddick and Alec. Brown then showed up well in some very, smart work, and Brown, making a nice opening, transferred to Jordan, who dashed over, foe a good try. critchley made no mistake with the goal-kick. Rochdale immediatelytook play into the Warrington half, and some good passing was indulged in, Hopwood eventually receiving the final transfer from , Burton, and scored at the corner. The goal-kick failed. Ruddick time and again got away, but lie received such poor support from Tranter and Dickenson that all his good work was spoiled. At half-time Warrington led by, five points to three.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0269.txt | included=true | included =====
A Scrappy Second Half.

--- - . The second half • was much on a par with the initial moiety, and play at times • was very scrappy. Critchley had very hard lines with a penalty kick, hitting the upright. Ruddick was seen to advantage and got well away on the right,- but he was unfortunate in knocking the ball forward. Both sets of forwards got away in turn, Blundell at one period almost scoring. Tranter should have passed to Jordan after the Warrington backs had made much progress, but the wing man received too late. Ruddick was at last rewarded for his efforts to break down. the Rochdale defence, scoring a beautiful try. Critchley, however, did not add the extra points. Warrington now played with a man short, Blundell being assisted off the field. He, however, soon returned. No further scoring took place, and the game ended: Warrington "A," 1 goal 2 tries (8 points); Rochdale "A," 1 try (3 points).

Ruddick the " Star " Artiste.

As I have said, Warrington did not give a very satisfactory display. This remark applies more especially to the full-back and the two .centres. The forwards played well to a man, and it was to this department—and Ruddick—that the home men owe their victory. It would be un- fair to individualise amongst them, but I Must say that the old "war horse," Naylor, gave an object-lesson to his threequarters in the art of tackling. Ruddick, at half-back, was undoubt- edly the "star" artiste, and the "little 'un " was here, there and. everywhere. I have noth- ing but praise for his work on Saturday. Indeed, I think he is the smartest half-back the War- rington Committee can put in the field. Had he received anything like support from Dickenson and Tranter the score might have been greatly augmented. His fine swerves and dashes were a treat' to watch, and he was most unselfish in the way of keeping his " threes " well fed. A. Brown also played well, but I have seen him play much better. He was penalised far too often in "putting the ball in," and ought to take a lesson fromsthis. On the whole, however, his play was very creditable, and he was really responsible for Jordan's 'try.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0270.txt | included=true | included =====
Dickenson and Tranter Too Slow.

The tireequarters could not compare with the nippy Hornets' set. Jordan, the emergency man, I think, was about the pick, but he re- ceived little encouragement from Tranter. He was the only one to tackle with anything like precision, and he played well on the defence. Baker, on the other wing, did not play with his usual dash.. I thought his .tackling was very weak indeed: His greatest fault in this respect was that he went too high for his man, instead of going for his legs. This remark applies to others besides Baker, and I hope they will bear this in mind in future games. Rochdale were allowed to make much 'progress through this error, and it was quite a common thing to see their speedy threequarters elude the clutches of the Wilderspool men.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0271.txt | included=true | included =====
Tranter's Weak Point.

This was Tranter's first appearance after his suspension, and I cannot say it was a very reassuring one, and heNwill have to show a great improvement before, in my opinion, he is worthy of a place with the seniors. He was altogether too srow in his movements,- and his tackling was not what it ought to have been, and why he did not serve Jordan more passes my comprehension. Dickenson, too, was dis- appointing. I thought he would have been able to " shine " with the combination team. It was pitiable to see the good work of Ruddick entirely thrown away through the weakness of the centres.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0272.txt | included=true | included =====
Bramhalk Again Disappoints.

_ Bramhall again had the place of honour, but I .cannot say, he filled it with much 4edit to himself. He was good and bad at times— mostly had. I am greatly disappointed in him. As I have mentioned before, I have seen him play some " great " games on the Gorsey-lane enclosure, and why he should fail so miserably at Wilderspool I cannot understand. His kick- ing was again weak, and I think it was a great mistake on his part to use the short kick so often instead of putting some shoe leather behind, the ball and making more use of 'touch: On Saturday he hesitated far too much, and I was sorry he had not taken the hint I threw 'out to him in these columns a week or two ago about standing too far away from his "threes." It is a great mistake of his, . and one the Rochdale men did ,not fail to see—and takg advantage of. However. I do not give up all hopes of him and look for better things in future games...

Rochdale are a very smart team and they have enjoyed quite a measure of success this season, this, I believe, being their, first defeat. Their threequarters were much faster than the War- rington set, and Burton (who, by the way, is a

Webb increased the score for the Adult School, and, the final result was: Adult School, three goals; Earlestown one.

FRIENDLY GAMES. Longford United v. Orford Juniors.

This friendly fixture was played at Latchford. Longford United—Holloway; Holden . and Helsby ; Blears, Finch and Sherratt; LytlLigoe, A. Finch, Moseley, W. Lea and Houghton. Orford Juniors—Pickering; Retford and Gregory; Mangham, Archer and Berry; .Riley, J.. O'Day, Williams, J., Retford and Jackson. Referee, Mr: C. S. Es,senhigh. The visitors at once began to press, -Holloway saving a hot shot,. from Williams. The ball was taken to the luiinet quarters, but the defence prevailed, and the visitors' goal underwent a heavy bombardment; Pickering distinguishing himself by some capital saves. Even exchanges followed, but no score had been registered at the interval. Moseley restarted the game and the visiting forwards gained possession and tested Holloway, who saved several shots in clever fashion. Pick- ering did likewise at the other end. The game o w became keen and exciting, both sets of play- ers, striving :hard Tor points, the defenders on both sides playing a good game. A fine indivi- dual run by J. Retford along the touch-line ended in a centre in the goal-mouth, and Wil- liams registered the only goal of the match for the Juniors. Holloway, Holden, H. Pinch and Sherratt played well for the losers, while Pick- ering, the Brothers Retford, Williams and jdck- son were the best for the winners.

Emmanuel Institute v. Newton Farm School. . At Newton. Emmanuel started briskly, but offside spoiled an effort. A nice pas4ing move- ment by the visiting forwards ended in Oakes putting in a capital shot, which gave the home goalkeeper no chance at all. After this the home team played strongly, hut Could not break down the sound defence- of A. Newton and S. Hughes. Again the visitors dashed away, and after a neat piece of combination W. Knight scored a goal. Aroused by this, the home team reduced the lead by a somewhat lucky goal. The visiting goalkeeper, P. Heacock, miskicked, which gave the 'lame centre-forward an open goal. Half-time: Emmanuel, two goals; New- ton, one.

. Resuming, the visitors put more life into their work, and after a smart piece of play between Oakes, Roberts tind Bannef, the former placed. a strong shot just outside. Following this the home team were awarded a penalty against Oakes, from which they equalised. Then two more goals, one another penalty, came in quick succession. In the last minute the visitors were awarded a penalty, this being entrusted to S. Hughes, but, the shot hit the upright and bounded out of play. The match ended in a victory for the home club by four goals to two. For Emmanuel, W. Newton, Ayres, Oakes and ! Roberts were the pick.

ANGLING YARNS.

Old Journalist" writes :

"If," says Mr. Chadband, "the master of this house were to go forth into the city and there see an eel, and were to come back and were to call untoe him the Mistress of this house, and were to say, Sarah, rejoice with me, for have seen an elephant,' would that be Terewthr Or put it that he saw an elephant and return- ing said, 'Lo, the 'city is barren. I have seen but an eel,' would that be .Terewth?" The members of the Warrington Working Men's Angling Association are equally sticklers for the "Terewth," and r have previmisly taken occasion to comment upon their strict :veracity. Indeed, if my observation and experience go tor anything, they are singular in this respect —among the angling fraternity, of course, I mean, from whom we have long been accus- tomed to innocent and harmless exaggeration. But that little reference by a last week's con- tributor to the manner of one's landing of a 41b. pike distinctly raised my spirits, and en- courages me to hope for better things of the too matter-of-fact Warringtonians. Just by way of encouragement, let me 'reproduce an- other of Mr. Carter Platt's gems. He tells us that an angling visitor in a Yorkshire dale was enlarging on his exploits—at any rate his hearers were' convinced that he was enlarging very elastically—to a group of villagers. "Yes, gentlemen," he assured them, "remember I was fishing with a trout rod-.and, trout tackle when that salmon fastened, and L played him four hours before I could 'make an impression on him. Then he caved in, turned up his side, and weighed sixteen pounds when he was lifted out."

The villagers kept silence ; then one of them, in a tone of sweet, undiluted ingenuousness, mur- mured: "We've salmon in the river up here, and the last I caught I had on longer than you had your's, and he was a bigger fish, too." "how long had you had him on before you landed him?" asked the visitor sharply. "Three months, and a half," sighed the villager softly. ." You see, I played him for ten hours, until I'd got him to his last gasp, and then I found he was too big for me to pull out, and there wasn't a landing net in the dale large enough to hold him. S'o I tethered him with the line to a plane tree on the bank, and went to fetch our old mare to drag him out. Well, you ,see, I should have got him then, only the old mare was out in the pastures, and she took a dm- founded lot of catching in the dark—for, of course, it was late at night by then—and by the tune I could get her yoked and down to the river—well, I. forgot to tell you it was the last day of the season, in the middle of November, when I hooked the fish, and by the time I could get the old mare down to the river it was after midnight, and the close season had started. So. I had to keep that salmon on, tethered to, the tree, for three Months and a half, until the season Caine on again on:the first of March, when I dragged him out with the old mare." And "when he had finished speaking the visitor ' had vanished. Warrington anglers need not go :leloy this length, but—well, you know.

CROSS COUNTRY RUNNING.

Warrington Athletic Club had 24 runners out, from headquarters, for a run over about five .miles of mixed country. Over the first half of the journey a moderate gait was maintained, then the pack split up into groups, principally in pairs, and came home at quite a good pace, with H. Holbrook and W. Walters the leading couple, who were closely followed by C.' Vose and J. Hanson. Then came S. Grounds, A. Col- lins, A. Inman and J. Hayes, with J. Parsonage and H. Jepson in attendance. J. Scott and F. Price were showing good form just in front of H. Woods and A. Potts.

SEWING OR SM6KING ?

Mrs. Ziba Armitage and the Use of

Sales of Work.

===== 0002601_19131101_art0307.txt | included=true | included =====
£77 SECURED AT LATCHFORD.

An artistic and tasteful display was made at the annual sale of work held in the Latchford Baptist Church Schoolroom on Thursday. Mr. J. W. Hayward presided and Mrs. Ziba Armitage performed the opening ceremony. There were also on the platform the Pastor, Rev. J. S. Chedburn, the Rev. W. Reid, Mr. W. T. Wilkin- son, and Mr. S. Albinson. The joint secretaries of the sale were Mrs. Wilkinson and Mr. J. R. Conway, with Mrs. W. Adams as treasurer. The Rev. J. Curtis was amongst those present. The Rev. J. S. Chedburn said sales of work had come to be recognised and used by every branch of religious workers. They were based on the soundest principles. In olden days, when people were unable to bring their offerings in the specific kind required by the law, they were privileged to substitute some equivalent, more in the Scope of their own means and capa- bilities, and that was what they made provision for there. Those who could not give money gave their time and industry. (Hear, hear.) The Chairman referred to the sale as one of the brightest he had seen. They at Wash-lane had stuck to their work and had done it nobly, manfully, bravely and well. (Applause.) Mrs. Armitage, in opening the sale, said a good deal was said against sales of work, but there was a good deal to say in favour of them. Whilst women sewed and made things together, friendly feelings were promoted amongst them. Sewing amongst women was like- smoking amongst men, only women did something use- ful. (Laughter.) She wished them every suc- cess in their effort. (Applause.) Mr. Wilkinson proposed, and Mr. Albinson seconded, a vote of thanks to the Chairman and Mrs. Armitage, which was unanimously accorded The proceeds amounted to £77.

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

"THE PIRATE SHIP."

As a highly sensational production, Mr. C. Watson Mills' latest drama is a success, and to satisfy lovers of hair-raising incidents the author has chosen a capital theme. A more blood- thirsty gang of sea rovers it is impossible to imagine than those who man the brig around which the plot evolves. A tale of love, jealousy, treasure hunting and revenge dominates, whilst there is the faintest smattering of humorous incidents.
