===== 0002601_19131101_art0311.txt | included=true | included =====
BOLD STREET WESLEYAN CHURCH_

An Off-shoot That Became a Great Tree.

HISTORY AND ASSOCIATION.

The Wesleyan Church in Wt4Tington is an off-shoot, Liverpool, and was formed into a circuit in the year 1791, and its first ministers were the liev. George 'Snowden and the Bev. W. Palmer. In the year 1792 it became identi- fied with, and formed part. of, the Northwich Circuit, and remained so until. the year 1811. In the year 1812 it separated from Northwich, and became, as it is now known, the Warring- ton Circuit.

Solid Foundations. During the many years thud, followed, -able men have been numbered among its minis- ters. It is not a little thing to have had Richard Smetham in the circuit. In 1836 the Rev. W. 'W. Stamp was the superintendent minister. He subsequently became the Gover- nor of the Theological College at Richmond, and President-of the Conference in 1860. Such men ,must have added elevation to every senti- ment, and solidity to every enterprise. Tiwy laid deep foundations, and did much honest and most durable masonry. The services- were held in Bank-street, in a chapel erected more than 100 years ago. The building subsequently became the County Couit offices, and is at Present occupied as a Ragged /S'chool. John Wesley bintself preached there during his visit to \Varrington. At Lae time he was a feeble old man, and report says that so great was the crowd that came to hear the venerable Preacher, he had to be carried to the pulpit on le shoulders of the young men of the congre- gation. About the year 1848 the Rev. George Dickinson was appointed to the circuit. He was then 28 years of age, full of energy and spirit, and Whose sermons were marked by considerable eloquence and power. The chapel very. soon got too small for the crowds attend- ing it, and often on the Sunday evenings hun- dreds were unable to obtain admission. It was then decided to seize the wider opportunities that presented themselves, and so, in 1849, a plot of land eminently suited to the purpOse was secured in Bold-street for the building of .a new chapel. It was the closing of one history and opening another, and they resolved to put up a better building in a better place. A Step Forward.

It says much for the sagacity and forethought of the brave men who were in office at the time, that they were so well able to understand the signs of the tiines, and that they were ready when their opportunity came. An honour to such Men as James Carter, Thomas Hadfield,. James Dutton, Dr. Smith, John Guest, NV. San- derson, and many others, and the circuit to-day holds their names in affectionate remembrance. It must in those days have been a great scheme, but they answered the challenge with that in- destructible spirit and courage, which alone was worthy of their antecedents, and the erec- tion of the handsome church in Bold-street is a standing memorial to their faith and enter- prise. The .appeal for money was liberally re- sponded 'to—the people were full of enthusiasm and hope, and by donations, and a bazaar, held either in the Lion Hotel Assembly Room or the Old Music Hall, the church was built, and in the autumn of 1850 was opened. A story is told that while the procession was on its . way from Buttermarket-street School to the laying of the foundation stone in Bold-street, a clergy- man, standing upon the steps of Trinity Church, remarked, "See what a few shop- keepers can do. We couldn't make such a show." The opening sermons were preached by the Rev. G. B. Macdonald, Dr. Newton, Dr. Beaniont, the Rev. Samuel Broadbent and the Rev. George Dickinson, the two latter being the circuit ministers at that time. Nearly all who were at those opening services have gone; few only remain until this day. Among the ministers who have travelled in the Warrington Circuit in more recent times have been Dr. Agar Beet, an eminent theologian, and author of several theological works; the Bev. Wilkins Flees, a writer of considerable literary ability, and many othee ministers, who have occupied honourable and . responsible positions in the Methodist Church.

Prominent laymen have ably supported the ministers in their work.

The Off-shoot Grows.

For niany years Bold-street met the needs of the Methodist Society and congregation, but as time went on, and the town grew in size and importance, it became necessary to increase the, 'seating accommodation, and Bewsey-road Church was built in the year 1874. Bold-street has held an honoured place amongst the churches. True, it has had to suffer much from suburban tendencies of the population. It is solid. It is trustwprthy. The people who have been thoroughly brought under its influence can never quite throw it off. They respond to great; occasions. They abhor shabby practices.

Handsome' chapels and schools have been erected at Penketh, Padgate, Latchford, Stock- ton Heath., Lymm,-and enlargements and reno- vations have been made at Many other places in the circuit.

The Anniversary.

The anniversary sermons are to be preached to-morrow (Sunday), 2nd November, by the Rev. J. S. Simon, D.D., ex-president of the Confer- ence. The musical part of the service wil. be of a very high. Order. "For all the saints" will be sung to an appropriate setting by Dr. C. T. Reynolds, a name well known in War- rington. ,Solos will be rendered by Mrs. Booth, Miss Emily Taylor and Miss Hilda Cragg-James, who will be supported by an augmented choir. Miss Hilda Cragg-James comes with the very highest . credentials, and altogether it is ex- pected that the music will be of a very high character, and will be worthily rendered by the 'choir. .

The Bev. Hugh Williamson, BA., will give the address in the afternoon, and the chair will • 'taken by Councillor P. Peacock.

HOW TO NEUTRALISE

DANGEROUS STOMACH ACIDS.

Few people besides physicians realise the im- portance of keeping the food contents of the stomach free from acid fermentation. Healthy normal digestion cannot' take .place while the delicate lining of the stomach is being inflamed and distended by acid and wind—Vie results of fermenting food in the stomach. To secure perfect digestion, fermentation must be stopped or prevented, and the acid neutralised. ffor this purpose physicians usually recommend get- ting a little bisurated magnesia from the chem- ist and taking half a teaspoonful in a little hot or cold water. immediately after eating. They recommend bisurated magnesia because it is pleasant to take, has no disagreeable after effects, and intantly stops fermentation, neu- tralises the acid and makes the sour acid food bland, sweet and easily digested. The regular use of bisurated magnesia—be sure you get the bisurated, as other kinds of magnesia are of little value—is an absolute guarantee of healthy, normal digestion, for it overcomes and prevents that acid condition which alone is tlie,cause of trouble.

A PROTECTIONIST LEAGUE. A:rile origin of the Tariff Reform League is not Zaetly lost in obscurity. Most Tariff Reform- i2 heartily wish it were. Other of-tsPtis exist, but most of them are ashamed parentage and try to slur it over.-From 'Lle Tariff Reform Mirage," by W. E. Dowding. THE GATEWAY OF CANADA. The City of Quebec was of old, and is still, the gateway of the new world. The majority of immigrants to Canada pass through that gate- way, some to proceed to the Far West, but many to spread themselves out over the vast, thinly populated regions of the Province of Quebec, now the largest of all the Canadian pro- vinces. This year's influx is not yet over, and it is thought that not less than 250,000 new settlers will have entered Canada through the Quebec gateway before the close of the year. To cope with this enormous number of new arrivals, immigration buildings are now in lthearg e w co unie.se o construction at Quebec. It is re- ported that of their kind they will be a model. They will be ready for 9ccupation at the begin- ning of next season. The average age of 400 old folks who attended a treat at Risliton on Saturday was said to be 68 years.

• CHESHIRE MOTOR 'BUSES. Service from Warrington. The scheme to provide'a connection of motor 'buses throughout Cheshire is maturing, and Warrington is promised a service with Knuts- ford and. Macclesfield. The type of 'bus which it is -proposed to run between Macclesfield and Warrington has been on exhibition during the past few days, and has create 4 an extremelY favourable impression. The Chelforcl Parish Council have given their consent to the scheme, and Knutsford are fav- ourable, and the necessary permission to run through the district will be given here also. The Bucklow Rural Council and the Macclesfield Council have still to be approached, and assum- ing the permits are granted in these cases the 'buses will have the length of the road right up to the Warrington tram terminus at Latchford.

Theatre, Warrington, next week. The scenery and costumes are entirely new, and "The Pride of Byzantia " is now presented on a much more lavish scale than previously. It 'is really an autumn pantomime, and the cast is composed of pantomime "stars." Mary O'Hara, the little Irish girl, who has played in "The Pride of Byzantia " since its first production, will again appear in the play as Winnie. PALACE AND HIPPODROME. "All Square" is a thrilling one-act Western play, Which is presented with success this week-. A saloon squabble develops into a shooting affair and the rough-and-ready inethods of the "boys" is typified. Noblett is one of the smartest quick-change artistes we have seen. /Daring gymnastic feats are accomplished by the Car- patti Bros., whose somersaulting feats are ex- ceedingly well executed. Will Hay, the schoolmaster comedian, is as popular as ever with his " bend down." Song. Les .Andres gives a capital dancing turn. NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME. The Playlet, " The Son of a Jew," to be pre- sented by Herbert Landeck and his company, takes the premier position next week, and a turn which will run it very close is that pro- vided by Frank Powell, a first-rate patter come- dian. A novelty in the way of a rag-time danc- ing competition will ,be inaugurated by Gordon and Elgin, American society dancers. THEATRE ROYAL, The theatre :this week re-opened, under the management of Mr. H. Griffin. "The Shatighraun," a jolly Irish play., was presented, in which the character of Rielly. was taken by Mr. Harry Val Vousden, that of Claire Ffolliott by Miss Beatrice Malvern, and that of Father Dolan by Mr. T. Healey. Next week ' The Cowboy's Sweetheart" will be presented, when Mr. Harry Val Vousden will a.nnear in the role of Pawney
